# Sūrah Āli ʿImrān (Chapter 3) — Expanded Verse-by-Verse Commentary

## Introduction to the Sūrah

**The Family of Imran and the Crucible of Steadfastness**

Āli ʿImrān, "The Family of Imran," is the third chapter of the Qur'an, comprising 200 verses of late-early Medinan revelation. Named after the pious lineage of Imran—the father of Maryam (Mary) and grandfather of ʿĪsā (Jesus)—the sūrah stands as the fraternal twin to Sūrah al-Baqarah. In prophetic tradition, the two chapters are jointly venerated as *al-Zahrāwayn* (the Two Radiant Ones), which the Prophet ﷺ promised would arrive on the Day of Resurrection like two dense clouds, two canopies of shade, or two flocks of birds in flight, pleading for the souls of those who recited and internalized them (Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim 804). While al-Baqarah engaged predominantly with the Mosaic legacy and the establishment of communal law following the triumphant victory at Badr, Āli ʿImrān addresses the Christian religious landscape and navigates the psychological trauma and internal fractures that followed the catastrophic setback at the Battle of Uḥud in 3 AH. It is a sūrah of ideological clarification and moral resilience, forging an unyielding spiritual temper capable of withstanding religious seduction from without and battlefield despair from within.

**The Structure and Thematic Modulation**

The architecture of Āli ʿImrān moves from metaphysical epistemic principles to historical narrative, and from argumentative discourse to the psychology of military struggle. The opening section (1–32) establishes the interpretation of divine scripture, famously distinguishing between foundational, clear verses (*muḥkamāt*) and allegorical verses (*mutashābihāt*), while exposing how diseased hearts pursue ambiguity to foment discord. The second movement (33–63) details the sacred history of the Imranic house: the barren wife of Imran dedicating her unborn child to God, the dedicated sanctuary life of Maryam nurtured with miraculous fruit, the miraculous birth of Yahya to elderly Zechariah, and the sublime conception of ʿĪsā through the divine word *Kun* ("Be!"). Verses 64–99 summon the People of the Book to a common platform of absolute monotheism (*“Come to a word that is equitable between us and you,”* 3:64), deconstructing sectarian claims by demonstrating that Abraham preceded both the Torah and the Gospel. The fourth movement (100–120) charges the Muslim community with institutional vigilance, establishing them as *“the best community brought forth for humanity”* through the imperative of enjoining good and forbidding evil. The fifth and largest movement (121–189) provides the sweeping commentary on Uḥud: unmasking the whispering of the hypocrites, comforting the bereaved, forbidding predatory interest (*ribā*) in the midst of military struggle, and celebrating the immortal life of the martyrs (*“Never think of those killed in the cause of Allah as dead,”* 3:169). The epilogue (190–200) closes with the famous contemplation of the cosmic signs by those of deep intellect (*ulū al-albāb*), concluding with the comprehensive moral imperative: *“Be patient, encourage endurance, station yourselves, and be mindful of Allah, that you may prosper.”*

---

## Verse 3:1

> Alif-Lãm-Mĩm

**Three Letters Standing Where a Sentence Should Stand**

Three letters, not a word. The reciter names them one after another — *alif*, *lām*, *mīm* — and stops. Arabic does not pronounce the group as a syllable, so the sūrah opens with an utterance that is neither noun nor verb nor sentence, and the pause after the third letter is the point at which revelation proper begins.

Nothing in the Book decodes them. Twenty-nine of the Qur'an's sūrahs open in this way, singly or in clusters: *ṣād*, *qāf*, *nūn*, *ḥā-mīm*, and the pairs and triples built from the same alphabet. This sūrah uses exactly the three that open the sūrah before it — the translation of 2:1 is letter for letter the same line as the one above (2:1). A reader who has just come from al-Baqarah hears the same three notes struck again, and the ear cannot treat them as noise; the repetition turns them into a signature.

Old copies of the Qur'an often set these letters apart, larger or ornamented, as a title-page rather than a sentence. The convention reflects what the text itself does not contradict: whatever else they are, they mark a beginning.

**The Alphabet as the Argument**

The letters are the alphabet of the audience, and that fact carries the sūrah's first implicit claim. The Book is not written in a private script or a heavenly vocabulary; it is built from the twenty-eight sounds that a Makkan child learned at his mother's knee. The challenge attached to that claim runs through the Qur'an: *“If ˹all˺ humans and jinn were to come together to produce the equivalent of this Quran, they could not produce its equal, no matter how they supported each other.”* (17:88). Its shorter forms invite the doubter to bring a single sūrah of the same quality (2:23; 10:38).

Read that way, the opening letters are a dare. Here are your materials — take them and do what this does. The argument has force precisely because the raw material is common property, and the outcome is not: identical bricks, a building nobody else could raise. That is why the letters are followed immediately by a statement about God rather than by a story about the Prophet.

**Named Among the Greatest Names**

The weight these three letters carry is shown by where the Prophet places them. Asmāʾ bint Yazīd heard him say that God's greatest name — the name by which, when He is called, He answers — lies in two places: the verse *“Your God is ˹only˺ One God. There is no god ˹worthy of worship˺ except Him—the Most Compassionate, Most Merciful.”* (2:163), and the opening of this sūrah (Sunan al-Tirmidhī 3478, graded ṣaḥīḥ by al-Tirmidhī; Sunan Abī Dāwūd 1496, graded ḥasan by al-Albānī; Sunan Ibn Mājah 3855).

The report attaches the greatest name to 3:1–2 as an unit, which means the letters are recited as part of the most powerful invocation the Prophet knew. Recited beside the sūrah of the Cow, this sūrah is called one of the *zahrāwān*, the two bright ones, which the Prophet said would come on the Day of Resurrection like two clouds or two flocks of birds in ranks, pleading for those who recited them (Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim 804). The three letters are the doorway into that reading.

---

## Verse 3:2

> Allah! There is no god ˹worthy of worship˺ except Him—the Ever-Living, All-Sustaining

**A Sentence That Clears the Field First**

The line has three movements, and the order matters. First the name alone; then the removal of every rival — a denial that is stated before anything is affirmed about the one who remains; then the two attributes that explain why He can be trusted with the whole of existence.

*“He is the Ever-Living. There is no god ˹worthy of worship˺ except Him. So call upon Him with sincere devotion, ˹saying,˺ ‘All praise is for Allah—Lord of all worlds.’”* (40:65). The same architecture appears at 2:255, the verse of the Seat, and at 112:1–2 in the shortest possible form: *“He is Allah—One ˹and Indivisible˺; Allah—the Sustainer ˹needed by all˺.”* (112:1–2). The Qur'an does not introduce God by arguing with the objects people worship. It states who He is, and the rivals fall away as a consequence of the description.

**Al-Ḥayy: Life That Was Never Given**

*Al-Ḥayy* says more than "living". Every living thing in the sentence's shadow received its life as a loan; God did not. His life is not a quality added to Him at some point or maintained by anything outside Him, which is why the verse that calls Him *al-Ḥayy* calls Him at the same time imperishable: *“Put your trust in the Ever-Living, Who never dies, and glorify His praises. Sufficient is He as All-Aware of the sins of His servants.”* (25:58).

The Qur'an draws the practical conclusion from the name rather than leaving it as a metaphysical decoration. If life cannot be drained from Him, then the one who leans on Him is leaning on something that cannot fail — not because He is strong, but because the failing He would have to undergo does not exist in Him. The declaration at 20:111 turns the same name into an image of the last hour: *“And all faces will be humbled before the Ever-Living, All-Sustaining. And those burdened with wrongdoing will be in loss.”* (20:111). The proud faces of this life will be lowered in front of the name in the next.

**Al-Qayyūm: The One Who Stands and Holds Others Up**

*Al-Qayyūm* is built on the verb *qāma*, to stand, in an intensive form, so the name carries two directions at once: He stands by Himself, owing His existence to no one, and He keeps everything else standing. The translation "All-Sustaining" follows the second direction; the first is not lost in the Arabic.

The Qur'an spells the job out in the verse of the Seat: to Him belongs whatever is in the heavens and the earth, and *“the preservation of both does not tire Him. For He is the Most High, the Greatest.”* (2:255). The statement is an answer to a very human worry. If the universe were held up by something that could weary, everything in it would be living on borrowed stability, and the borrowed kind eventually runs out. The two names together cover what a creature cannot supply for itself: life, and support under the weight of being alive.

---

## Verse 3:3

> He has revealed to you ˹O Prophet˺ the Book in truth, confirming what came before it, as He revealed the Torah and the Gospel

**The Verb That Says the Book Came Down**

The Arabic of the verse uses two forms of one root for two acts of revelation: *nazzala*, "He sent down", attached to the Book given to the Prophet, and *anzala*, attached to the Torah and the Gospel. A reader who works only in translation sees the same English verb twice and misses the variation.

Al-Zamakhsharī and al-Rāzī read the variation as intentional and proportionate: the Qur'an came down in portions across twenty-three years, so the intensive form suits it, while the earlier books arrived as complete units, so the plain form suits them. Al-Ṭabarī answers that the two forms are used interchangeably throughout the Qur'an and the distinction cannot be pressed. His objection carries weight, but so does the fact that the Qur'an elsewhere says the same thing about itself plainly: *“˹It is˺ a Quran We have revealed in stages so that you may recite it to people at a deliberate pace.”* (17:106). The difference between the two positions is not what happened; it is whether the wording was chosen to tell us about the timing. On the text alone, al-Ṭabarī is the more careful reader, and the portioning of the Qur'an is established by verses that do not depend on the grammar of this one.

**Confirming What Came Before**

*Muṣaddiq* is the word translated "confirming", and it describes a witness, not a replacement. The new revelation testifies that the earlier ones were genuine, and the Gospel does the same for the Torah before it: *“Then in the footsteps of the prophets, We sent Jesus, son of Mary, confirming the Torah revealed before him. And We gave him the Gospel containing guidance and light and confirming what was revealed in the Torah—a guide and a lesson to the God-fearing.”* (5:46). The Book's relation to its predecessors is stated most compactly in 2:41: *“Believe in My revelations which confirm your Scriptures.”*

Jesus puts his own mission in the same verb when he announces a messenger still to come (61:6). So the chain runs forward as well as backward: each revelation shows the one before it and points to the one after, and no link in the chain is presented as the cancellation of its predecessor. Confirmation and correction are compatible; erasure is not what the word means.

Read against this verse, the report supplies a shape. The night mentioned in 97:1 is the night this sūrah's Book began to come down.

**What the Torah and the Gospel Name**

The Torah is normally understood to mean the five books of Moses, and the Gospel the revelation given to Jesus, and the commentators asked how those names relate to the scriptures as they now exist. The Book says that the revelation given to the Prophet confirms what came before it, and al-Rāzī raised the difficulty of how a text from the past can be spoken of as present, answering that the earlier lore is treated as present because it is so widely known. One line of explanation takes the charge against the People of the Book to concern their interpretation rather than the wording of their books, which is the position of those who speak of distortion of meaning (2:75; 4:46; 5:13, 41). Another allows that the Gospel given to Jesus is conveyed by the Gospel the Christians have without being identical to it, so that what they have carries the message while also containing material of a different kind. The discussion is not settled by the verse, which states the relation between the books and leaves the detail of their transmission to be examined.

---

## Verse 3:4

> previously, as a guide for people, and ˹also˺ revealed the Standard ˹to distinguish between right and wrong˺. Surely those who reject Allah’s revelations will suffer a severe torment. For Allah is Almighty, capable of punishment

**Al-Furqān: One Name, Several Books, One Function**

The word translated "Standard" is *al-furqān*, and the Qur'an uses it of more than one revelation. Moses receives it: *“And ˹remember˺ when We gave Moses the Scripture—the standard ˹to distinguish between right and wrong˺ that perhaps you would be ˹rightly˺ guided.”* (2:53), and Aaron is joined with him in the same gift (21:48). It is used of the Qur'an itself: *“Blessed is the One Who sent down the Standard to His servant, so that he may be a warner to the whole world.”* (25:1). Even the day of Badr is called *yawm al-furqān*.

The dictionaries separate the word from "Qur'an" sharply. *Furqān* means the act of separating, of making a distinction, built on the pattern that Arabic uses for intensity and instrument; *Qur'an* means recitation. A book is named for what it says and what it does — recited when read aloud, separating when applied. Some commentators, noticing that the Qur'an is already called the Book in this sentence, took the Standard to be another scripture, even the Psalms; others took it as a description of all three books together. Both readings respect the same underlying claim: revelation's first job is discrimination.

**Why Denial of Revelation Draws the Harshest Warning**

The sentence that follows the list of books does not say "those who reject this Book". It says *those who reject Allah's revelations* — the plural that includes every descent just named. The warning is attached to a refusal that covers the whole series, and the punishment is described as severe.

The Qur'an keeps this charge in view elsewhere with unmistakable phrasing: *“And who does more wrong than the one who is reminded of Allah’s revelations then turns away from them? We will surely inflict punishment upon the wicked.”* (32:22). The same threat is repeated to the Prophet's opponents when they imagine the messenger's removal would free them: *“Even if We take you away ˹from this world˺, We will surely inflict punishment upon them.”* (43:41).

Rejecting a sign is not an intellectual misjudgement in this vocabulary; it is a decision made in the presence of evidence. What protection would remain for anyone, in heaven or earth, once that decision has been taken?

**"Almighty, Capable of Punishment" — an Attribute, Not a Threat**

The Qur'an attaches the same pair of attributes to very different contexts so that the reader learns to hear it as a fact rather than as a flourish. It closes a verse about hunting during pilgrimage with it (5:95) and stands as a rebuke to doubters elsewhere: *“So do not think ˹O Prophet˺ that Allah will fail to keep His promise to His messengers. Allah is indeed Almighty, capable of punishment.”* (14:47). Revelation was refused by people who assumed that the refusal carried no cost. The cost is stated in the same breath as the gift.

In the month of fasting, the Book is described as *“a guide for humanity with clear proofs of guidance and the standard ˹to distinguish between right and wrong˺”* (2:185), which places the criterion inside the guidance rather than beside it.

---

## Verse 3:5

> Surely nothing on earth or in the heavens is hidden from Allah

**A Sentence With No Corner Left Out**

The verse takes the two halves of the created world and puts them in one clause: earth and heaven, nothing in either concealed from God. Hebrew and Arabic speech both use this pairing as a way of saying "everywhere", and the Qur'an uses it constantly. The translation preserves the shape of the phrase, which is why it sounds like a survey rather than a proposition.

Other passages fill in the same sentence with consequences. Abraham's descendants pray with it in mind: *“Our Lord! You certainly know what we conceal and what we reveal. Nothing on earth or in heaven is hidden from Allah.”* (14:38), and the Book of Records is described in identical terms: *“For there is nothing hidden in the heavens or the earth without being ˹written˺ in a perfect Record.”* (27:75). The same claim is made about the small things, the ones no ledger notices: *“With Him are the keys of the unseen—no one knows them except Him. And He knows what is in the land and sea. Not even a leaf falls without His knowledge, nor a grain in the darkness of the earth or anything—green or dry—but is ˹written˺ in a perfect Record.”* (6:59).

**What "Hidden" Covers**

The word *khafiya* is about concealment, and the Qur'an's usage shows how wide that is meant to be. It covers deeds done when nobody is looking: *“There is no activity you may be engaged in ˹O Prophet˺ or portion of the Quran you may be reciting, nor any deed you ˹all˺ may be doing except that We are a Witness over you while doing it.”* (10:61). It covers what has not happened yet, and what is buried under other facts, and what the agent himself has not admitted.

Commentators pressed the point to its psychological edge. If God's knowledge of His own creation were incomplete, the incompleteness would have to be explained by something in the creation that escaped Him; the Qur'an refuses that possibility with a question: *“How could He not know His Own creation? For He ˹alone˺ is the Most Subtle, All-Aware.”* (67:14). The knowledge is described as belonging to the act of making, not as acquired afterwards. Whoever assembled something knows where its joints are.

**A Comfort, Not Only a Warning**

It also explains a repeated Qur'anic habit of joining knowledge with power, as in the closing words of 3:29: *“And Allah is Most Capable of everything.”* (3:29). Knowing and being able to act on what is known are two different things, and creatures have both in broken measure — they know little, and they can do less than they know. The verse above the reader states the undivided version, which is why it can be recited as reassurance without being consoling in a cheap way. Nothing is hidden, and the one who sees can act.

---

## Verse 3:6

> He is the One Who shapes you in the wombs of your mothers as He wills. There is no god ˹worthy of worship˺ except Him—the Almighty, All-Wise

**Shaping in the Dark**

The verb is *yuṣawwiru*: He gives form, He fashions. The place is the hiding place: the wombs of mothers, where nothing human can see and where nothing human is doing the work. The clause is a claim about both capability and intimacy, and it is stated with the ordinary vocabulary of the workshop.

Other passages open the process up. The Qur'an says human beings are created *“from a single soul”* and that God *“creates you in the wombs of your mothers ˹in stages˺, one development after another, in three layers of darkness”* (39:6). At 22:5 the sequence is described in detail — dust, a drop, a clinging clot, a lump of flesh, formed or unformed — and the purpose is given explicitly: to show divine power. A related verse about formation uses the same imagery for the whole species: *“Who created you, fashioned you, and perfected your design, moulding you in whatever form He willed?”* (82:7–8), and 40:64 praises the same act: *“He shaped you ˹in the womb˺, perfecting your form.”*

Each of these verses treats the shaping as evidence rather than information. The making of a person is not offered to explain biology; it is offered as a reason to worship the maker.

**The Shaping of Jesus as the Sūrah's Silent Case**

This sūrah will put a man who entered a womb and left it by way of a mother at the centre of a dispute about his nature. A reader who approaches that dispute carries the question this verse answers: could someone formed in a womb be God? The Book answers elsewhere in plain words: *“The Messiah, son of Mary, was no more than a messenger. ˹Many˺ messengers had ˹come and˺ gone before him. His mother was a woman of truth. They both ate food.”* (5:75).

There is a second edge to the same principle, and the sūrah will use it. A community that claims privileged descent from a chosen ancestor is claiming something about formation — that ancestry can settle a person's standing before God. The verse answers that the shaping that produced the ancestor and the shaping that produced his critics were the same act of will, exercised in the same hidden place, with no lineage consulted.

**Power and Wisdom Named Together**

The verse ends with two names, *al-ʿAzīz* and *al-Ḥakīm*: Almighty and All-Wise. They are the pair that answers each half of the sentence just read. Power accounts for the fact that shaping happens at all; wisdom accounts for the fact that it happens "as He wills", which is to say according to something better than chance.

---

## Verse 3:7

> He is the One Who has revealed to you ˹O Prophet˺ the Book, of which some verses are precise—they are the foundation of the Book—while others are elusive. Those with deviant hearts follow the elusive verses seeking ˹to spread˺ doubt through their ˹false˺ interpretations—but none grasps their ˹full˺ meaning except Allah. As for those well-grounded in knowledge, they say, “We believe in this ˹Quran˺—it is all from our Lord.” But none will be mindful ˹of this˺ except people of reason

**Both Descriptions Apply to the Whole Book**

The Book is not divided into two libraries. Elsewhere it describes itself in terms that overlap the two labels: *“˹This is˺ a Book whose verses are well perfected and then fully explained”* (11:1), where the root is the same as *muḥkam*. *“˹It is˺ Allah ˹Who˺ has sent down the best message—a Book of perfect consistency and repeated lessons”* (39:23), where the word is the same as *mutashābih*.

**The Deviant Heart and the Use It Makes of Ambiguity**

The charge is not that some people read difficult passages. It is that they *follow* them — the verb implies pursuit, choosing the loose stones out of a wall while ignoring the beams. Their aim is named twice: seeking *fitnah*, trouble and discord, and seeking an interpretation of their own.

The grammar exposes the method. Ambiguity is not dangerous in itself, but it is useful to anyone who wants a particular conclusion badly enough to select the passages that permit it. The Qur'an notes elsewhere that God uses such tests deliberately: a parable or a number in the text becomes a dividing line, *“In this way Allah leaves whoever He wills to stray and guides whoever He wills.”* (74:31). The same function is attached to the smallest of the parables: *“Through this ˹test˺, He leaves many to stray, and guides many.”* (2:26).

**Where the Sentence Stops: An Old Disagreement About This Very Line**

The words "none grasps their full meaning except Allah" can be read as the end of a sentence, with the next words beginning a fresh one: *and those well-grounded in knowledge say, "We believe in it."* On that reading, the interpretation of the elusive passages belongs to God alone, and the scholars' contribution is faith. The other reading joins the two halves: the well-grounded know the meaning too, and their words of faith follow as their response to it.

Each position has a reason. The first preserves the verse's own warning: if even the skilled can assign meanings to the elusive, the door the deviant heart walks through is left ajar, and the scholars keep the field. The second rests on the sentence that follows, which has the well-grounded speaking with knowledge in their possession rather than confessing ignorance.

Neither reading changes the practical demand. On both, the well-grounded are described as people who accept the whole Book.

**"We Believe in It; It Is All From Our Lord"**

The answer given by the well-grounded is not an interpretation. It is a statement about the source: everything in the Book, the plain and the difficult, comes from the same Lord, so the difficult passages are not a reason to distrust the plain ones, and the plain ones are not a reason to discard the difficult.

That is a posture, not a technique, and the verse closes by naming the faculty it requires: *none will be mindful of this except people of reason*. The Qur'an repeats the phrase at 2:269 and 13:19 in connection with wisdom and with recognising revealed truth.

The Prophet gave his community a way to recognise the method rather than the person. ʿĀʾishah reports that he recited this verse and then said: *If you see those who follow what is elusive in it, they are the ones God has named, so beware of them* (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī 4547).

---

## Verse 3:8

> ˹They say,˺ “Our Lord! Do not let our hearts deviate after you have guided us. Grant us Your mercy. You are indeed the Giver ˹of all bounties˺

**Zaygh: the Word for a Heart That Slips Sideways**

The verb translated "deviate" is *zaygh*, and it is the same word used two lines earlier for *those with deviant hearts* who chase the elusive verses. The link is deliberate. The disease described in 3:7 as a settled condition is the danger this prayer asks to be spared, and the difference between the two is not a difference of intellect but of footing. Deviation here is a movement, a tilt, a leaning off a straight line, not a sudden departure.

The Qur'an uses the root for hearts that turned after they had known better: *“So when they ˹persistently˺ deviated, Allah caused their hearts to deviate”* (61:5). It is used almost gently of the believers at a moment of extremity: *“after the hearts of a group of them had almost faltered”* (9:117) — a state that God then repaired. Hearts that tilt are not yet hearts that have left; the prayer exists because the tilt can begin before anyone notices it.

**The Heart Held Between Two Fingers**

The Prophet described the situation with an image this prayer fits exactly. ʿAbdullāh ibn ʿAmr reported that he said: *Verily, the hearts of the children of Adam, all of them, are between two fingers of the Merciful as one heart. He directs them wherever He wills* — and then he would add: *O Allah, the Director of the hearts, turn our hearts to Your obedience* (Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim 2654).

**Mercy From His Presence: the Word Ladun**

The second request uses a word the translation renders "Your mercy" with an added implication in the Arabic: *raḥmatan min ladunka*, mercy from *ladun*, a preposition meaning "in the possession or presence of" someone. It is the word of a gift given from inside the giver, not passed along from elsewhere.

The Qur'an uses it when the need is extreme. The youths of the cave pray: *“Our Lord! Grant us mercy from Yourself and guide us rightly through our ordeal.”* (18:10), and Moses finds a servant whom God had granted *“mercy from Us and enlightened with knowledge of Our Own”* (18:65). Muslim scholars later coined the phrase *al-ʿilm al-ladunī* — knowledge given directly by God — from the second of these. The supplicant in this verse asks for exactly that kind of mercy: mercy that comes from the source and needs no intermediary to be enough.

**"Giver of All Bounties": Asking in the Right Name**

The prayer closes by naming God *al-Wahhāb*, the one who gives constantly and freely, on a form in Arabic that suggests abundance and repetition rather than a single bestowal. The name matches the request. Someone asking for mercy from the divine presence addresses the one whose habit is giving.

---

## Verse 3:9

> Our Lord! You will certainly gather all humanity for the ˹promised˺ Day—about which there is no doubt. Surely Allah does not break His promise.”

**Mīʿād: the Appointed Meeting**

The word translated "promised Day" is *mīʿād*, a noun for an appointment, a place or time fixed in advance when two parties meet. It is related to *mawʿid* with the same sense, and both come from the root that gives *waʿd*, a promise, and *waʿīd*, a threat.

The Qur'an uses the family of words to say something specific about the Last Day. It is not an event that happens; it is an event that is scheduled. *“Say, ˹O Prophet,˺ ‘A Day has ˹already˺ been appointed for you, which you can neither delay nor advance by a ˹single˺ moment.’”* (34:30). The Qur'an attaches the same language to the good news for the righteous: *“But those mindful of their Lord will have ˹elevated˺ mansions, built one above the other, under which rivers flow. ˹That is˺ the promise of Allah. ˹And˺ Allah never fails in ˹His˺ promise.”* (39:20). Threat and promise share one vocabulary, which is how the Book trains a reader to treat both as appointments rather than moods.

The phrase attached to the Day recurs in verse after verse in the Book, always in the same form. It is stated in the context of resurrection: *“And certainly the Hour is coming, there is no doubt about it. And Allah will surely resurrect those in the graves.”* (22:7). It is stated to those who think the question can be postponed: *“˹It is˺ Allah ˹Who˺ gives you life, then causes you to die, then will gather you ˹all˺ on the Day of Judgment, about which there is no doubt. But most people do not know.”* (45:26).

**The Confidence That God Keeps His Word**

The prayer ends with the grounds of its own confidence: *Surely Allah does not break His promise.* God's truthfulness is not a trait fastened on to His power; it is what makes His promises worth building a life on. The Book says it of protection as well as of threat — *“So do not think ˹O Prophet˺ that Allah will fail to keep His promise to His messengers. Allah is indeed Almighty, capable of punishment.”* (14:47) — and 13:31 closes with the same assurance after describing the disasters that will pursue the deniers.

The well-grounded state it once more in their longer prayer later in this sūrah, where the appeal is to the promise itself: *“Our Lord! Grant us what You have promised us through Your messengers and do not put us to shame on Judgment Day—for certainly You never fail in Your promise.”* (3:194). Faith in this Book is not credulity about God's generosity. It is confidence in a speaker who has never had to be taken back, and the prayer of verse 9 is what that confidence sounds like when a believer says it out loud.

The companions of the Prophet said the same in their own words at 3:194, asking that the promise be granted and that they not be disgraced.

---

## Verse 3:10

> Indeed, neither the wealth nor children of the disbelievers will be of any benefit to them against Allah—and they will be the fuel for the Fire

**"Against Allah" — the Preposition That Decides the Sentence**

The Qur'an uses the same construction in a verse that closes the loop: *“Neither their wealth nor children will be of any help to them against Allah whatsoever. It is they who will be the residents of the Fire. They will be there forever.”* (58:17). The phrase *against Allah* assumes that the crisis a person will actually face is a reckoning with God, and it measures everything a person owns by its usefulness for that moment.

Once the measure is set, the Book's scattered statements about prosperity fall into one doctrine. Wealth and children are *adornment* of this life while good deeds outlast it (18:46). They can be a *test*, not a certificate (8:28; 64:15). They can turn from support into diversion: *“O believers! Do not let your wealth or your children divert you from the remembrance of Allah. For whoever does so, it is they who are the ˹true˺ losers.”* (63:9). The Book never denies that these things are good in themselves; it refuses to let them be evidence of anything.

**Fuel for the Fire: the Image and Where It Recurs**

The same image anchors the Book's earliest warning to its opponents: *“then fear the Fire fuelled with people and stones, which is prepared for the disbelievers.”* (2:24), and the believers are taught to protect their households from that same fire (66:6). Polytheists and their objects of worship are named together as its fuel (21:98), which is worth noticing: in this image nobody's status survives. The king and the stone are consumed by the same appetite.

Notice also where the fuel comes from. The verse does not say the deniers will be thrown into a fire that burns other things; it says they themselves are the fuel, which means the fire's continuance is drawn from what they are. The Qur'an describes the reciprocal case in the punishment of hoarders, whose own treasure is heated until it marks them (9:35). What a person has accumulated, whether metal or self, is turned into the substance of his penalty. That is the reversal the image carries: the security a man spent his life assembling becomes the material of the thing he feared.

**When a Blessing Becomes Evidence Against You**

The Qur'an is willing to follow the doctrine to a conclusion that unsettles comfortable readers: the very gifts can be a means of punishment. *“So let neither their wealth nor children impress you ˹O Prophet˺. Allah only intends to torment them through these things in this worldly life, then their souls will depart while they are disbelievers.”* (9:55).

A believer reading that sentence has to ask what he is doing with what he has been given. And the Book elsewhere shows the same goods being hoarded into a specific kind of condemnation: treasures heated until they brand the skin of the one who accumulated them, with the words, *“This is the treasure you hoarded for yourselves. Now taste what you hoarded!”* (9:35). Nothing in either passage makes a rich man a bad man. Both passages insist that property is an instrument with a direction, and that its direction is the only thing that survives its loss.

---

## Verse 3:11

> Their fate will be like that of the people of Pharaoh and those before them—they all rejected Our signs, so Allah seized them for their sins. And Allah is severe in punishment

**A Comparison That Is Also a Verdict**

The verse answers the previous sentence about wealth and children by naming a case, which is the Qur'an's standard method for confirming a principle: it points at a defeated power and asks the audience to look. What happened to the people of Pharaoh is not being recalled as ancient history for its own sake. It is offered as the fate that repeats.

*Daʾb*, the word behind "fate" in this translation, means a wont, a regular way of proceeding, the manner in which something habitually happens. The same comparison appears with the same vocabulary at 8:52: *“Their fate is that of the people of Pharaoh and those before them—they all disbelieved in Allah’s signs, so Allah seized them for their sins. Indeed, Allah is All-Powerful, severe in punishment.”* By calling the destruction a habit, the Qur'an presents it as a law that anyone can verify in the record of nations, not a stroke of unusual severity.

**"And Those Before Them" — the Chain That Makes the Lesson General**

The same method appears in the list of consumed nations: *“So We seized each ˹people˺ for their sin: against some of them We sent a storm of stones, some were overtaken by a ˹mighty˺ blast, some We caused the earth to swallow, and some We drowned. Allah would not have wronged them, but it was they who wronged themselves.”* (29:40). *“like the fate of the people of Noah, ’Ȃd, Thamûd, and those after them. For Allah would never will to wrong ˹His˺ servants.”* (40:31). The variety of punishments matters less than the uniformity of the charge, and the closing sentence of 29:40 removes the only defence that could be raised: the accusation at trial is against the accused, not the judge.

**One Charge, Stated Over Every Nation**

The pattern is spelled out in the fate of the Egyptians, where the signs are named one by one: *“So We plagued them with floods, locusts, lice, frogs, and blood—all as clear signs, but they persisted in arrogance and were a wicked people.”* (7:133). Their doom is stated as a direct consequence of that persistence: *“So We inflicted punishment upon them, drowning them in the sea for denying Our signs and being heedless of them.”* (7:136). The Book ties a nation's destruction to a decision it kept making in the presence of evidence.

**The Word That Can Mean Either a Case or a Way**

The illustration the commentators attached to the warning comes from the story of Egypt, where the Book lists the floods, locusts, lice, frogs, and blood that were sent as clear signs while the people remained arrogant (7:133). The verb rendered as seized was explained through its use elsewhere, where being taken by God means being punished, as in the demand made of Moses for a visible proof and in the warning given to the people of Thamūd about the she-camel (4:153; 7:73).

The related verb in 8:54 tells the same story with the sea as the instrument and the sins as the reason: *“That was the case with Pharaoh’s people and those before them—they all rejected the signs of their Lord, so We destroyed them for their sins and drowned Pharaoh’s people. They were all wrongdoers.”* Nobody in the narrative is ambushed.

The same attribute is attached to God in contexts about stewardship, since He is exact as well as severe: *“He is the One Who has placed you as successors on earth and elevated some of you in rank over others, so He may test you with what He has given you. Surely your Lord is swift in punishment, but He is certainly AllForgiving, Most Merciful.”* (6:165).

---

## Verse 3:12

> ˹O Prophet!˺ Tell the disbelievers, “Soon you will be overpowered and driven to Hell—what an evil place to rest!”

**"Soon You Will Be Overpowered" — Certainty in the Future Tense**

*Sa-tughlabūna* uses the Arabic future prefix with the particle of nearness: "you are going to be overpowered, and soon". There is no condition attached and no escape clause offered. Whatever the audience does — fight, negotiate, wait, trade — the outcome is fixed, and the immediacy of "soon" gives it a date without giving a day.

The Qur'an sets this same assurance beside the events it refers to. The community is warned against reading the prosperity of its opponents as evidence of divine approval: *“Do not be deceived by the prosperity of the disbelievers throughout the land. It is only a brief enjoyment. Then Hell will be their home—what an evil place to rest!”* (3:196–197). The phrase that closes verse 12 closes those verses too. A reader who meets the same sentence twice in the same sūrah is meant to hear it as a refrain, one that holds the beginning and the near-end of the argument together.

**Mihād: a Resting Place That Is Not Rest**

The last words — "what an evil place to rest" — render *mihād*, which lexically means a place smoothed and prepared for someone to lie down: a bed, a cradle, a floor spread out. The same word family is used of the earth itself as a resting surface for human beings: *“Have We not smoothed out the earth ˹like a bed˺”* (78:6), and other passages use the related *mahd* for the earth as a cradle (43:10).

The Prophet is told to *tell* them, not to persuade them, and the message is a statement about the future rather than a demand.

**A Report That Some Readers Connected With a Market-Place Warning**

The commentators report that the Prophet delivered a warning of this kind in Madinah after Badr, addressing the Banū Qaynuqāʿ in their market and telling them that a fate like the Quraysh's could be theirs. The account is preserved in the sīrah and tafsīr literature rather than in the sound collections, and it is mentioned here as background for how the community heard such verses, not as established ḥadīth.

.

---

## Verse 3:13

> Indeed, there was a sign for you in the two armies that met in battle—one fighting for the cause of Allah and the other in denial. The believers saw their enemy twice their number. But Allah supports with His victory whoever He wills. Surely in this is a lesson for people of insight

**Muslim 1763 and the Night of the Supplication**

The Prophet's own state before the fighting is remembered in a report that is difficult to read without stopping. He turned to the *qibla*, stretched out his hands, and pleaded with God to bring about what had been promised to him, adding: *O Allah, if this small band of Muslims is destroyed, You will not be worshipped on this earth* (Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim 1763). His cloak slipped from his shoulders while he prayed; Abū Bakr picked it up, wrapped it round him, and told him that his Lord would fulfil what He had promised.

**"Twice Their Number" — the Art of Seeing Numbers**

The verse reports what the believers saw: their enemy at double their strength. The concrete figures behind it are given in other accounts — the Quraysh said to number about a thousand, the Muslims a little over three hundred — and the discrepancy between "double" here and "triple" there has occupied the commentators.

**What the Sign Actually Demonstrates**

The sūrah's other references to the day fill the picture without changing its point. The believer is told elsewhere that the enemy was made to appear few in his eyes so that a purpose already decided could be carried out (8:44), and the believers are told how their cry for help was answered with angels (8:9–10). The reinforcements, the arithmetic, and the outcome are one story. Whoever reads only the count of troops reads the smallest part of what the day contains, and the verse's final word — insight — is a reminder that such readings are available only to those willing to look past the surface.

---

The verse's two clauses are the sūrah's doctrine of battle in a single line: the two armies looked unequal, and the outcome was decided by what the parties brought rather than by the count. The account of the encounter fills out the same point in the sūrah of the Spoils, where God made the enemy appear few in the believers' eyes and made the believers appear few in the enemy's, so that what had been destined would be established (8:44). The section's report of the Prophet's supplication on the night before Badr, with his hands stretched toward the qibla and his plea that God would bring about what he had been promised, is the human side of the same verse, and the Book records the answer in the descent of the angels and the rout of the outnumbering force.

## Verse 3:14

> The enjoyment of ˹worldly˺ desires—women, children, treasures of gold and silver, fine horses, cattle, and fertile land—has been made appealing to people. These are the pleasures of this worldly life, but with Allah is the finest destination

**"Made Appealing" — the Passive Voice and Its Meaning**

*Zuyyina* is passive: the appeal was made, laid on, arranged. The verse does not say that people chose to desire these things from a neutral starting point; it says the desire was placed in them, like decoration on a surface. The same verb is used of the world's attraction for those who reject the message: *“The life of this world has been made appealing to the disbelievers, and they mock the believers.”* (2:212).

Nothing in the sentence calls the objects evil. A wife and children, gold and silver, horses, herds, and farmland are all described elsewhere in the Book as good things, and gratitude for them is commanded.

**"Pleasures of This Worldly Life" — the Word matāʿ**

The Qur'an uses the term this way throughout its descriptions of the world. This life is *“no more than play, amusement, luxury, mutual boasting, and competition in wealth and children”*, like crops that flourish and then dry up (57:20), and like rain whose harvest the earth presents beautifully until it is mown down (10:24). The sūrah of Competition states the same with two lines of extraordinary brevity: *“Competition for more ˹gains˺ diverts you ˹from Allah˺, until you end up in ˹your graves˺.”* (102:1–2). The word *matāʿ* is doing the work at the end of this verse, and it is a quiet word for a serious idea: what looks like a destination is equipment.

**The Diction That Punctuates the List**

Two details in the Arabic repay attention. Gold and silver are gathered in *qanāṭīr*, a plural of *qinṭār*, a measure the grammarians disputed — some described it as a weight of gold, others as a quantity that could not be counted. The translation's "treasures" preserves the amplification without inventing a figure. The commentators mostly agree that in this context the number is beside the point; the word is there to sound large, and it does.

**The Comparison, Not the Condemnation**

What it attacks is the scaling of desire above everything else. The Book states the danger in a form that leaves no room for ambiguity: *“If your parents and children and siblings and spouses and extended family and the wealth you have acquired and the trade you fear will decline and the homes you cherish—˹if all these˺ are more beloved to you than Allah and His Messenger and struggling in His Way, then wait until Allah brings about His Will.”* (9:24).

---

## Verse 3:15

> Say, ˹O Prophet,˺ “Shall I inform you of what is better than ˹all of˺ this? Those mindful ˹of Allah˺ will have Gardens with their Lord under which rivers flow, to stay there forever, and pure spouses, along with Allah’s pleasure.” And Allah is All-Seeing of ˹His˺ servants

**Rivers and Spouses and "To Stay There Forever"**

The description begins with the standard marks of the Garden: gardens belonging to the Lord, rivers running beneath them, permanence, and pure spouses. Each element corrects a limitation of the world it is being weighed against.

Rivers that flow under a garden are an image of abundance that does not have to be fought for. The Book's other descriptions add that they flow with rivers of water, milk, wine, and honey (47:15), and the dwellers are told that the rivers are theirs to manage and the fruits never exhausted. The spouses *made pure* are named in 2:25 and 4:57 with the promise that the dwellers will remain there always, and the purity is not only moral: it removes the weariness and disappointment that attend every human intimacy. Permanence is the last item precisely because it is the answer to the first verse's deepest problem — that everything desirable here is temporary.

The comparison is silent but total. Nothing on the earlier list is immune to loss. Nothing here can be lost.

**"Along With Allah's Pleasure" — the Item at the Top**

The Book describes this contentment as reciprocal, and its language for it is intimate. At 5:119 the declaration is *“Allah is pleased with them and they are pleased with Him”*, and the same pair of clauses closes 98:8. The fullest statement is given to the soul at the gate: *“Return to your Lord, well pleased ˹with Him˺ and well pleasing ˹to Him˺.”* (89:28). If the gardens are the answer to the world's instability, this clause answers the world's loneliness — the deepest human want, which is not to own good things but to be welcomed by the one who made them.

**Who Enters: the Mindful, Not the Ascetic**

The condition given is a single word: *those mindful*. *Taqwā* here is the same quality the Book names at the beginning of the sūrah of the Cow: *“This is the Book! There is no doubt about it—a guide for those mindful ˹of Allah˺”* (2:2). It is a watchfulness, a guardedness about one's own conduct before God.

**Where the Word for Contentment Is Used Elsewhere**

The term rendered here as pleasure is the same word the Book uses when it speaks of God's good pleasure as the highest item in the reward. A passage in the sūrah of Repentance promises the believers gardens and splendid homes and then adds, as the greatest of them, *“the pleasure of Allah. That is ˹truly˺ the ultimate triumph.”* (9:72). The same state is described in the sūrah of the Table as a mutual contentment: *“Allah is pleased with them and they are pleased with Him.”* (5:119).

The word also belongs to this world. The Book speaks of people who seek God's good pleasure in their conduct long before they reach any reward (5:16; 48:29; 57:27; 59:8), and it sets that pursuit against its opposite in the form of a question: *“Are those who seek Allah’s pleasure like those who deserve Allah’s wrath?”* (3:162). The pair appears again in the sūrah of Muḥammad, where the people of the second kind are described as following what displeases God (47:28). The contentment named in this verse is therefore the end of a pursuit that begins in the present life, and its opposite is not indifference but wrath.

---

## Verse 3:16

> who pray, “Our Lord! We have believed, so forgive our sins and protect us from the torment of the Fire.”

**The Clause That Tells Us Who They Are**

The previous verse ends with the phrase *All-Seeing of His servants*, and this verse picks the sentence up without a new beginning: *who pray*. The Arabic relative construction ties the people described here to the servants just named, so the reader learns that the mindful ones are identified by what they say to God when nobody else is listening.

**The Qur'an Defines Groups by Their Supplication**

Naming a community by its prayer is a habit of this Book, and the habit is instructive. The people of understanding are introduced by what they ask for: *“˹They are˺ those who remember Allah while standing, sitting, and lying on their sides... ˹and pray˺, ‘Our Lord! You have not created ˹all of˺ this without purpose. Glory be to You! Protect us from the torment of the Fire.’”* (3:191). The balanced worshipper is the one who asks: *“Our Lord! Grant us the good of this world and the Hereafter, and protect us from the torment of the Fire.”* (2:201). Even the angels who carry the Throne are quoted in prayer, interceding for the believers before God: *“Our Lord! You encompass everything in ˹Your˺ mercy and knowledge. So forgive those who repent and follow Your Way, and protect them from the torment of the Hellfire.”* (40:7).

**"Shield Us": the Verb and Its Root**

The word rendered "protect us" is *qinā*, from the same root as *taqwā*, the mindfulness that verse 15 made the condition of entry. A shield is a thing that stands between a person and a blow; the verb means to place such a covering over someone.

The connection between the two verses is therefore deliberate at the level of the lexicon. The mindful person is one who has been shielded, and his mindfulness is the trace of that protection in his conduct. The same root appears in the description of the Helpers of Madinah, who were praised for giving preference to others over themselves: *“And whoever is saved from the selfishness of their own souls, it is they who are ˹truly˺ successful.”* (59:9). The danger a person most needs shielding from turns out to be inside him, and 64:16 repeats the promise in the same words. Forgiveness covers what is done; the shield stands between a man and what he might still do.

**A Prayer Said in the Plural**

Both requests are phrased in the first person plural: *forgive us*, *protect us*. The verse is describing a group of people who speak as one even when they are alone, and the grammar repeats the opening — *our Lord* — which is the same address the well-grounded used in verse 8 and again in verse 9.

---

## Verse 3:17

> ˹It is they˺ who are patient, sincere, obedient, and charitable, and who pray for forgiveness before dawn

**Five Marks, and the Order They Come In**

The verse draws a portrait in a line. The people promised the gardens are *patient*, meaning steadfast under pressure; *sincere*, meaning truthful in word and intention; *obedient* in the specific sense of devotion that stands and waits before God; *charitable*, spending from what they have; and finally those who ask forgiveness in the last hours of the night.

**Qānitūn: the Posture Behind the Word**

The translation renders *qānit* as "obedient", which is accurate but thin. The word carries standing, constancy, silence, and a settled conformity to what is asked — the posture of someone who has taken his place and stayed there. The Book uses it of Mary, told to be *devout to her Lord* along with those who bow (3:43), and of the women and men who obey with devotion in 33:31.

In older Arabic a *qānit* could be the one who stands silent in prayer, and the meanings connect: devotion here is not an emotional warmth that comes and goes, but a stance. A person who has understood what he is before God does not need to be persuaded into position again each time his feelings change. That is why the word sits between truthfulness and charity in a list of durable qualities; it is the quality that makes the others durable.

**Charity in the Qur'an's Sense Is Not Only Money**

The participle rendered "charitable" belongs to the verb *anfaqa*, to spend. The commentaries were careful to say that the spending in view is not limited to currency: effort, knowledge, time, and influence all fall under it. The Book's praise for those who spend attaches a condition to the act — that it be in the way of God — which is what distinguishes the generosity being praised from generosity performed for reputation.

**"Before Dawn": an Hour Chosen for Its Darkness**

The final phrase translates *asḥār*, the last part of the night before the sky begins to lighten, and the choice is deliberate. The Book describes the night prayer as a habit of the righteous in more than one place: they *“used to sleep only little in the night. Pray for forgiveness before dawn”* (51:17–18), and the Prophet is told to *“rise at ˹the last˺ part of the night, offering additional prayers, so your Lord may raise you to a station of praise.”* (17:79).

The hour has nothing to recommend it to a person seeking an audience. It is the time when sleep is heaviest and no one is watching, when the body argues against getting up and there is no reputation to be gained by doing so. The Book mentions those who stand in it as a class of their own: *“˹They are˺ those who spend ˹a good portion of˺ the night, prostrating themselves and standing before their Lord.”* (25:64). The command to the Prophet runs to the same effect: *“Stand all night ˹in prayer˺ except a little”* (73:2).

---

## Verse 3:18

> Allah ˹Himself˺ is a Witness that there is no god ˹worthy of worship˺ except Him—and so are the angels and people of knowledge. He is the Maintainer of justice. There is no god ˹worthy of worship˺ except Him—the Almighty, All-Wise

**The Three Witnesses and the Order Between Them**

God's witness comes first, and it needs no corroboration: He testifies about Himself, which the Book elsewhere describes as a sufficiency — *“And Allah ˹alone˺ is sufficient as a Witness.”* (4:166). The angels come next because they are the created beings whose knowledge of God is unclouded by appetite or interest. The people of knowledge come last, and their inclusion is the verse's real surprise.

A human being qualifies as a witness to the unity of God not by lineage, authority, or office, but by knowledge. That single word opens the testimony to anyone who studies the evidence in the world and in revelation and reaches the conclusion the verse states, and it also sets the standard against which a scholar's claim is measured. The `ulamāʾ` of this verse are not the custodians of a privilege; they are the ones whose learning leads them to bear witness.

**"Maintainer of Justice": the Phrase in the Middle**

Between the two halves of the testimony sits a sentence about God's own standing: *He is the Maintainer of justice*. The participle *qāʾim* conveys standing, upholding, and keeping something in place, and *qisṭ* is equity — the giving to each what is due. God is not described as merely just in His rulings. He is described as the one who keeps justice standing in existence.

**Testifying to God and Testifying in Court**

The Book repeatedly takes the vocabulary of this verse into the public sphere. Believers are commanded to become witnesses in the same sense: *“O believers! Stand firm for Allah and bear true testimony. Do not let the hatred of a people lead you to injustice.”* (5:8), and the command is sharpened to include the hardest cases: *“Stand firm for justice as witnesses for Allah even if it is against yourselves, your parents, or close relatives.”* (4:135).

**The Sentence That Opens and Closes the Verse**

The declaration *there is no god except Him* appears twice in this verse, once as the content of the testimony and once attached to the names Almighty and All-Wise. The repetition is a frame, and it mirrors the structure of the sūrah itself: the same words opened it at 3:2, and they will return at intervals as the argument develops.

---

## Verse 3:19

> Certainly, Allah’s only Way is Islam. Those who were given the Scripture did not dispute ˹among themselves˺ out of mutual envy until knowledge came to them. Whoever denies Allah’s signs, then surely Allah is swift in reckoning

**"Islam" as a Description Before It Became the Name of a Community**

The Arabic word *islām* means handing oneself over, submitting, placing one's affairs in the hands of another. Long before it named the community that gathered around the Prophet Muhammad, the Book applies its participle to people who lived centuries earlier. Abraham and Jacob are recorded advising their children: *“Indeed, Allah has chosen for you this faith. So do not die except in ˹a state of full˺ submission.”* (2:132), and the disciples of Jesus answer him in the same vocabulary: *“We believe in Allah, so bear witness that we have submitted.”* (3:52), and again at 5:111.

The sūrah's later verse — *“Whoever seeks a way other than Islam, it will never be accepted from them”* (3:85) — is often read back into this one, and the two do belong together. What the Book presents here is a single demand made of every person: not membership in a particular community, but the surrender of the self to God. The community that bears the name is the community that has been given the task of carrying that demand to everyone else.

**The Dispute Began in Envy, Not in Ignorance**

The second sentence gives the diagnosis of the division among the scriptured peoples: they fell into disagreement *out of mutual envy*, and they did so *after* knowledge reached them. The accusation is precise. The problem was never an absence of information; the clearer the truth became, the harder the parties pushed against one another.

The Book states the same history elsewhere with the same emphasis: *“It was not until this clear proof came to the People of the Book that they became divided ˹about his prophethood˺”* (98:4). The Arabic term for envy and transgressive rivalry, *baghy*, carries the sense of wanting what another has and being unwilling to see him hold it. Applied to revelation, it means the parties could not accept that the truth might arrive through someone from outside their own community.

**"Swift in Reckoning": the Word ḥisāb**

The Qur'an describes the same audit with an image of scales that will not miss the smallest weight: *“We will set up the scales of justice on the Day of Judgment, so no soul will be wronged in the least.”* (21:47). The verses about the reckoning of deeds are not there to terrorise; they are the background against which the human claim to superiority loses its force. Whoever denies the signs, whether out of envy or out of pride, will meet an account that has no interest in the rank he held among his people.

**The Verse's Three Movements**

The sentence moves in three steps, and each of them is taken up again later in the sūrah. The first names the religion that God accepts, and the passage that follows states that whoever seeks a way other than submission will not have it accepted from him (3:85). The second describes the dispute that began after knowledge arrived, and the warning given to the believers against dividing after the proofs reached them repeats the same diagnosis for a new community (3:105). The third declares that God is swift in reckoning, and the same word for reckoning is used at the end of the sūrah, where it closes the description of those whose reward is with their Lord (3:199).

---

## Verse 3:20

> So if they argue with you ˹O Prophet˺, say, “I have submitted myself to Allah, and so have my followers.” And ask those who were given the Scripture and the illiterate ˹people˺, “Have you submitted yourselves ˹to Allah˺?” If they submit, they will be ˹rightly˺ guided. But if they turn away, then your duty is only to deliver ˹the message˺. And Allah is All-Seeing of ˹His˺ servants

**"I Have Submitted Myself" — a Verb, Not a Label**

The Arabic is *aslamtu*, from the same root as *islām* in the verse before it: I have handed myself over. The statement is an act, described in the first person singular, and it is followed immediately by a claim about a community: *and so have my followers*.

**The Two Groups Who Are Asked**

The question goes to two audiences: *those who were given the Scripture* and *the illiterate*. The second term, *al-ummīyūn*, describes people without a revealed book — those who had no scripture to argue from, and among whom the Prophet himself appeared, which is why the Book calls him an unlettered prophet (7:158).

**"Your Duty Is Only to Deliver the Message"**

The verse closes the invitation by removing a burden the Prophet might have carried: the outcome is not his responsibility. If the questioners turn away, his task is finished rather than failed, and the Book says as much elsewhere with unusual directness: *“So, ˹continue to˺ remind ˹all, O Prophet˺, for your duty is only to remind. You are not ˹there˺ to compel them ˹to believe˺.”* (88:21–22).

The same limit is stated about guidance generally: *“You are not responsible for people's guidance ˹O Prophet˺—it is Allah Who guides whoever He wills.”* (2:272). The instruction shapes how the message is delivered in practice. A messenger who believes he must produce the result will be tempted to coerce, to exaggerate, or to trade away parts of the message; a messenger who knows his duty ends at delivery is free to be honest. The Book's model of preaching rests on that division of labour.

**The Attribute That Sees Servants**

Both are servants of the one who sees them, and the sūrah has already told its readers how accurately He sees. Nothing in the closing clause invites a community to assess its own membership. It invites each person to consider what is visible to the one who will assess him.

**What the Verse Says and to Whom**
The verse has the Prophet declare his submission to God and his following of the community of the believers, and then it tells him what to say to the People of the Book and to the men who know nothing: whether they have submitted or turned away, and that his duty is only the delivery. The Book gives the same instruction in the Sūrah of the Bee, where the Prophet is told to call to the way of his Lord and to bear with the people (16:125), and it repeats the limit on his responsibility in the Sūrah of the Table and the Sūrah of the Cave.
**What the Word Is Doing**
The verse's use of the word submit for the Prophet's own position is deliberate, and the commentators read it as an answer to the men who were asking him to join their religion or to take theirs. The Sūrah of the Cow states the same refusal in the account of Abraham, who is called neither a Jew nor a Christian but one who submitted (2:140), and the Sūrah of the Family of ʿImrān's own account of the two groups' claims comes just before this verse. The word is the same one that names the religion, and the verse puts it in the first person as a statement of the Prophet's own position rather than a demand of others.
**What the Verse Leaves With the Hearer**
The verse ends with the sentence that the believing community is the one that has submitted, and it leaves the rest to the Day. The sūrah then turns to the signs in the creation, and the passage after it addresses the People of the Book directly with an invitation to agree on the worship of God alone (3:64). The verse in front of us is the first movement of that argument, and its function is to state the two positions plainly before the dialogue begins.

---

## Verse 3:21

> Indeed, those who deny Allah’s signs, kill the prophets unjustly, and kill people who stand up for justice—give them good news of a painful punishment

**Who Are "Those Who Enjoin Justice"?**

The middle of the verse concerns prophets, but the end concerns people who stand up for justice. The Arabic phrase describes those who command equity — a category that includes prophets and extends past them to ordinary men and women who insist on what is right in the presence of power.

The commentators found the point significant, and their evidence is prophetic. The Prophet is reported to have said that among the greatest forms of struggle is *a word of justice spoken before a tyrannical ruler* (Sunan Abī Dāwūd 4344; Sunan al-Tirmidhī 2174, graded ḥasan by virtue of corroborating narrations). The Book itself provides a portrait of such a man at the edge of a city, running toward danger to support the messengers: a figure whose brief narrative ends with the reward of Paradise. The verse's indictment therefore covers more than the murder of prophets. It covers the murder of anyone who says that the powerful must be just.

**"Give Them Good News" — the Inversion of a Kind Word**

The same startling inversion appears elsewhere: *“Give good news of a painful punishment to hypocrites”* (4:138). The figure works because it refuses to soften the message at the last moment. A sentence delivered as news is spoken in the tone of a messenger reporting what has been decided, and the reader who flinches at the wording has understood the point the Book is making: the most terrible events in this verse are reported in the calmest grammar available.

**The Killing of the Prophets in the Record of Israel**

The Book presents the killing of messengers as a recurring feature of the history of the Children of Israel, and it does so without hedging. In the wilderness narrative, the punishment is traced directly to it: *“they invited the displeasure of Allah for rejecting Allah’s signs and unjustly killing the prophets.”* (2:61). The charge is repeated in the account of the covenant broken and renewed: *“˹They were condemned˺ for breaking their covenant, rejecting Allah’s signs, killing the prophets unjustly”* (4:155).

The pattern is stated as normal rather than exceptional in 5:70: *“Whenever a messenger came to them with what they did not desire, they denied some and killed others.”* The verse above inherits that record and treats it as a habit that generates its own future. The Book addresses the community that carried the habit in the present tense. Commentators relate that this verse was understood to refer to particular groups among the Children of Israel, and some transmit a report that forty-three prophets were killed in a single morning and that a company of men who stood up to denounce the killing were killed with them on the same day.

.

---

## Verse 3:22

> They are the ones whose deeds are wasted in this world and the Hereafter. And they will have no helpers

**Deeds That Come to Nothing**

The Arabic verb is *ḥabiṭa*, used of something that loses its value and effect, and the Book applies it to the works of people whose religious effort was considerable. The word appears in several places with the same meaning: associating others with God voids a person's deeds (39:65), and so does despising what God has revealed (47:9).

**"In This World and the Hereafter"**

The loss is stated twice, and the two halves describe different kinds of failure. In this world, the work loses its fruit: the Book compares the deeds of those who disbelieve in their Lord to *“ashes fiercely blown away by wind on a stormy day. They will gain nothing from what they have earned.”* (14:18). The same image returns at the Day of Judgment, when God turns to whatever good they did and reduces it to scattered dust (25:23).

In the Hereafter the verdict is simpler: what weight there was has gone. *“It is they who reject the signs of their Lord and their meeting with Him, rendering their deeds void, so We will not give their deeds any weight on Judgment Day.”* (18:105). A scale with nothing on it settles the question, and the verse's double statement makes sure the reader understands that both currencies — the reputation of the world and the reward of the next — are lost by the same act.

**Why the Intention Decides the Deed**

The commentators explained the waste by pointing at intention. A good act performed by someone who has withheld the recognition owed to God is not a partial good that God overlooks; it is an act whose author has refused the relationship that would give it meaning. The Book says this in its own voice about a different group: *“That is because they detest what Allah has revealed, so He has rendered their deeds void.”* (47:9).

The principle is uncomfortable because it applies to worship. The men of Madinah and Makkah who recited scripture and prayed were not being told their practice was too small.

**"No Helpers" — the Removal of the Social Net**

The verse ends with a phrase that carries a great deal of the ancient world's reality. In that culture a person's security was his clan: helpers who would avenge him, ransom him, or plead for him. The Book repeatedly uses the impossibility of helpers in the Hereafter as the closing note of a warning, and it names each of the supports a person might hope for.

*“Guard yourselves against the Day on which no soul will be of help to another. No intercession will be accepted, no ransom taken, and no help will be given.”* (2:48). The wrongdoers will have *“neither a close friend nor intercessor to be heard”* (40:18), and where they had counted on the mediation of the righteous, the answer is flat: *“So the pleas of intercessors will be of no benefit to them.”* (74:48). The verse above states the same in two words. No alliance survives into a court where the judge needs no allies.

---

## Verse 3:23

> Have you not seen those who were given a portion of the Scriptures? Yet when they are invited to the Book of Allah to settle their disputes, some of them turn away heedlessly

**"A Portion of the Scriptures" — the Precision of the Phrase**

The verse does not say that these people were given the Scripture; it says they were given *a portion* of it, and the difference is the whole portrait. The Book uses the same phrase at 4:44 and 4:51 about people whose relationship with revelation is partial, and the choice of wording marks the gap between possessing texts and being had by their purpose.

**Invited to the Book of Allah**

The invitation is described as a call to *the Book of Allah* to settle a dispute. Commentators discussed which book is meant here, and the readings differ: some understood the reference to the Torah, since the Torah was the book these people already had and the one that could settle a matter within their own community. Others read it more broadly as the revelation that had now come to the Prophet.

The Book's complaint at 5:43 uses the narrower reading as its premise: *“But why do they come to you for judgment when they ˹already˺ have the Torah containing Allah’s judgment, then they turn away after all? They are not ˹true˺ believers.”* If the disputants are asked to consult their own scripture and refuse, the refusal is a verdict on their attachment to it.

**The Judicial Setting and the Refusal**

The scene is a courtroom rather than a classroom. The invitation is to have a case decided, and the Book's other account of the same behaviour makes the setting explicit: *“And as soon as they are called to Allah and His Messenger so he may judge between them, a group of them turns away.”* (24:48).

There is a reason the Book frames the issue as judgment instead of instruction. A person can listen to teaching and postpone; a person who accepts a judge has already conceded the authority of the decision before he knows what the decision will be. That concession is what the attendees of this verse refuse, and the sūrah states the standard elsewhere without qualification: *“they will never be ˹true˺ believers until they accept you ˹O Prophet˺ as the judge in their disputes, and find no resistance within themselves against your decision and submit wholeheartedly.”* (4:65). The hesitation being described is not doubt about evidence. It is a refusal in advance.

**"Some of Them" — the Verse Stops Short of Collectivising**

The turn away is attributed to *some of them*, and the qualifier is deliberate. The Book distinguishes carefully within communities, and it does so in verses that would have been much easier to write as general condemnations.

The same restraint appears in the stories of covenant and covenant-breaking told elsewhere: *“Do you believe in some of the Scripture and reject the rest?”* (2:85) addresses a specific conduct without declaring every member of a community guilty of it. The Qur'an's argument are aimed at positions and practices, and the reader is expected to keep the distinction that the text itself keeps. A community has custodians, dissenters, and quiet people, and the sentence here reaches only one of those groups.

---

## Verse 3:24

> This is because they say, “The Fire will not touch us except for a few days.” They have been deceived in their faith by their wishful lying

**The Claim That Made the Refusal Comfortable**

The previous verse described people turning away from a book that could settle their disputes. This verse gives the reason: a doctrine about their own future. They believed that whatever fire awaited them would be brief, and the belief was doing steady work in their lives, because a person who expects to be scorched lightly can afford to ignore the corrections he is offered. The convenience of the doctrine is part of what the sūrah is exposing, since a claim that removes the cost of disobedience will be defended long after its grounds have failed.

The Qur'an quotes the claim in an earlier sūrah in nearly the same words: *“˹Some of˺ the Jews claim, ‘The Fire will not touch us except for a number of days.’ Say, ˹O Prophet,˺ ‘Have you taken a pledge from Allah— for Allah never breaks His word—or are you ˹just˺ saying about Allah what you do not know?’”* (2:80). The formulation there exposes the logic. An exemption from punishment is a promise, and promises are made by the one who owns the punishment. Nobody had been told.

**A Doctrine That Serves Its Owners**

The Qur'an answers the theory elsewhere in the same tone: *“The Jews and the Christians each say, ‘We are the children of Allah and His most beloved!’ Say, ˹O Prophet,˺ ‘Why then does He punish you for your sins? No! You are only humans like others of His Own making.’”* (5:18). The answer does not argue about days. It denies the premise that any community's ancestry or standing changes the terms of the accounting, and it puts the claimants back where the sūrah put everyone: human beings made by the same hands.

**"Deceived in Their Faith" — the Grammar of Self-Deception**

The second sentence says they have been deceived *in their faith* by their own fabrications, and the phrasing keeps the responsibility where it belongs. The Book does not present them as victims of a trick played by someone else. What has deceived them is what they invented.

The Qur'an uses the vocabulary of deception for the world's false promises in other places, and it always names the deceiver plainly. The life of this world is a delusion (57:20); the promise of God is true, so do not let the life of this world deceive you (35:5); what emboldened a person against his Lord if not the assumption that generosity meant approval? (82:6). The pattern is consistent: the texts of the Book treat self-deception as an act a person performs on himself, with his own desires supplying the material.

---

## Verse 3:25

> But how ˹horrible˺ will it be when We gather them together on the Day about which there is no doubt—when every soul will be paid in full for what it has done, and none will be wronged

**An Exclamation Instead of a Scene**

The verse opens with a rhetorical question — how will it be when We gather them — and the question carries the whole weight of what follows.

A detailed description of the Day would occupy pages and invite arguments about its imagery; an exclamation focuses the reader on the contrast between the claim of verse 24 and the reality that answers it. The question is left hanging and then completed by the sentence about accounts that follow: the answer to "how will it be" is that every soul will be settled with, without exception.

**The Day About Which There Is No Doubt**

The phrase attached to the Day has already appeared in this sūrah, in the prayer of the well-grounded who asked God to gather all humanity on a Day about which there is no doubt (3:9). It recurs across the Book with the same formula: at 4:87 God swears by His own truthfulness that He will gather all of humanity, at 6:12 He has taken mercy upon Himself with the gathering as a settled fact. At 45:26 the gathering is announced to a people who do not know.

The repetition is a strategy. A statement that no one can successfully doubt, repeated in different contexts, removes the possibility of treating the Day as a speculative item that some groups believe in strongly. The Book treats the gathering as part of the structure of reality, and it uses the same formula whenever it wants a warning to land without the padding of an argument.

**"Every Soul Paid in Full"**

The sentence about payment uses the vocabulary of debts and wages, and the Book applies it consistently to the judgment. *“Every soul will be paid in full for its deeds, for Allah knows best what they have done.”* (39:70). *“˹Consider˺ the Day ˹when˺ every soul will come pleading for itself, and each will be paid in full for what it did, and none will be wronged.”* (16:111). The same formula closes the last of the legislative verses of the sūrah of the Cow: *“Be mindful of the Day when you will ˹all˺ be returned to Allah, then every soul will be paid in full for what it has done, and none will be wronged.”* (2:281).

**"None Will Be Wronged" — the Guarantee Inside the Threat**

The verse's final clause is an assurance, and it is attached to a scene that has just been described as horrifying. The Book joins the two repeatedly: the scales of justice will be set up and no soul will be wronged in the least (21:47), and the record laid open will not leave any deed, large or small, unlisted (18:49), and God never wrongs anyone even by the weight of an atom (4:40).

For the reader who had reason to fear the account, the clause is a strange comfort and a real one. The horror of the Day, as the Qur'an presents it, is not that the judgment is arbitrary. It is that it is exact. Nothing hidden is lost, nothing forgotten is forgiven by default, and no one stands in a queue behind the powerful.
