The same guarantee that terrifies the man who hoped his community's claim would cover him protects the man who has no advocate at all.

---

## Verse 3:26

> Say, ˹O Prophet,˺ “O Allah! Lord over all authorities! You give authority to whoever You please and remove it from who You please; You honour whoever You please and disgrace who You please—all good is in Your Hands. Surely You ˹alone˺ are Most Capable of everything

**Mālik al-Mulk: the Title and Its Weight**

The address *Lord over all authorities* renders *mālik al-mulk*, and the two words of the phrase are related to each other and to a family of terms: *malik*, a king; *mulk*, dominion or rule; *malakūt*, the inner sovereignty. The title appears at 67:1, where the Book states that all authority rests in His hands, and 25:2 describes Him as the one to whom the kingdom of the heavens and the earth belongs, who has never had offspring and no partner in governing.

The sūrah states the same claim at its end: *“To Allah ˹alone˺ belongs the kingdom of the heavens and the earth. And Allah is Most Capable of everything.”* (3:189). Setting that sentence beside this prayer shows how the Book approaches politics. Earthly rule is not ignored or dismissed; it is located inside a larger ownership, so that no throne is described as independent and no dynasty is described as permanent.

**"You Give Authority to Whoever You Please"**

The second clause turns the doctrine into history. Authority is granted and withdrawn by a will that is not accountable to the holders, and the Book illustrates the point with rulers of very different kinds. Saul is appointed over a people who complained that he lacked wealth and lineage, and the appointment is defended on grounds they had not considered: knowledge and stature (2:247). Nimrod argues with Abraham on the strength of his kingship and is silenced by a question about sunrise (2:258). Korah, who trusted in his own power, is swallowed by the earth with no helper left (28:81).

**"All Good Is in Your Hands"**

The sentence adds a restriction that is easy to pass over: all *good* is in His hands. No parallel clause says that evil is in them, and the commentators drew conclusions from the omission. Whatever God decrees is good in itself, even when it arrives as loss, and what appears evil in the world is attributable to the acts of creatures or to the limited view of the observer.

The Book supports the restriction with a blunt statement about jurisdiction: *“He cannot be questioned about what He does, but they will ˹all˺ be questioned.”* (21:23). That sentence is the reason the prayer can put the whole of political fortune into God's hands without embarrassment about the human cost. The Book does not explain every event; it locates the authority that governs events beyond the reach of cross-examination.

**"Most Capable of Everything" — the Ground of the Prayer**

The address closes with the attribute *qadīr*, capable of everything, and the sūrah repeats it at 3:29 and 3:189. Its function in this verse is to make the earlier clauses more than a description. It is one thing to say that God owns authority; it is another to say that He can move it, and the closing phrase states the second.

Without that attribute, the prayer would be a statement of resignation. With it, the prayer becomes a request that expects an answer, and the community praying it is told where to direct a petition about its own survival. Nothing in the sentence asks God to be generous to a particular side. It asks Him to do what He already does.

---

## Verse 3:27

> You cause the night to pass into the day and the day into the night. You bring forth the living from the dead and the dead from the living. And You provide for whoever You will without limit.”

**Two Sentences About Time and Life**

The prayer continues, and it moves from authority to two things that every human being experiences and no human being controls: the alternation of night and day, and the passage between life and death. Both sentences are phrased as ongoing actions — you cause, you bring forth — rather than as past events.

**The Merging of Night and Day**

The image in Arabic is of one period sliding into the next: the night drawn over the day and the day drawn over the night, with each stealing part of the other. The Book uses the same expression at 22:61, at 31:29 where the sun and moon are described as subservient and orbiting for a fixed term, and at 57:6, where the verse immediately adds that God knows what the heart conceals.

The progression of those passages is instructive. The physical observation belongs to everyone; the conclusion drawn from it is that the one who moves the hours also knows what happens inside them. Time in this Book is not a neutral container in which events occur. It is administered, which is why the Book so often connects the passing of days with the possibility of repentance: a person's remaining hours are borrowed from someone whose will governs the borrowing.

**Living From the Dead, Dead From the Living**

The Book uses the same formula to answer the denial of resurrection: *“Indeed, Allah is the One Who causes seeds and fruit stones to sprout. He brings forth the living from the dead and the dead from the living. That is Allah! How can you then be deluded ˹from the truth˺?”* (6:95), and again in a verse that closes with the resurrection itself: *“He brings forth the living from the dead and the dead from the living. And He gives life to the earth after its death. And so will you be brought forth ˹from the grave˺.”* (30:19). The argument is elegant: the thing being denied is happening in every field and every womb, and has been acknowledged by the denier every time he ate.

**"Without Limit" — the Phrase in Its Two Directions**

The prayer ends with provision *without limit*, *bi-ghayri ḥisāb*. The Arabic can mean that the gift is given without measure, and also that God gives without rendering an account to anyone. Both senses are present in the Book's usage. Provision without reckoning is promised to the generous at 24:38, to the believers at 40:40, and to the patient at 39:10, where the reward promised for patience is precisely the absence of a limit.

The phrase is a deliberate inversion of the way people think about resources. Human beings distribute what they have according to accounts kept — measures, entitlements, ledgers. The Book says the distribution of provision is not run on that system, which is why the visible world contains inequalities that no moral ledger explains. A person may find the arrangement unjust until he remembers that the accounts are being kept for another Day.

---

## Verse 3:28

> Believers should not take disbelievers as guardians instead of the believers—and whoever does so will have nothing to hope for from Allah—unless it is a precaution against their tyranny. And Allah warns you about Himself. And to Allah is the final return

**Walī: One Word Behind Several Relationships**

The term rendered "guardians" is *awliyāʾ*, the plural of *walī*, and the dictionaries give it a wide band of meanings: protector, ally, patron, client, close friend, the person to whom one's affairs are entrusted. In the tribal society the Qur'an addresses, the word described the relationship that determined a person's safety, obligations, and standing.

**"Instead of the Believers" — the Clause That Defines the Crime**

The Book draws the line itself in a passage that explains this verse at length. Toward those who are not at war with the Muslims, the instruction is generous: *“Allah does not forbid you from dealing kindly and fairly with those who have neither fought nor driven you out of your homes. Surely Allah loves those who are fair.”* (60:8). The prohibition is attached to the hostile case: *“Allah only forbids you from befriending those who have fought you for ˹your˺ faith, driven you out of your homes, or supported ˹others˺ in doing so.”* (60:9). The criterion throughout is conduct in the conflict, not the label a neighbour carries.

**The Consequence: Hope Withdrawn**

The same argument appears twice more in the Qur'an with a questioning tone: those who choose disbelievers as allies are asked whether they are seeking honour through that company, when all honour belongs to God (4:139); and the believers are warned against handing God a clear case against themselves (4:144). The point is not that God punishes a person for making friends. It is that a man who has placed his protection outside God's community has already decided where he expects help to come from, and the expectation is answered in the terms it was made.

**Prudence, Not Dissimulation: the Exception Stated Here**

The verse permits one escape: *unless it is a precaution against their tyranny*. The Arabic noun *tuqāh* is related to *taqwā*, the guardedness that protects a person from harm, and the commentators read the exception as covering a believer who fears for his life or his family under a power that would persecute him.

The Book's parallel ruling is stronger and clearer: a person who is forced to deny his faith verbally while his heart remains firm is not condemned (16:106). The commentators drew a careful boundary around this permission. Words spoken under compulsion may be excused; the major prohibitions of the religion may not be violated under their cover, and a coerced denial does not license theft, murder, or adultery. What the exception protects is a life, not a licence.

---

## Verse 3:29

> Say, ˹O Prophet,˺ “Whether you conceal what is in your hearts or reveal it, it is known to Allah. For He knows whatever is in the heavens and whatever is on the earth. And Allah is Most Capable of everything.”

**A Second Command to Speak**

The verse begins with the same imperative as verse 26: *say*. Once more, a statement is placed in the Prophet's mouth and directed at the community, and once more the subject is a fact about God rather than a rule about behaviour.

The placement is deliberate. The previous verse handled an external act — taking another community as protector — and this one reaches behind it.

**"It Is Known to Allah" — the Basis of the Sentence**

The claim that follows gives the reason: God knows whatever is in the heavens and on the earth. The Book states the same knowledge about the contents of hearts in verse after verse. *“And your Lord knows what their hearts conceal and what they reveal.”* (28:69). The same knowledge is described where it matters most, in a warning about mutual resentment among believers: *“When they meet you they say, ‘We believe.’ But when alone, they bite their fingertips in rage.”* (3:119). And it is made the ground of a covenant: *“And be mindful of Allah. Surely Allah knows best what is ˹hidden˺ in the heart.”* (5:7).

The repetition of the theme across the Book gives this verse its weight. The knowledge being asserted is not an inference from behaviour; it is direct acquaintance with what a person is, which means that self-presentation, however well managed, addresses an audience that does not include the one who matters.

**What the Verse Does in Its Context**

Read between the instruction about allegiance and the warning about the Day that follows, this sentence performs a specific service. It removes the most attractive option available to a frightened minority: keeping its standing in two camps at once.

**The Mustard Seed Hidden in a Rock**

Elsewhere the Book supplies an image for the reach of this knowledge that makes concealment look almost absurd. Luqmān tells his son that even a deed the weight of a mustard seed, hidden in a rock or in the heavens or the earth, will be brought out by God, who is Most Subtle and All-Aware (31:16).

A mustard seed inside a rock is not only well hidden; it is a figure for something that has no chance of being found by any search. The Book uses it for the smallest human act, which tells the reader that the question raised in this verse is not confined to large betrayals. The knowledge in question notices the small things too, and the Allah who knows what hearts conceal is defined by that subtlety rather than by surveillance.

**"Most Capable of Everything" — the Attribute That Keeps Recurring**

The verse closes with the attribute *qadīr*, which the sūrah has now used at 3:26 and 3:29 and will use again at 3:189. Its presence at the end of a statement about knowledge is important: knowing and being able to act on what is known are different things.

A being who knew everything but could do nothing would be a witness rather than a judge; a being who could do everything but knew nothing would be a force. The Book closes its knowledge statements with capacity because the two belong together in any adequate account of accountability, and because the humans reciting the verse need to be told that what is known about them will be dealt with by someone who can act.

---

## Verse 3:30

> ˹Watch for˺ the Day when every soul will be presented with whatever good it has done. And it will wish that its misdeeds were far off. And Allah warns you about Himself. And Allah is Ever Gracious to ˹His˺ servants

**Every Soul Presented With Its Good**

The verse says that each soul is presented with whatever good it has done, and the Book's other accounts of this moment describe the presentation in detail. At the resurrection of the earth's smallest deeds, every atom's weight of good and of evil is seen (99:7–8). At the opening of the record, the wicked are handed a document that omits nothing, and they cry out at its completeness (18:49). The scales are set up on that day, and no soul is wronged in the least (21:47).

Together these passages give the scene its character. It is not a trial in which a defendant hears charges he must answer; it is an inventory in which the whole of a life arrives to be seen. The verse's own emphasis falls on the good, which is presented first: whatever good a person has done comes forward. The mercy in that ordering deserves attention, since it means the deeds a person may have forgotten are not lost, and are the first thing named.

**The Wish for Distance**

The second sentence reports the reaction rather than the content: the soul will wish that its misdeeds were far off. The Arabic verb describes a hope for something to be remote, postponed, or placed outside one's reach.

Other passages show the same wish in more vivid terms. The wrongdoers would offer everything the earth contains twice over to ransom themselves from the punishment they see. What they see will be what they never expected (39:47); and the person who dragged others toward error will tell his companion on that day that he wishes they had been as distant from each other as the east is from the west (43:38). The Book speaks of this regret without sentimentality, as a fact about how people will perceive their own records when they can no longer revise them. It is a description of an emotion that arrives too late to be useful, and the Book's regular advice is to have it now.

**"Ever Gracious to His Servants" — the Sentence That Follows the Warning**

The verse ends in tenderness. After the warning comes a description of God as *raʾūf* — deeply kind, compassionate — toward His servants. The Book uses the same pair of qualities of the Prophet himself: he is described as ever caring and merciful to the believers (9:128).

The placement of the attribute carries the teaching about the passage. A warning issued to servants whom the warner knows to be weak is a form of care; a warning that gave no opportunity for amendment would be a sentence. The Book's logic is that the kindness and the warning come from the same source and serve the same end, and it says so by putting them in the same verse, one clause apart.

**A Person's Own Testimony**

The verse leaves no need for prosecution because the evidence is carried by the accused. The Book says as much at 75:13, where each person is informed of what he sent ahead and left behind, and at 81:14, where every soul on that day knows precisely what it has brought.

---

## Verse 3:31

> Say, ˹O Prophet,˺ “If you ˹sincerely˺ love Allah, then follow me; Allah will love you and forgive your sins. For Allah is All-Forgiving, Most Merciful.”

**A Claim of Love Put to the Test**

The verse is addressed to people who were saying something about themselves. Commentators connect it to delegations and communities that had declared their love of God — some from among the Christians of Najrān, some from among the Arabs of Makkah who said they worshipped their idols out of love for the Most High — and to anyone whose religion consisted of affection without allegiance.

That is the whole weight of the verse's test. The Prophet is the measure in a way that can be examined, because his conduct was public and transmitted in detail.

**Ḥubb: the Word, and the Direction of Love in the Qur'an**

That distribution shapes the doctrine. The verse above is not an invitation to work up an emotion. It is a statement that the initiative lies with God, whose love is the goal and whose love for His servants is described elsewhere as resting on their conduct: *“He will replace them with others who love Him and are loved by Him.”* (5:54). A person who follows the messenger is entering a relationship that God has already described the terms of, and the terms are favourable.

**Who Was Making the Claim, and the Report About Hidden Idolatry**

Al-Thaʿlabī connects the command with the delegation from Najrān, whose members are reported to have said that they worshipped the Messiah out of love for God and that they loved their Lord. The same claim had been made by the idolaters of Makkah, whose words the Book reports in the sūrah of the Groups: *“We worship them only so they may bring us closer to Allah.”* (39:3). In both cases an attachment to God is offered as the reason for a practice that the revelation rejects.

---

## Verse 3:32

> Say, ˹O Prophet,˺ “Obey Allah and His Messenger.” If they still turn away, then truly Allah does not like the disbelievers

**A Two-Part Command in One Sentence**

The verse gives an instruction in the imperative and pairs two objects of obedience in a single phrase. The pairing is the point of the sentence. Obedience to God is not being divided or supplemented here; it is being located, so that a person who claims to obey God is told where that obedience is to be sought in practice.

Earlier in the sūrah, a test of love was set by the phrase *follow me*. This verse moves from following to obeying, and the two are not the same relation. Following describes a direction and a model; obedience describes a requirement with authority behind it. The Book uses both because a claim of devotion needs both: it must be a road, and it must be binding.

**Why the Messenger Is Named Beside God**

The command does not say *obey Allah* and stop. It names the Messenger as the second object, and the Book does this consistently — at 4:59, at 4:80, and in a series of verses that place obedience to the Prophet second only to obedience to God.

**"If They Still Turn Away" — the Refusal That Repeats**

The clause assumes a specific reaction: not an argument, not a counter-command, but a turn away. The same verb was used of people invited to the Book of God to settle their disputes, and the Book uses it again here for those who hear the instruction to obey.

---

## Verse 3:33

> Indeed, Allah chose Adam, Noah, the family of Abraham, and the family of ’Imrân above all people ˹of their time˺

The sentence rests on a single verb, *iṣṭafā*, to choose or select something as pure, to take one thing out of a group. All four entries in the list fall under that act. The Book uses the same verb for the election of individuals, families, and communities throughout: Moses is chosen above the people of his time, Mary is chosen above the women of the world, Israel is preferred above the nations of its era.

**The Word Behind the Choosing**

The verb for choosing, iṣṭafā, is used in the Book of several figures besides the four named here: Mary is chosen above the women of the world (3:42), and the same language appears in the lists of the prophets given in the sūrahs of the Cattle and of Ṣād (6:84–87; 38:47). The Prophet himself is called al-Muṣṭafā, the chosen one, in the community's usage, which shows how closely the word is tied to the idea of a selection made by God and not by people. The term for family in this verse means a familial line rather than a household, and the phrase rendered above all people is the one used elsewhere of Israel (2:47) and of other figures (6:86; 7:140), where it is set alongside the same limitation to a person's own time.

**The Father of Mary in the Sources**

The ʿImrān of this verse is the father of Mary, named again in the vow his wife makes a few verses later and once more in the sūrah of the Prohibition (3:35; 66:12). He is not named in the Bible, and the figure known in extra-biblical Christian literature as Joachim, whose name means he whom God set up, is generally taken to be the same person. Commentators from an early period identified ʿImrān with Amram the father of Moses, and some texts treat the two as the same man, though they also distinguish between them and note the centuries that separate them. The identification was accepted by writers as early as Muqātil ibn Sulaymān, who died in 150 after the Hijrah, and the Quran and the reports attributed to the Prophet do not name the father of Moses, so the equation rests on the similarity of the two names rather than on a text.

**Two Readings of the Chain**

The statement in the following verse that these houses are descendants of one another has been taken in two ways. Al-Māturīdī read it as lineage: the house of ʿImrān descends from the house of Abraham, which descends from Noah, who descends from Adam, so the list is a single family tree. Al-Ṭabarī read it as a kinship of service, in which the houses and figures are joined by their worship of God and their obedience to Him rather than by a recorded pedigree. The two readings meet in the effect the phrase has on the reader, since the same construction appears in a legal context in the sūrah of Women, where it is rendered as one of you is as the other (4:25). Whether the link is blood or obedience, the sentence refuses to let any of the named houses claim a standing that the others do not share.

---

## Verse 3:34

> They are descendants of one another. And Allah is All-Hearing, All-Knowing

**The Two Readings the Commentators Recorded**

Commentators discussed what kind of descent is meant here, and two answers are recorded. One takes the phrase to describe blood: the house of ʿImrān traces back through Israel to Jacob, Isaac, and Abraham, and through them to Noah and Adam. The other takes it to describe spiritual kinship, arguing that these houses are linked by their worship and obedience rather than by a documented pedigree.

Both readings have support. The physical reading fits the list's construction of named genealogies and the Book's habit of naming lineages for the prophets. The spiritual reading fits the sūrah's insistence elsewhere that standing before God is not settled by ancestry, and it is supported by a parallel use of the same phrasing at 4:25, where *one of you is as the other* applies to people who are not related at all. Reading them together gives the fuller sense: descent here is descent within a mission, which a family carries both by blood and by belief.

---

## Verse 3:35

> ˹Remember˺ when the wife of ’Imrân said, “My Lord! I dedicate what is in my womb entirely to Your service, so accept it from me. You ˹alone˺ are truly the All-Hearing, All-Knowing.”

The words *what is in my womb* locate the vow before the birth, at the earliest point a human being can be spoken for. The Arabic term rendered "dedicated entirely to Your service" is *muḥarrar*, a word from the vocabulary of setting a person free — a slave released from every claim to serve a single purpose. Applied to an unborn child, it means that the mother is surrendering her own claim.

**The Father Who Did Not Live to See It**

The reports attached to this vow supply a detail the verse itself leaves out. Mary's father ʿImrān, it is said, died before she was born, so the child his wife promised to the service of the sanctuary was one he never saw. The words are therefore spoken by a woman who had already lost her husband and who was dedicating the only child she expected to bear.

The commentators describe what the dedication involved. The child would be given to the service of the Temple, would study and learn from the Book, and would be released from the responsibilities that occupied other people, so that its time belonged to worship (al-Rāzī; al-Ṭabarī). The promise is a description of a whole life rather than of a single act, which is why the sentence that follows it asks for acceptance rather than for a particular outcome.

---

## Verse 3:36

> When she delivered, she said, “My Lord! I have given birth to a girl,”—and Allah fully knew what she had delivered—“and the male is not like the female. I have named her Mary, and I seek Your protection for her and her offspring from Satan, the accursed.”

**The Words of a Mother Meeting Her Child**

The verse reports the delivery and then the first thing the mother said. Her sentence is not a statement of doctrine or a formula of thanks; it is the observation of a woman holding a child and telling God what she is holding. The Book preserves it in the plainest possible form, without softening the disappointment that runs beneath it.

**"I Have Named Her Mary"**

Naming a child is an act of claiming, and the mother's sentence does it in the middle of her prayer. The name given, Maryam, is the one the Book uses for her throughout, and it is the name by which the sūrah's readers know the household's story.

Naming also fixes a fact about the future. Everything the child will become is spoken for in advance by a woman who does not yet know it, and the Book's practice of naming key figures early — John, Jesus, and here Mary — connects her to a pattern in which the name arrives before the person's role does. The mother had promised a servant to the temple; she now claims a daughter, by name, as the one she is placing there.

**"I Seek Your Protection for Her and Her Offspring From Satan"**

With the child named, the mother asks for protection, and she extends the request beyond her daughter to her descendants. The Arabic verb is the ordinary one for seeking refuge, and the Book commands the practice elsewhere: when you recite the Qur'an, seek refuge in God from the accursed Satan (16:98), and when a provocation from Satan comes, seek refuge in God (7:200; 41:36).

**A Prayer the Prophet Confirmed by Reciting It**

The request in this verse is the earliest recorded act of protection given to Mary, and the Prophet's own tradition answers it. Abū Hurayrah reports that the Prophet said: *There is none born among the offspring of Adam but Satan touches it. A child cries loudly at the time of birth because of the touch of Satan, except Mary and her child* — and then Abū Hurayrah recited this very verse with its prayer for protection (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī 3431).

---

## Verse 3:37

> So her Lord accepted her graciously and blessed her with a pleasant upbringing—entrusting her to the care of Zachariah. Whenever Zachariah visited her in the sanctuary, he found her supplied with provisions. He exclaimed, “O Mary! Where did this come from?” She replied, “It is from Allah. Surely Allah provides for whoever He wills without limit.”

**"A Pleasant Upbringing" — the Image Behind the Words**

The phrase rendered "blessed her with a pleasant upbringing" uses the vocabulary of growth. The Arabic noun for upbringing is related to the sprouting of plants. The Book's other uses of the image connect a good life to a good tree: a good word is like a good tree, its roots firm and its branches in the sky, yielding fruit in every season. A bad word is like a tree torn out of the ground with nothing to hold it (14:24–26).

**"Where Did This Come From?" — the Question and the Answer**

Her reply — that it is from Allah, and that Allah provides for whoever He wills without limit — is the same phrase that closed the great prayer of verses 26 and 27, and the sūrah is careful to reuse it. The Book takes a formula that was spoken in a petition about all provision and places it in the mouth of a young woman answering a question in a small room. What a supplicant asked for in general terms is showed in the narrative in the most concrete way the text can manage.

---

The detail that Zakariyyā "found with her provision" is the verse's quiet evidence, and the commentators read the two words closely: he entered the sanctuary, found food she had not brought and could not have obtained, and asked where it came from. Her answer is in the next verse's account and the prayer that follows it: it is from Allah, who provides for whom He wills without measure (3:38). The Qur'an had already stated that principle earlier in the same sūrah, in the verse that names God as the provider "without measure" (3:27), so Maryam's table is not a marvel set apart from the rest of the sūrah's teaching — it is the principle made visible in one room. The word for her chamber, *miḥrāb*, recurs in the Qur'an for the private place of worship (19:11), and the two uses together give the term its sense: a space set aside for prayer where the world's ordinary supply is not what sustains the person in it.

## Verse 3:38

> Then and there Zachariah prayed to his Lord, saying, “My Lord! Grant me—by your grace—righteous offspring. You are certainly the Hearer of ˹all˺ prayers.”

**"Then and There" — What Prompted the Prayer**

The verse's opening words tie the supplication to what the previous scene contained. Zachariah has just seen a girl supplied without a supplier, and his response is not a remark about provision in general. He asks for a child of his own, which means the scene in Mary's room acted on him as evidence that the ordinary rules were not the only rules in operation.

The sequence is worth naming because it is the sūrah's method. A person sees an act of God, and the act produces a request.

**"By Your Grace" — the Word From His Presence**

The petition carries the phrase *min ladunka*, from Your presence, the same construction used by the youths of the cave asking for mercy from their Lord and by Moses finding a servant taught knowledge from God's own presence (18:10; 18:65). The word describes something given from inside the giver rather than passed along a chain.

**The Setting of the Prayer**

The words rendered then and there tie the request to what the previous verses have just described, and al-Ṭabarī explained the phrase as meaning that Zachariah prayed at the moment he saw the provision granted to Mary in the sanctuary. The fuller account of the same story appears in the sūrah of Mary, where Zachariah explains that he fears what his surviving relatives would do after his death and speaks of his own age and his wife's barrenness (19:2–15). The prayer in this verse is therefore given in the Book twice, once in detail and once in summary, and the summary keeps the attention on the request and its answer rather than on the circumstances.

**Confirming a Word From God**

The description of the child in the next verse as confirming a word from God was explained by al-Rāzī, al-Ṭabarī, and others as a reference to John's later confirmation of Jesus, who is called a word from God a few verses further on (3:45). To confirm, in the Book's usage, is to affirm the truth of something, and the same verb describes the Qur'an as affirming the scriptures that preceded it (2:41; 3:4; 5:48). The promise given to Zachariah therefore describes a life that would stand as a witness, and the witness is specified: the child would recognize the messenger who came after him. The detail belongs to the sūrah's larger argument, since the passage is building toward the account of Jesus and the disputes about him.

**The Titles Given to the Child**

The promise lists several qualities, and the commentators spent attention on each. The word rendered noble carries the sense of a leader or a master, and Ibn Kathīr and al-Ṭabarī recorded explanations of it as learned, devout, wise, generous, God-revering, and temperate. The word rendered chaste was read by some as complete abstention from relations with women, while Ibn Kathīr and others held that it means abstention from what is unlawful rather than from marriage, which fits the Book's praise of marriage among the prophets. A prophet from among the righteous closes the list, and the phrase places John in the company of those whose mission the sūrah has been describing. The titles are given before the child is born, which is what makes the answer to the prayer a promise rather than a report.

---

## Verse 3:39

> So the angels called out to him while he stood praying in the sanctuary, “Allah gives you good news of ˹the birth of˺ John who will confirm the Word of Allah and will be a great leader, chaste, and a prophet among the righteous.”

**The Name Chosen Before the Birth**

The announcement does not merely promise a son; it gives him a name. In the fuller account the name is described as one never given to anyone before: *“O Zachariah! Indeed, We give you the good news of ˹the birth of˺ a son, whose name will be John—a name We have not given to anyone before.”* (19:7).

**"Confirming a Word From Allah"**

Among the qualities announced is that the child will confirm a word from God. The commentators understood this as a reference to Jesus, who is described in this sūrah as a word from God given to Mary, and they took the announcement to mean that John would be the one to affirm his truth — which is how the Gospel tradition also presents him.

The verb *ṣaddaqa*, to confirm or declare true, is the same word the Qur'an uses for its own relation to the earlier scriptures: the Book confirms what came before it (2:41), and the Book given to the Prophet is a confirmation of previous scriptures (5:48). John's function as the verse describes it is therefore the function the sūrah assigned to the Qur'an itself at its opening. Two revelations, generations apart, settled on the same act: to affirm what was genuine before them.

**Sayyid: What "Great Leader" Carries**

The word translated "great leader" is *sayyid*, and its range is wider than the translation suggests. The lexicographers gathered meanings under it: a man of learning and devotion, generous, self-controlled, temperate, dignified, able to master his own desires. Al-Ṭabarī and Ibn Kathīr record these explanations, and most of them describe states of character rather than rank.

This is significant because the child being promised is given a title of authority, and the Book fills the title with character. The fuller portrait of John in another sūrah shows what was meant: wisdom granted while he was still a child, purity and compassion from God, and kindness to his parents, with neither arrogance nor disobedience in him (19:12–14). A leader in this vocabulary is not a man who commands others but a man who commands himself.

**The Answer's Place in a Life of Service**

The Book's account of John's family in another sūrah ends with the reason they were answered: *“Indeed, they used to race in doing good, and call upon Us with hope and fear, totally humbling themselves before Us.”* (21:90).

---

## Verse 3:40

> Zachariah exclaimed, “My Lord! How can I have a son when I am very old and my wife is barren?” He replied, “So will it be. Allah does what He wills.”

**The Same Astonishment in Other Households**

The sūrah of Hūd narrates a nearly identical scene. The messengers bring Sarah news of a child, and she laughs and exclaims at the impossibility, saying that she is an old woman and her husband an old man, and describing the news as astonishing (11:72). The reply carries the same correction as the answer given to Zachariah, and it is followed by a blessing on the household (11:73). The sūrah of the Scatterers reports the same woman coming forward with a cry, holding her face, and asking how a barren old woman could bear a child, with the answer that her Lord has decreed it and is All-Wise, All-Knowing (51:29–30).

**An Astonished Question, Not a Denial**

Zachariah's response to the announcement is a question, and the Book reports it without any note of censure. He states two facts — his own advanced age, his wife's barrenness — and asks how the thing announced can happen. This is the same response recorded in fuller form in the sūrah of Maryam, where he adds an appeal to God's earlier creation of him from nothing (19:8–9).

The distinction between a question and a rejection matters in this Book, and it is drawn by the text itself. When Abraham asks to be shown how God gives life to the dead, he is answered without rebuke and even told what to do to have his heart reassured (2:260). When Gabriel announces a birth to Sarah, the astonishment is met with the gentle reproof that the decree of God should not surprise anyone: *“Are you astonished by Allah’s decree?”* (11:73). Zachariah's question belongs to that family of exchanges: a believer asking how, not whether.

**"So Will It Be" — the Answer Given**

The Book's formula for creation appears at exactly this point in several places: God says to a thing *Be*, and it is (2:117; 36:82; 40:68). The same formula is used in this sūrah when Mary asks how she can have a child with no man having touched her, and the answer there adds the extra clause that when God decrees a matter He simply tells it to be (3:47). Zachariah's reply and Mary's reply are constructed from the same material, which is why the two announcements belong together in the sūrah's structure.

**"Allah Does What He Wills" — the Doctrine in One Clause**

The clause at the end of the answer deserves its own attention because it is the ground of everything the sūrah says about prayer. God is not constrained by the order He has established; the order is His instrument, and He acts within it or beyond it as He wills. A statement of the same kind closes the account of creation's submission in another sūrah: whatever is in the heavens and the earth bows to Him, and whoever He disgraces none can honour, for He does what He wills (22:18).

**What the Sign Consisted Of**

The request for a sign and the answer given to it are reported more fully in the sūrah of Mary, where Zachariah is told that he will not speak to people for three nights while remaining sound in body (19:2–15). The sign described in the passage before the reader is the same: speech is withheld while the man is otherwise well, so that the silence itself becomes the evidence. The commentators explain that he communicated by gesture or by writing during those days, and that the inability to speak was not an illness but a sign attached to the promise. The account leaves the reader with a man who asked for assurance and who received it in the form of a condition he had to live through rather than a statement he could simply hear.

---

## Verse 3:41

> Zachariah said, “My Lord! Grant me a sign.” He said, “Your sign is that you will not ˹be able to˺ speak to people for three days except through gestures. Remember your Lord often and glorify ˹Him˺ morning and evening.”

**A Sign Asked For After the News, Not Instead of Believing**

Zachariah has just been told that his prayer is answered. His next request is for a sign, and the Book reports it as a request rather than as a doubt. The distinction is preserved in the fuller account, where he asks for a sign and is given one without any suggestion that he has failed a test (19:10).

This is the same request Abraham makes when he asks to be shown how the dead are raised and explains that he asks so that his heart may be reassured (2:260). The Book's treatment of such requests is consistent: a believer may ask for confirmation of what he has been told, and the asking is not treated as unbelief. What distinguishes it from a demand is the posture of the asker and the purpose of the request, which in both accounts is the strengthening of a heart, not the testing of a claim.

**The Sign Itself: Speech Withheld, Health Intact**

The answer describes a personal condition rather than a public miracle. He will not be able to speak to people for three days, and the fuller account specifies that this will happen while he is healthy, using words that rule out illness as the explanation (19:10). The sign, in other words, is a deliberate interruption of a faculty rather than a symptom.

**"Remember Your Lord Often" — the Command Attached to the Sign**

The instruction that follows is the heart of the verse. During the silence he is told to remember his Lord often, and then to glorify Him morning and evening. The sign does not leave a vacuum; it redirects the faculty it has taken.

The Book treats remembrance as the occupation of a heart that is turning toward God. It places the command in a wide range of situations: the believers are told to remember Allah often and to glorify Him morning and evening (33:41–42), a command attached in that sūrah to the honour due to the Prophet (48:9). The same pairing of remembrance with praise is given to the Prophet himself as a remedy for the stress of opposition (20:130). In the sign given to Zachariah the instruction is so not a punishment attached to silence; it is the assignment of a new activity to the hours that speech used to occupy.

**The Fixed Hours of Praise**

The phrase *morning and evening* recurs in the Book as a pair, and its regularity is the point. The Prophet is commanded to praise before sunrise and before sunset, and at the hours of the night, and at the ends of the day (20:130; 50:39–40). The same two ends of the day are named in the instruction to praise and seek forgiveness (40:55), and in the command to remember the name of the Lord morning and evening (76:25).

A day with a fixed practice at each end is a day whose middle is protected. The Book gives this pattern to the Prophet, to the believers, and here to a priest serving a sign, which suggests that it is meant to be the ordinary discipline of a religious life rather than an exceptional measure. Two anchors, one at each end of the light.

The Book's fuller narrative shows him coming out to his people and signalling to them to glorify God morning and evening (19:11).

---

## Verse 3:42

> And ˹remember˺ when the angels said, “O Mary! Surely Allah has selected you, purified you, and chosen you over all women of the world

**Angels Speaking to a Woman in Her Own Right**

The verse reports a direct address: the angels come to Mary and speak to her about herself. Nothing precedes their words about a husband, a father, or a priest; the addressee is the woman, and the announcement concerns her own standing.

That directness is unusual in the literature of the ancient world and it is a pattern in this sūrah. The same narrative has opened with a mother's vow, continued through a child's acceptance and miraculous provision, and now arrives at an address from the angels to the woman herself. The Book is building a story in which a woman is not a step in a man's genealogy but the subject of the account.

**What "Purified" Means in This Sūrah**

Commentators explained the purification as purity of intention and worship, and also as protection from the defilement that contact with evil people brings. The Book's language of purification is used elsewhere for the household of the Prophet, where God states His intention to remove impurity and to purify its members completely (33:33).

**"Over All Women of the World"**

The second selection is stated in the widest terms available. The same phrase, used of the chosen families in verse 33, was qualified there by the words *of their time*. The qualification helps here as well: Mary is selected over the women of her era, and commentaries that placed Khadījah, Fāṭimah, and Āsiyah at the summit of believing women were not contradicting this verse.

The Prophet's own statements on the subject are recorded in several collections. Anas reports him saying that four women are enough for a person among the women of the world: Mary the daughter of ʿImrān, Khadījah, Fāṭimah, and Āsiyah the wife of Pharaoh (Sunan al-Tirmidhī 3878). Abū Mūsā reports him saying that many men reached perfection but that among women only Āsiyah and Mary attained it (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī 3411). The reports do not cancel this verse; they show how the community understood it — as the elevation of a particular woman in a particular history, not as a ranking designed to diminish the other women named with her.

---

## Verse 3:43

> O Mary! Be devout to your Lord, prostrate yourself ˹in prayer˺ and bow along with those who bow down.”

**The Command That Follows the Honour**

Having told her that she has been chosen, the verse immediately tells her what to do. The order is deliberate and it recurs throughout the Book: a station conferred by God is accompanied by an obligation, and the greater the selection the more definite the instruction.

There is no interval in which Mary is invited to enjoy her position. The verbs arrive one after another — be devout, prostrate, bow — and they are all imperatives addressed to the same person who was just told that she stands above the women of her era.

**Qānitah: Devotion as a Posture**

The command "be devout to your Lord" uses a word whose feminine form is *qānitah*, from the root that describes standing, constancy, silence, and settled conformity. The Book uses the same word of devout women and men in a verse that lists their qualities together (33:35), and of the obedient wife whose devotion is part of her household's good order (4:34).

**Prostration and Bowing Named in Order**

The verse then names two specific postures, prostration first and bowing second. The order is interesting because the prostration is the lower of the two, and elsewhere the Book lists bowing before prostration when it describes the physical vocabulary of worship: bow down, prostrate yourselves, worship your Lord (22:77).

**"Along With Those Who Bow Down"**

The command is to join a body of worshippers. The Book uses a similar formulation when it commands the Israelites to bow with those who bow down (2:43), and the repetition of the formula for a woman in a sanctuary shows how the Book conceives of devotion: an individual practice embedded in a community's practice. A person's worship is not measured by its originality, and the reference point for it is the community that has been worshipping the same way.

**The Congregation the Command Assumes**

The instruction to bow with those who bow carries a setting inside it. Mary is told to join a body of worshippers rather than to perform an act on her own, and the Book uses the same phrase when it commands the community to bow down with those who bow down (2:43). The sanctuary in which she had been placed was served by a company of men, and the command places her among them in the act of worship.

The Book describes the House that Abraham and Ishmael were told to purify as a place for those who circle it, meditate in it, and bow and prostrate themselves in prayer (2:125). The two postures named in this verse therefore belong to a form of worship older than the community that reads the passage, and the command given to Mary is phrased in its vocabulary. Nothing in the sentence restricts the instruction by gender or by age. What it asks for is participation in the worship of the house where she had been dedicated, and the sūrah gives the command as the direct answer to the announcement of her election.

---

## Verse 3:44

> This is news of the unseen that We reveal to you ˹O Prophet˺. You were not with them when they cast lots to decide who would be Mary’s guardian, nor were you there when they argued ˹about it˺

**Ghayb: What "the Unseen" Covers**

The verse describes what it has just narrated as news of the unseen, and the Arabic word behind the phrase is *ghayb*, which covers everything absent from human perception: what is hidden in space, what is past, what is still to come, and what belongs to another order of being altogether.

The Book uses the term for revelation's content throughout. At Sinai, the Prophet is told that he was not present on the western side of the mountain when the commandments were entrusted to Moses, nor was he there in his time (28:44). The story of Joseph is introduced as one of the narratives of the unseen which God reveals to him, adding that neither he nor his people knew it beforehand (12:102; 11:49). The same construction frames the narrative of these verses about Mary. What the Prophet recites is knowledge he has no means of possessing, and the Book presents that fact as part of its claim.

**"You Were Not With Them" — an Argument From Absence**

The proof is stated as a fact about the Prophet’s biography, and its plainness is the argument. He was not among the priests of the sanctuary when they made their decision, and he was not present at their dispute. He was born centuries later, in a region where these events were not recorded and where the surviving traditions of the Jewish and Christian communities were not available to him in their original languages.

**The Casting of Lots in a Sanctuary**

The verse refers to a procedure: the men concerned cast lots to decide which of them would take charge of the girl. The Book mentions a similar practice in the story of Jonah, who drew straws with the passengers of a ship and lost, and was thrown overboard (37:141).

**Who Was Disputed, and on What Grounds**

Commentators recorded two views about the dispute. The majority held that the priests of the sanctuary wanted to be Mary's guardian and cast lots to determine who would have the privilege, and that God's will gave her to Zachariah. Al-Ṭabarī preserved a second view in which Zachariah's position was not contested at all, since he was related to Mary's mother, and the dispute concerned other aspects of her care or of who would serve her in the sanctuary.

---

## Verse 3:45

> ˹Remember˺ when the angels proclaimed, “O Mary! Allah gives you good news of a Word from Him, his name will be the Messiah, Jesus, son of Mary; honoured in this world and the Hereafter, and he will be one of those nearest ˹to Allah˺

**"A Word From Him" — the Phrase and What It Carries**

The child is called a word from God, and the phrase has occupied commentators and theologians since the earliest generations. The Book uses the same language elsewhere when it addresses the People of the Book: the Messiah, Jesus son of Mary, is described as the fulfilment of God's Word through Mary and a spirit from Him, in a verse that immediately forbids the extremes of doctrine (4:171).

**"Honoured in This World and the Hereafter"**

The child is promised honour in both worlds, and the promise covers the whole span of his story. In this world, the Book says, God delivered him from those who disbelieved and elevated his followers above the deniers until the Day of Judgment (3:55), and the record of his miracles is preserved in the scripture he brought.

In the Hereafter the honour is stated in the same Book that insists on his servanthood: he is a servant whom God favoured and made an example for the Children of Israel (43:59). The two descriptions belong on one page. The Book's presentation of Jesus is consistently double: extraordinary in his birth and station, a servant in his relation to God, and it never lets either half of that description displace the other.

**"One of Those Nearest to Allah"**

The last phrase names a company: those brought near. The Book calls the foremost believers the ones nearest to God (56:11), and the same category is used of the blessed in the Hereafter, whose record is witnessed by the nearest to God and whose drink flows from a spring reserved for them.

Being placed in that company is the highest honour the sūrah can name, and it is worth reading carefully because it is a ranking within creation rather than a statement about divinity. The company has members. A person does not join a company by being its origin; he joins it by being brought near to someone else, and the phrase itself says so. What the announcement promises the child is proximity to God, which is the promise made to the righteous generally, in the sharpest form available.

**Servant and Sign: How the Sūrah Holds Both Together**

The chapter will repeatedly insist on a description that the announcement's language might appear to soften. Jesus speaks in the cradle and identifies himself, in the words the sūrah records elsewhere, as a servant of God who has been given scripture and prophethood (19:30).

---

## Verse 3:46

> And he will speak to people in ˹his˺ infancy and adulthood and will be one of the righteous.”

**A Child Who Speaks, and Speaks as His Own Witness**

The verse closes the angelic announcement with a promise about the child's speech: he will speak to people in infancy and in adulthood. The infancy clause is the one that carries the sign, and the Book attaches it to a specific situation which the sūrah of Maryam narrates, where the child in the cradle answers the accusations being made against his mother.

The scene in that sūrah gives the miracle its purpose. A woman returns to her people with an infant and is met with the worst accusation a community could make of her. The Book records her response as a gesture, and then has the child speak for himself: he identifies himself as a servant of God, given scripture and prophethood, made a blessing wherever he goes, and commanded to prayer and alms for as long as he lives (19:30–31). The first recorded utterance of Jesus in the Qur'an is an argument in his mother's defence.

**"In Infancy and Adulthood" — the Two Stages as One Promise**

The promise names two stages and so covers a life rather than an episode. Infant speech would be a wonder on its own. A lifetime of speech after it makes the wonder a pattern, and the sūrah of the Table describes exactly that, when God reminds Jesus of His favour in supporting him so that he spoke to people in his infancy and in his maturity (5:110).

**"And One of the Righteous" — the Company He Belongs To**

The verse ends by placing him among the righteous, the same phrase used of John in verse 39 and of the prophets listed elsewhere in the Book. In the sūrah of the Cattle, Zachariah, John, Jesus, and Elias are named together as men who were all of the righteous (6:85).

**The Tradition of Those Who Spoke in the Cradle**

The Prophet's own report limits the wonder to a very small number. Abū Hurayrah heard him say: *None spoke in cradle but three: Jesus, Juraij. A child* — and the report goes on to tell the story of the second, a hermit wrongly accused by a woman and vindicated by the speech of her infant. Of the third, a baby who answered his mother's wishes with a prayer that he not be like a tyrant and, in another case, that he be like a woman falsely accused (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī 3436).

---

## Verse 3:47

> Mary wondered, “My Lord! How can I have a child when no man has ever touched me?” An angel replied, “So will it be. Allah creates what He wills. When He decrees a matter, He simply tells it, ‘Be!’ And it is

**The Mother's Question**

The announcement has been given, and Mary answers it with a question about how it can happen. The fuller account in the sūrah of Maryam records the objection in her own words, including her insistence that no man has touched her and that she is not unchaste (19:20).

**"Allah Creates What He Wills"**

The clause that follows the agreement is a statement about creation, not about reproduction. The Book does not say that God will use an unusual means; it locates the event in the category of things God creates because He wills them.

The parallel account in the sūrah of Maryam carries the same reply with an addition: the matter is already decreed (19:21). Elsewhere the Book states the principle without a birth attached to it, so that the reader meets it as a doctrine rather than as an exception. What is being taught is a view of creation in which the divine will is an enough cause, and the annunciation becomes one illustration of a rule that governs everything that exists.

**The Command "Be" and What It Means for a Creature**

The verse then supplies the mechanism, and the mechanism is a word. When God decrees a matter, He tells it to be, and it is. The Book uses the formula for creation generally: the Originator of the heavens and the earth says to a thing *Be*. It is (2:117); the same words describe His action in the sūrah of the Bees, of the Bee's Lord who says to what He wills to exist, *Be*, and it is (16:40).

The sūrah of Maryam uses the formula in a passage that forbids the very conclusion the annunciation might invite: it is not for God to take a son, and when He decrees a matter He tells it to be (19:35). So the formula is a statement about power, and at the same time a statement about the distance between the one who commands and the thing that comes to be at the command. What is created does not become the creator by having been created directly.

**Placed Beside Adam**

The sūrah states the comparison outright a few verses later, where the example of Jesus before God is given as the example of Adam: created from dust, told to be, and he was (3:59). The pairing settles the question of what a miracle of origin proves.

Adam had no parents at all; Jesus had a mother and no father; the rest of humanity has both. If the absence of a father were a claim to divinity, the absence of both parents would be a stronger one, and the Book's handling of Adam makes clear that being created directly is a statement about God's power rather than about the creature's status. The same formula covers both cases, and both men are servants.

---

## Verse 3:48

> And Allah will teach him writing and wisdom, the Torah and the Gospel

**A Curriculum Announced Before the Birth**

The verse continues the angelic announcement and promises that the child will be taught. The verb is in the future, and it is attached to a person who has not yet been born: writing, wisdom, the Torah, and the Gospel are his before anything has happened to him.

The Book's habit of describing prophetic preparation as divine instruction is consistent across its narratives. The Prophet of this sūrah is reminded that God has revealed to him the Book and wisdom and taught him what he never knew (4:113), and the community is told that the messenger recites God's revelations, purifies them, and teaches them the Book and wisdom (2:151; 3:164). The wording used for Jesus is the same as the wording used for Muhammad, which places both men in one category of recipients rather than two categories of being.

**"Writing and Wisdom" — the First Pair and the Question Behind It**

The first pair in the list has been discussed at length because the word rendered "writing" can also mean a book, and the sūrah has already used the same word for the Scripture given to Moses. Some commentators resolved the difficulty by taking the term here in its sense of the ability to write, since the Scripture given to Moses was centuries old and could not be something Jesus was taught as a new text.

**"The Torah and the Gospel"**

The two named scriptures are the revelation given to Moses and the revelation given to Jesus. The sūrah has already described the second of them: the Gospel was given to Jesus as guidance and light, confirming what was revealed in the Torah and serving as a guide and a lesson to the God-fearing (5:46).

**Teaching as God's Act**

The verse's grammar keeps the initiative on God's side throughout. It does not say that Jesus learned; it says that God will teach him. The distinction is not pedantic, since the Book uses the same construction for the Prophet of this sūrah and for figures such as Luqmān, who was given wisdom by God without any account of how he acquired it (31:12).

**The Other Verses That Pair the Book With Wisdom**

The pairing named in this verse is one the Book uses when it describes what God gives to a messenger. The community is told that a messenger has been sent among them to recite the revelations, purify them, and teach them the Book and the wisdom (2:151), and the same gift is described as the favour shown to the believers with the addition that they had earlier been clearly astray (3:164). The descendants of Abraham are said to have received the Book and the wisdom along with great authority (4:54), which shows how old the pairing is.

The Gospel narrative uses it for Jesus, where God recounts the favour of teaching him writing, wisdom, the Torah, and the Gospel (5:110), and the sūrah of the Kneeling mentions the Scripture, the wisdom, and prophethood as what was given to the Children of Israel (45:16). The list in this verse therefore belongs to a pattern the reader meets repeatedly: wisdom is named alongside the text rather than derived from it, and the two are given together by the same act.

---

## Verse 3:49

> and ˹make him˺ a messenger to the Children of Israel ˹to proclaim,˺ ‘I have come to you with a sign from your Lord: I will make for you a bird from clay, breathe into it, and it will become a ˹real˺ bird—by Allah’s Will. I will heal the blind and the leper and raise the dead to life—by Allah’s Will. And I will prophesize what you eat and store in your houses. Surely in this is a sign for you if you ˹truly˺ believe

**A Messenger With a Named Audience**

The announcement now appoints the child to a mission and specifies the people it concerns: he is made a messenger to the Children of Israel. The Book states the same limit when it reports him speaking to that nation and identifying himself as God's messenger sent to them (61:6), a verse that goes on to say he announced a messenger coming after him.

The restriction is worth comparing with the mission given to the Prophet of this sūrah, which the Book describes as addressed to all of humanity (34:28), and with the declaration that he is God's messenger to all people without exception (7:158). The Qur'an does not blur the difference between the two commissions; it states each in its own terms and leaves both standing, and the reader is meant to weigh what each messenger was sent to do.

**"By Allah's Will" — the Clause Attached to the Miracles**

The phrase occurs twice in this verse, once after the bird and once after healing and resurrection, and it is repeated in the reminder at 5:110 with the divine voice saying "by My Will" at each healing and each raising. The pattern is too consistent to be incidental.

The clause does the central work of the sūrah's teaching about Jesus. A sign performed by permission belongs to the one who permits it, and the Book's insistence on the clause at the very moments a reader might conclude otherwise is a deliberate safeguard. It also matches the way the Book describes the Prophet's own miracles elsewhere: works beyond human power occur at the command of God, and the servant who performs them remains a servant.

**Healing the Blind and the Leper**

The Prophet's teaching addressed the fear such illnesses produced in his own society: he rejected the idea that disease transmits itself independently of God's will, and he instructed people in the same report to keep away from a leper as one would flee from a lion (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī 5707).

**Raising the Dead**

The third sign is the greatest in the sequence, and it is named in a single clause. The Book repeats it in the reminder of favours: how he brought the dead to life, by God's permission (5:110). The claim is accepted in the sūrah as a fact about his mission, and the same claim is made in the Gospel accounts, so that a reader of either tradition meets it as familiar ground.

**"A Sign for You If You Truly Believe"**

The verse closes with a condition, and the condition is the sūrah's recurring theme. The signs are called a sign for you, if you believe; the benefit of evidence depends on the disposition of the person receiving it.

The Book states that principle repeatedly. Signs and warners are of no benefit to those who refuse to believe (10:101), and the Prophet is told that if their rejection is unbearable he should consider that God could have guided them all without any of it, since signs are not what opens a heart (6:35). The list of miracles in this verse is therefore offered to a specific audience, and the Book is honest about what it can and cannot do. Evidence settles a case for a mind that has not already decided, and the miracles of a prophet are addressed to people who are willing to look at them.

---

## Verse 3:50

> And I will confirm the Torah revealed before me and legalize some of what had been forbidden to you. I have come to you with a sign from your Lord, so be mindful of Allah and obey me

**Confirming the Torah, Once More**

The speech continues and returns to the theme the sūrah opened with. The messenger confirms the Torah revealed before him, which is what the Book says of the Gospel itself: We gave him the Gospel containing guidance and light and confirming what was revealed in the Torah (5:46). The same relation is stated of the Qur'an at the start of this sūrah, which comes confirming what was before it.

**"So Be Mindful of Allah and Obey Me"**

The command couples the two obligations the sūrah has already joined in its instruction to the community: fear God and obey the messenger. The phrase itself is a formula of the prophets. The Book puts it in the mouth of messenger after messenger — Noah, Hūd, Ṣāliḥ, Lot, and Shuʿayb each say, in the sūrah that reports their missions, the same words: so fear Allah, and obey me (26:108; 26:126, and the parallel verses throughout that sūrah).

**What the Confirmation Means Here**

The sentence places Jesus in a chain that the sūrah has already described in its second and third verses. He confirms the Torah, and the same verb is used of the Book given to the Prophet when it is said to confirm what came before it (2:41, 91, 97; 3:3; 4:47; 35:31; 46:30). Elsewhere the Gospel is said to confirm the Torah as well (5:46), and the same language appears once more when Jesus is described as confirming what was sent before him (61:6). The wording is therefore not a claim about Jesus alone; it is the Book's standard way of describing the relation between its messengers, each one affirming what his predecessor brought rather than replacing it.

**Which Rules Were Relaxed**

The phrase about making lawful part of what had been forbidden was referred by al-Qurṭūbī and al-Ṭabarī to dietary rules rather than to the foundations of the law, since the prohibitions of murder, theft, and adultery were not the subject of any relaxation. The restrictions in question were either part of what the Torah itself laid down, including the rules the Book mentions in the sūrah of Women and the sūrah of the Cattle (4:160; 6:146), or they were practices the Israelites had imposed on themselves in later generations, which is how Ibn Kathīr and al-Qurṭūbī read the verse that mentions what Israel forbade itself before the Torah was sent down (3:93). On the second reading Jesus changed nothing in the original revelation, and the statement in the sūrah of the Ornaments that he came to make clear some of the matters on which they differed supports it (43:63). Al-Ṭabarī recorded the view that what Jesus brought was gentler than what Moses brought, an alleviation rather than a new law, and the verse's wording allows for that description of the change.

**The Formula the Prophets Share**

The command to be mindful of God and to obey the messenger appears on the lips of several prophets in the Book. Noah speaks it to his people, and so do Hūd, Ṣāliḥ, Lot, and Shuʿayb, each addressing his own community with the same pair of clauses (26:108, 126, 144, 163, 179). The declaration that God is my Lord and your Lord is likewise shared: Jesus says it in three other places in the Book (5:72, 117; 19:36; 43:64), Hūd says it to his people (11:56), and Moses says it to Pharaoh (40:27; 44:20). The verses of this passage therefore give Jesus the same words the earlier messengers used, which is the point the sūrah has been making about the unity of the prophetic message.

---

## Verse 3:51

> Surely Allah is my Lord and your Lord. So worship Him ˹alone˺. This is the Straight Path.’”

**"My Lord and Your Lord" — One Lordship, Two Relations**

The Book puts this phrase in the mouth of the same prophet on several occasions, and each time it answers a different error. In the sūrah of Maryam, Jesus declares that God is his Lord and theirs and that this is the Straight Path (19:36). In the sūrah of Ornaments of Gold the words are again his, followed by the same call to worship God alone (43:64). In the sūrah of the Table, the declaration is quoted as the answer to the claim that God is the Messiah: the Messiah himself said, worship Allah, my Lord and your Lord (5:72). The verse under discussion is the sūrah's own statement of the same formula, and its placement inside the list of miracles is deliberate.

**The Sentence That Closes a Prophet's Speech**

The verse ends a speech that began four verses earlier, and it ends on a creed rather than on a wonder. The list of signs has been given — the bird of clay, the healing of the blind and the leper, the raising of the dead, the knowledge of what is hidden in houses — and the speech closes with a single declaration about God.

That arrangement is the sūrah's argument in miniature. A messenger who performs signs that no human being can perform, and who then says nothing about his own status, has answered the question his signs raise before anyone asks it. The sentence he chooses to end on is not "I am" but "He is", and the conclusion he draws from his own miracles is an instruction to worship God.

The words name a shared relationship without flattening it. God is the speaker's Lord and the hearers' Lord, which means both parties stand in the same position of dependence, and the difference between them lies in the mission, not in the nature.

**Other Prophets Said the Same Words**

The phrase is not unique to Jesus, and every reader of the sūrah has heard it before. Moses uses the same pair of possessives when he seeks refuge: I seek refuge in my Lord and your Lord from every arrogant person who does not believe in the Day of Reckoning (40:27), and again when he asks not to be stoned (44:20). Hūd uses the form in the sūrah of Hūd, and the formula recurs throughout the missions recorded in the Book.

The uniformity is the point of revelation's presentation of the prophets. What every messenger announces is that God is the Lord of both speaker and audience, which makes the messenger a servant of the same Lord as the people he addresses. A prophet who stood outside that relation would be a different kind of being, and the Book does not have such a category.

**Worship as the Only Conclusion**

The instruction that follows the declaration is *so worship Him alone*. The particle connects the two sentences logically: because He is the Lord of both, worship is owed to Him by both. The sign and the command are bound together.

---

## Verse 3:52

> When Jesus sensed disbelief from his people, he asked, “Who will stand up with me for Allah?” The disciples replied, “We will stand up for Allah. We believe in Allah, so bear witness that we have submitted.”

**A Prophet Reading His Own People**

The verse begins with a prophet detecting something: when Jesus sensed disbelief from among his people, he asked a question. The word *kufr* here describes not a private opinion but a settled hostility, and the commentaries understood it as the point at which the rejection had become active — the hostility that made it necessary for him to look for support elsewhere.

**The Word They Use About Themselves**

The clause rendered "we have submitted" uses the participle, and the disciples by this take the same word the sūrah used of everyone who has handed himself over to God. The Book uses it of Abraham and Jacob advising their children, and of the disciples again in another sūrah where their declaration is recorded with the word for belief and the request for witness (5:111). The word is not the property of any later community; it is what a person is when God's claim has been accepted.

**The Same Exchange in Another Sūrah**

The Book recounts the disciples' answer a second time as part of a longer passage. The believers are told to stand up for God as Jesus asked the disciples, quoting the question and the answer and adding the sequel: a group of the Children of Israel believed and another disbelieved, and God supported the believers against their enemies so that they prevailed (61:14).

---

The disciples are named *ḥawāriyyūn*, and the term is used of them in three places: here, at the inspiration that moved them to believe (5:111), and at the sūrah of the Ranks, where the same exchange between Jesus and the disciples is held up as the model for the believers' standing up for God (61:14). The response recorded here — "we are Allah's helpers, we believe, so bear witness that we are those who submit" — is answered in the next verse by the plan of the disbelievers of Israel and the counter-plan of God (3:54). What the passage gives the chapter is a pattern: the prophet's question is put to a small group when the larger community refuses, and the group's answer is preserved for every later community that hears the same call.

## Verse 3:53

> ˹They prayed to Allah,˺ “Our Lord! We believe in Your revelations and follow the messenger, so count us among those who bear witness.”

**A Prayer Built From the Preceding Verse**

The disciples have affirmed their belief and their support to the messenger; they now say the same things to God. The verse is their supplication, and its three clauses repeat, in the language of worship, the three clauses they had spoken to a human being.

**"We Believe in Your Revelations"**

The wording avoids a narrowness that a reader might expect. These are men who belong to the community of Israel and who accept a prophet raised among them; their confession is not "we believe in our prophet" but "we believe in Your revelations". The distinction matters in a sūrah that repeatedly criticises people who accepted part of what they were given. The disciples' statement covers the whole; there is no portion of revelation they set aside.

**"Count Us Among Those Who Bear Witness"**

The request is unusual in its modesty and its precision. The disciples do not ask for reward, protection, or victory; they ask to be registered in a category. The category is *the witnesses*, and the Book uses it as one of its most honourable designations, applied to the community of the Prophet as a whole: you have been made an upright community so that you may be witnesses over humanity, and the Messenger a witness over you (2:143).

**Who the Witnesses Are in the Wider Book**

The theme is one of the sūrah's deepest concerns. At the opening of the passage about the witness-bearing community, God is called the foremost witness that there is no god but Him, with the angels and the people of knowledge testifying after Him (3:18). Later in the sūrah, those who refuse the invitation are invited to bear witness to the submission of the believers (3:64). The disciples' prayer in this verse slots into that structure: God witnesses to Himself, the learned witness to what they know, and a community of ordinary believers is asked to carry the same testimony before humanity.

---

## Verse 3:54

> And the disbelievers made a plan ˹against Jesus˺, but Allah also planned—and Allah is the best of planners

**Makr: the Word Both Sides Share**

The Arabic verb behind both halves of the sentence is *makara*, and using one word for the plotting of the enemies and for the action of God is a rhetorical choice the Book repeats elsewhere. The conspirators against the Prophet of this sūrah are described in the same terms: they plotted to capture, kill, or expel him, and the verse ends with the same declaration that God is the best of planners (8:30).

The Book uses the word freely of God, and it does not soften it into a different verb to protect a doctrine of divine conduct. What it does instead is to repeat the comparison in a range of contexts — 13:42 where the earlier communities planned and God had the ultimate plan, 27:50 where a plan was made and a plan was made in return while the plotters were unaware. Using one word for both keeps the contest intelligible: the same kind of activity is going on, with an unbridgeable difference of power and knowledge behind it.

**What God's Plan Was in This Case**

The sūrah states the answer three verses later, in the form of a reminder addressed to Jesus; other passages fill in the moment itself. The Book says of the conspiracy to kill Jesus that he was neither killed nor crucified as the plotters assumed, that those who argue for the crucifixion are in doubt about it, and that God raised him up to Himself (4:157–158).

**"Best of Planners" — a Comparison, Not a Contest**

The word also removes a misunderstanding the previous clauses might create. Saying that God "also planned" could suggest a deity who reacts to human schemes, arriving late with a countermeasure. The superlative says otherwise: the plans of creatures are the small, visible movements in a design they cannot see, and the Book's habit of speaking of God's planning in the same vocabulary as human plotting is a concession to human capacity for understanding, not a description of two equals at a board. What looks like a contest from below is an arrangement from above.

**The Three Other Passages About Plotting**

The Book states the same contrast in three further places, and each of them supplies a detail the sentence here leaves out. The sūrah of the Ant tells the same story of a people who made a plan while an unseen plan was being made for them, and it notes that they were unaware of it (27:50).

---

## Verse 3:55

> ˹Remember˺ when Allah said, “O Jesus! I will take you and raise you up to Myself. I will deliver you from those who disbelieve, and elevate your followers above the disbelievers until the Day of Judgment. Then to Me you will ˹all˺ return, and I will settle all your disputes

**God Speaking to His Servant**

The verse opens with a reminder of a conversation: when God said, O Jesus. The speaker is God and the addressee is the messenger, and the words that follow are a set of promises spoken at the moment the plot of the previous verse was in motion.

**Mutawaffī: the Word That Carries the Dispute**

The first promise uses a word from the root that governs taking and completing, and the sūrah's translation renders it as taking to Himself. The commentators spent considerable effort on it, and their disagreement is worth setting out plainly. A large group held that the word ordinarily refers to death, that God takes a person to Himself at the end of his life. That in this verse, in view of what the Book says about the crucifixion, it means God's removal of Jesus from the world rather than his death at the hands of enemies. Others held that the word does retain its ordinary sense here and that the verse describes God causing him to die.
