A third reading took it in the sense of sleep, comparing the verse that says God calls back souls by night (6:60) and the verse that says He takes the souls of people at their death and those who do not die during their sleep (39:42).

**"And Raise You Up to Myself"**

The second promise names an elevation, and the Book states the same event when it answers the boasts of the plotters: they did not kill him, and God raised him up to Himself (4:158). The two verses describe one act from two angles, one as the removal of a servant from a hostile world and the other as an ascent.

---

The promises God speaks to Jesus in this verse are read by the commentators word by word. "I will take you" uses the verb of taking in death or in sleep, and the two readings follow from it: death at the natural term, or the raising in a state of sleep before the ascension. "And raise you up to Myself" states the elevation itself, which the sūrah of the Women describes in its own terms — they did not kill him or crucify him, but God raised him to Himself (4:157–158) — and the sūrah of Maryam preserves the same three moments in one line: peace on the day he was born, the day he dies, and the day he is raised alive (19:33). The purification from the disbelievers and the elevation of his followers "above those who disbelieve until the Day of Resurrection" is the historical promise the Christian communities' survival in the world fulfilled, and the verse ends with the gathering to God that awaits every party.

## Verse 3:56

> As for those who disbelieve, I will subject them to a severe punishment in this life and the Hereafter, and they will have no helpers

**Punishment in This Life and the Next**

The punishment is described as severe and as falling in two places: the life of the world and the Hereafter. The Book applies the two-world pattern to both reward and punishment in several passages, and the commentators noted that the pairing is deliberate and consistent.

**The Speech Continues**

The verse remains inside the speech that began in verse 55, and it turns from the messenger to the people who refused him. The promises given to the servant are followed by the consequences prepared for those who rejected him, and the sūrah keeps both halves of the announcement in the same voice and the same passage.

What the punishment in this life consists of is left unspecified here, and other passages fill in the range: military defeat and displacement, the loss of the standing a community relied on, and the internal divisions that follow when a doctrine outlives the ability to defend it. The important element for the verse's argument is that the reckoning is not postponed completely to another world. The Book describes a God whose judgments operate inside history, and the sūrah has already offered the fate of the people of Pharaoh as the proof that they do.

**"They Will Have No Helpers"**

The closing clause is the one that gives the sentence its weight. The sūrah used the phrase earlier for the deeds of a particular group being wasted, and it recurs across the Book in warnings about the Day of Judgment. A helper in the vocabulary of the Qur'an is not a friend in the ordinary sense; he is the person whose support changes what happens to you.

**Two Arenas of Recompense**

The sentence names this life and the Hereafter together as the places where the punishment of the rejecters is meted out, and the same pairing appears in several passages. One verse speaks of those who are disgraced in this world and have a severe punishment in the next (2:114). The sūrah of the Family of ʿImrān makes the same statement earlier in its own words (3:22); and the sūrah of the Bee speaks of the evil of this life and the punishment of the Hereafter as two parts of one outcome (16:30). The two arenas are described for the believers in corresponding terms, since the Book promises good in this world and a better reward in the next to those who act well (16:122; 39:10). The verse therefore does not present the worldly suffering of a people as proof of its standing, nor worldly ease as a sign of favour.

---

## Verse 3:57

> And as for those who believe and do good, they will be rewarded in full. And Allah does not like the wrongdoers.”

**The Other Half of the Sentence**

The previous verse stated the end of those who disbelieved; this one states the end of those who believed and did good. The two form one sentence in the original sequence, and the sūrah gives the reward the second position, so that the passage closes on the group it hopes its readers will join.

The construction is familiar from the Book's treatment of the Day of Judgment, where the two outcomes are normally stated together. What deserves attention here is the order and the proportion. The warning occupies a full sentence about punishment in two worlds and the absence of helpers; the promise occupies a shorter sentence about full reward, and a clause about God's disposition that does not contain the word reward at all.

**"Rewarded in Full"**

The Book's language about reward goes further in other places, offering multiplication beyond measure, gifts without reckoning, and increases out of God's grace. Setting those verses beside this one shows how the Book holds two things together: what is owed is paid in full, and what is given exceeds what was owed. A servant who has done little and receives much is not being treated unfairly; he is dealing with a giver whose account of the transaction is generous rather than exact.

---

## Verse 3:58

> We recite ˹all˺ this to you ˹O Prophet˺ as one of the signs and ˹as˺ a wise reminder

**"We Recite It to You"**

The verb describes a recitation taking place in the present, and the Book uses it for the act by which revelation is delivered to the Prophet. The same verb appears at the openings of sūrahs where the revelation is being called to the reader's attention, and it places the Prophet in the position of a hearer rather than an author.

**The Two Descriptions: Signs and a Wise Reminder**

The verse calls what it has recited two things. First, the material is a sign, and the Book uses that word for both the verses of revelation and the events of the natural world; both are described as things put in front of a person to be read. Second, the whole of it is a reminder that is wise, and the commentators noted that this phrase is used elsewhere as a name for the Qur'an, with some taking it to refer to the Preserved Tablet from which revelation comes down.

---

This verse closes the passage on the birth and the ascension of Jesus (3:35–57) and opens the one that follows, where the People of the Book are called to the truth about him. The two words it uses for what has been recited are exact: "signs" (*āyāt*), the visible proofs, and "a wise reminder" (*dhikr ḥakīm*), the same word the sūrah uses for the Book in 3:7. The reminder is "wise" because its arrangement and its judgments are deliberate — the word is used in the Qur'an of the revelation as the thing that reminds people of what they already carry and what they have forgotten. The same form of words — we recite it to you — closes other passages of the sūrah (3:108), and its function each time is to mark the boundary between the recitation and the argument built on it: what God recites is the ground, and what the sūrah then does with it is the explanation.

## Verse 3:59

> Indeed, the example of Jesus in the sight of Allah is like that of Adam. He created him from dust, then said to him, “Be!” And he was

**Why Adam Is the Decisive Case**

The choice of Adam rather than another figure is deliberate, and it is worth seeing what the comparison does that a comparison with, say, Isaac would not have done. Isaac had a father and a mother, one of whom was beyond the age of childbearing; his case would have been an example of God overcoming a natural obstacle. Adam's case is categorically different: he had no parents at all, and his existence began with dust and a command.

If being created without a human father is evidence of divinity, then Adam has the better claim to it, since he was created without a human ancestry completely. Since nobody makes that claim for Adam, the inference from the virgin birth collapses. The Book's argument is so not that the miracle did not happen; it is that miracles of origin are statements about the one who makes them.

**Dust and the Command**

The verse describes the creation of Adam in two stages: God created him from dust, then said to him, Be, and he was. The Book states the same creation in its opening sūrah, where the creation of the first man is followed by the account of his knowledge and the prostration of the angels, and the phrase is used consistently to describe a bringing-into-being that has no process.

**The Formula "Be" Throughout the Book**

The command appears in the Book wherever creation is described, and its repetitions form one of the Qur'an's most consistent themes. The Originator of the heavens and the earth, when He decrees a matter, tells it to be and it is (2:117). God's affair, when He wills something, is to say to it Be and it is (36:82). He who created the heavens and the earth in truth will say on the Day of Judgment Be and it will be (6:73), and the same words describe the giver of life and death when He decrees a matter (40:68). In the sūrah of the Bee, the formula states that God's command, when He wills a thing, is a single word (16:40).

Placing this verse in that series shows that the creation of Jesus is not an isolated exception but an instance of the way God has always acted. The Book's doctrine of creation is that existence follows a command, and the fact that a particular existence had an unusual origin is a difference in the circumstances, not in the kind of act.

---

## Verse 3:60

> This is the truth from your Lord, so do not be one of those who doubt

**An Address Directed at the Prophet**

The verse changes addressee again and speaks to the Prophet of the sūrah about the account he has just received. What has been recited about Jesus, his birth, his mission, and the comparison with Adam is described as truth from his Lord, and the sentence closes with a prohibition against doubt.

The combination of a statement and a warning in one sentence is characteristic of the Book's manner with its own messenger. The Prophet is not excused from the demand the revelation makes on everyone who hears it, and the Book's treatment of him throughout contains warnings as well as commendations. A messenger who is told not to doubt is a messenger who is being held to the same standard he is delivering.

**"Truth From Your Lord"**

The construction recurs when the Book wants to end a discussion with a verdict rather than an argument. In the sūrah of the Cow, after the change of the direction of prayer and the disputes it produced, the verse states that what has come to them is the truth from their Lord and that they should not be among those who doubt. The sūrah here states the same formula at the close of the dispute about Jesus, and the effect is to remove the question from the plane of debate and return it to the plane of revelation.

---

## Verse 3:61

> Now, whoever disputes with you ˹O Prophet˺ concerning Jesus after full knowledge has come to you, say, “Come! Let us gather our children and your children, our women and your women, ourselves and yourselves—then let us sincerely invoke Allah’s curse upon the liars.”

**Who Was Brought to the Meeting**

The accounts differ in their details but agree on the essentials. The Prophet came out the next day for the appointment accompanied by his daughter Fāṭīmah and her two sons Ḥasan and Ḥusayn, and several accounts also include ʿAlī, her husband and the Prophet's cousin. Since the verse had named children, women, and selves, the party corresponds to the summons in each category.

The picture recorded in the tradition has become one of the most frequently cited scenes in later Islamic literature, and it is used to establish the standing of the household that was brought. The commentaries note that the Prophet brought no one else from among a large community, which the tradition reads as evidence of his confidence in the outcome. What the historical record shows without any interpretive layer is a small family group standing at an appointment that the other side did not keep.

**The Refusal and the Agreement**

The refusal of the challenge was followed by a settlement. According to the accounts, the Najrān delegation remained Christian, entered a treaty with the Muslim community, and undertook an annual payment, and the Prophet sent a trusted companion back with them at their request to serve among them as an arbiter.

---

The event is reported with a detail that completes it: when the appointed day came the delegation did not turn out for the invocation of the curse, and accepted instead the terms of protection under the Muslim polity — the outcome the sīrah records for the Christians of Najrān. The verse's expression for the challenge is precise: the meeting is called "after the knowledge that has come to you", which places the responsibility on those who now know and dispute anyway. The dispute itself is settled in the next verses, where the sūrah states what Jesus was and was not (3:59) and calls the family of ʿImrān part of the truth that the Prophet recites (3:61–63). The closing words of the verse, that the curse of God is upon the liars, are not spoken by the Prophet against his opponents; they are the sūrah's own statement of what a lie about God carries.

## Verse 3:62

> Certainly, this is the true narrative, and there is no god ˹worthy of worship˺ except Allah. And indeed, Allah ˹alone˺ is the Almighty, All-Wise

**Where the Argument Leaves the Reader**

The verse ends the dispute about Jesus with a declaration rather than a rebuttal, and it does not address the disputants again. What follows turns to the community of the Book and to Abraham, and it begins a new phase of the sūrah's engagement with the claims of the scriptured peoples.

Readers of the intervening centuries have found in these verses the summary of the Book's position on the nature of Christ, and the summary is complete on its own terms. The narrative is true, the deity is one, and the power and wisdom that arranged the birth belong to the one who arranged it. What the sūrah does next is to test the claims of the communities that disputed on the basis of an ancestor they shared with the Muslim community.

**The Formula Used Here**

The sentence has no god except God is not the wording of the community's standard testimony of faith. The usual formula begins with the negation of every deity and then affirms the one God; the version in this verse begins with the particle of restriction, and al-Rāzī and al-Zamakhsharī described the construction as a stronger form of the negation. The difference is one of emphasis rather than of content, and the commentators noted it because the community's daily testimony uses the other wording. The choice of the stronger form here suits the sentence's position at the end of a dispute about claims made about God and His messengers, and it leaves the reader with the affirmation on which the whole passage rests.

---

## Verse 3:63

> If they turn away, then surely Allah has ˹perfect˺ knowledge of the corruptors

**The Refusal Named Once More**

The verse follows the declaration of God's oneness with a conditional sentence: if they turn away, then God knows the corruptors. Three times in this sūrah the same refusal has been named, and each time the Book has responded differently — once by stating the duty of the messenger, once by naming the consequence in the language of divine disfavour, and here by referring the whole matter to knowledge.

The repetition traces the course of an argument that has been made and declined. The sūrah is not describing a fresh conversation; it is describing the position of people who have heard the recitation, the account of Jesus, and the creed, and have maintained their course. What the verse adds to the previous statements is a description of the refusal that is also a verdict, delivered as a fact about God rather than as a judgement passed by the messenger.

**A Conclusion That Opens the Next Section**

The transition is smooth because the question has been prepared. The sūrah has just declared the truth of its account of Jesus and the oneness of God; it now turns to the ancestor whom the disputing parties shared with the Muslim community, and to the claim that he belonged to one of them. The refusers have just been described as people who spoil what they hold, and the next verses will show what they do with Abraham.

---

## Verse 3:64

> Say, ˹O Prophet,˺ “O People of the Book! Let us come to common terms: that we will worship none but Allah, associate none with Him, nor take one another as lords instead of Allah.” But if they turn away, then say, “Bear witness that we have submitted ˹to Allah alone˺.”

**An Invitation Addressed to the Scriptured Communities**

The sūrah turns from the dispute about Jesus to a direct address to the People of the Book, and the address begins with an invitation rather than a charge. The Prophet is told what to say, and what he says is *let us come*.

**The Three Propositions**

The invitation names three commitments: worship of none but God, the association of nothing with Him, and the refusal to take one another as lords instead of God. The three form a single position stated from different angles.

The first two concern God directly, and they are the ground of everything the sūrah has said about Jesus: a messenger whose birth was a wonder is not a partner in divinity. The third concerns human authorities. The commentators offered two readings of it — that it forbids the prostration paid to religious leaders, and that it forbids obedience to them carried to the point of setting aside what is true. The Book states the same charge elsewhere in harsher language, describing people who took their rabbis and monks as lords apart from God, along with the Messiah, though they were commanded to worship one God (9:31). The clause in this verse is that criticism in the form of an invitation to its remedy.

**The Plural That Puts Both Sides Inside the Sentence**

The closing clause completes the movement. When the other party refuses, the community is told to state its own position rather than to press the case further, and the word it is told to use is the one the sūrah has attached to the religion itself (3:19). The same word is spoken by Abraham and Jacob to their children in the sūrah of the Cow, where the father's advice ends with the instruction not to die except in submission (2:132). The verse therefore ends where the community's own confession lives, and it leaves the invitation standing as an offer whose terms were public.

---

## Verse 3:65

> O People of the Book! Why do you argue about Abraham, while the Torah and the Gospel were not revealed until long after him? Do you not understand

The verse addresses the People of the Book again, and it addresses them with a question about their own claim: why do you argue about Abraham? The dispute in view is a competition over which community may claim him, and the Book's response is to point at the calendar.

The argument is as simple as it is decisive.

**Ḥanīf: the Word for What Abraham Was**

The sūrah's account of what Abraham actually was comes in the next two verses, and the term at its centre is *ḥanīf*, a word the Book applies to a person who turns away from false worship toward the one God. In the vocabulary of the Qur'an it describes an inclination rather than a membership, and its consistent companion is the phrase "and he was not a polytheist".

The pairing appears throughout the Book's references to Abraham: the sūrah of the Cow gives the believers the creed of Abraham the upright, who was not of the idolaters (2:135). The sūrah of the Bees describes him as a model of excellence, devoted, upright, and not a polytheist (16:120); the sūrah of the Cattle gives the same formula in the Prophet's own declaration (6:161). The Book treats the term as a description of Abraham's actual religion before any community organised around a book, which is why it serves as the answer to a competition between those communities.

**A Shared Ancestor and a Shared Difficulty**

The argument in this verse cuts against both claimants at once, and the sūrah means it to. The Jews of Madinah and the Christians of the region each had a narrative of belonging that ran through Abraham, and each found in him a warrant for its own standing.

---

## Verse 3:66

> Here you are! You disputed about what you have ˹little˺ knowledge of, but why do you now argue about what you have no knowledge of? Allah knows and you do not know

**A Rebuke in Two Stages**

The verse takes the form of an observation about the disputants' own record: you argued about things you had some knowledge of, and now you argue about something you have no knowledge of at all. The sentence sets two disagreements beside each other and marks the difference in what the parties could bring to them.

**What the Two Disputes Were**

Commentators offered several accounts of the disagreements in view. The matters the disputants had knowledge of were variously identified as their own scripture and the points at which they differed over it, the disputation over what their laws permitted and forbade, and the question of whether the new revelation agreed with or differed from the earlier books. The matter they had no knowledge of was their competing claims about Abraham, about which, as the sūrah has just pointed out, no direct report was available to them.

The distinction is one that any community with a tradition can understand. There is a difference between arguing from evidence within a field one has studied and arguing about a figure from a remote past on the basis of inherited pride. The verse does not dispute the community's competence in the first category. It points out that the second category has been treated as though it were the first.

**Disputing After Knowledge in the Sūrah's Other Sentences**

The distinction this verse draws is one the sūrah has been making since its opening pages. It states that the People of the Book did not dispute among themselves out of mutual envy until knowledge had come to them (3:19), and it warns the believers against becoming like those who divided and differed after the clear proofs had reached them (3:105). The Book returns to the same diagnosis in the account of the first community, which is said to have disputed the scriptures only after the proofs arrived, and to have done so out of jealousy (2:213).

---

## Verse 3:67

> Abraham was neither a Jew nor a Christian; he submitted in all uprightness and was not a polytheist

**Two Negations Before an Affirmation**

The verse answers the question of what Abraham was, and it begins by excluding the two categories that the disputants wanted him to occupy. He was neither a Jew nor a Christian, and he was not a polytheist. Only after clearing both names does the sentence say what he was.

**Ḥanīf: Turning Away and Turning Toward**

The word rendered "in all uprightness" describes a person who inclines away from the worship of anything other than God. In the Book's usage the term is tied to Abraham specifically, and it carries the idea of a deliberate turn — away from the practices of a people who had gone astray, and toward the one to whom worship belongs.

The Book pairs the term with Abraham in several places. The sūrah of the Cattle records the Prophet's own declaration of following the way of Abraham, the upright, who was not of the polytheists (6:161). The sūrah of the Bees describes Abraham as a model of excellence, devoted to God and upright, and again denies that he was a polytheist (16:120); and the sūrah of the Cow instructs the believers to say that their way is that of Abraham, the upright (2:135). The recurrence shows that the term functions in the Book as the name of a religion that existed before the books, which is why it can serve as the shared ground in this dispute.

**The Third Negation**

The clause denying that Abraham was a polytheist is not a repetition of the first two denials, and it deserves its own attention. A person could be neither Jewish nor Christian and still be a worshipper of other gods, since those were not the only options available in the ancient world.

---

The verse's argument is chronological, and the sūrah has just made the same move about the food laws: labels that postdate a person cannot describe him. Abraham was neither a Jew nor a Christian because the communities bearing those names came into being long after him; the word the verse keeps for him, *ḥanīf*, names the upright turning to God that preceded every later division, and the word *muslim* names what he did — the submission that gave the religion its name. The sūrah of the Cow already answered the claim in the same terms: when the Jews and Christians each said "follow our faith to be rightly guided", the reply was that the community follows the faith of Abraham the upright, who was not a polytheist (2:135). The next verse completes the thought: those closest to Abraham are the ones who followed him, this Prophet, and the believers (3:68).

## Verse 3:68

> Indeed, those who have the best claim to Abraham are his followers, this Prophet, and the believers. And Allah is the Guardian of those who believe

**Follower Before Ancestor**

The disputes the sūrah has been answering turned on ancestry, and this verse replaces ancestry with adherence. The Arabic term used for the followers describes those who came after a person on a road, taking his direction, and the Book uses it for the relation of a community to a prophet throughout.

The replacement has consequences that the sūrah's readers would feel. A claim resting on descent can be inspected in a genealogy; a claim resting on following can be inspected in conduct. The Book consistently prefers the second test, and it does so without denying that the first exists, since the descent of the Prophet of this sūrah from Abraham through Ishmael is part of the tradition. What the verse refuses is the notion that the genealogy settles the matter. A descendant who does not follow the ancestor's religion is not being described here as the ancestor's, and the Book makes that point elsewhere in explicit terms when it denies that a covenant extends to wrongdoers (2:124).

---

## Verse 3:69

> Some of the People of the Book wish to mislead you ˹believers˺. They mislead none but themselves, yet they fail to perceive it

**A Wish Directed at the Believers**

The verse reports an intention rather than an act. Some among the People of the Book want to lead the believers astray — the verb describes a wish to pull a person off a road — and the sūrah states the desire without describing any particular try.

**"They Mislead None but Themselves"**

The second half of the sentence reverses the direction of the harm. The wish to mislead is real, and the Book says its outcome falls on those who hold it.

The Book states this reversal repeatedly, and with a variety of images. The hypocrites are described as hoping to deceive God and the believers while deceiving only themselves (2:9); the community in the desert is told that those who broke faith wronged nobody but themselves (2:57); and elsewhere the Book says that a person's plotting encircles its author. The claim is not that the intended victims escape harm in every case, since the verse exists precisely to warn them. It is that the deeper damage of an act of corruption settles on the one who commits it, and the sūrah places that fact before it names the parties.

**The Envy Behind the Wish**

The sūrah's other account of the same situation names the motive: a group of the People of the Book wish to turn the believers back into disbelievers out of envy in their own souls, after the truth has become clear to them (2:109). The verse under discussion does not repeat the diagnosis, but the passages belong together, and the commentators read them as one report.

---

## Verse 3:70

> O People of the Book! Why do you reject the signs of Allah while you bear witness ˹to their truth˺

**A Question That Puts the Accused on the Spot**

The sūrah now addresses the People of the Book directly, and it begins with a question about conduct rather than belief: why do you reject the signs of God while you bear witness? The form of the question assumes that the addressees have an answer to defend and asks them to produce it.

**The Warnings This One Echoes**

The same charge is made twice more in the sūrah. A few verses later the People of the Book are asked why they turn those who believe away from the way of God, seeking to make it crooked. They are witnesses (3:99); the wording of that passage and of this one are close enough that the two sentences are read together by the commentators. The second part of the same warning, about concealing and confounding the truth, repeats the charge made in the sūrah of the Cow, where the recipients of the scripture are told not to mix truth with falsehood or to conceal the truth while they know it (2:42). The repetition of the same accusation at three points in the argument indicates how the sūrah treats the conduct it describes: not as an isolated error but as a settled policy.

**What Being a Witness Means**

Al-Ṭabarī read the verses as referring to the refusal of the Prophet's mission by Jews and Christians although, on the Muslim understanding, his coming is announced in their own books (7:157). The witness in the sentence therefore describes people who are in a position to recognize what they reject. Al-Qurṭūbī recorded another reading on which the witnessing refers to their own tradition: they bear witness to the truth brought by the prophets who preceded them, whose message agrees with what the Prophet is teaching, so that their rejection of him contradicts the testimony they already carry. On either reading, the question in the verse is not a request for information but a statement that the people addressed have grounds for accepting what they deny.

---

## Verse 3:71

> O People of the Book! Why do you mix the truth with falsehood and hide the truth knowingly

**The Second Question**

The verse keeps the same addressee and the same interrogative form, and it sharpens the charge. The first question concerned rejection; this one concerns the handling of truth and falsehood, and it names two acts: mixing the two, and concealing the truth while knowing it.

**The Image Behind "Mixing"**

The Book states the same charge in the sūrah of the Cow in almost identical words, warning the community not to clothe truth with falsehood nor to conceal the truth while knowing it (2:42). The two verses are usually read together, and the commentators treated them as describing one practice rather than two. The practice is the characteristic one of a community that wants to hold its position while retaining the authority of the revelation it holds. The truth is not denied; it is used as a vessel.

**Concealment and the Charge of Distortion**

The second act named is concealment, and the Book's treatment of it elsewhere is explicit about what is being hidden. In this sūrah's later verses, a group is described as distorting the book with their tongues so that what they say appears to be from it, and the verse ends by saying that they knowingly attribute lies to God (3:78).

**A Charge That Applies to Any Custodian of a Text**

Read without the immediate controversy, the verse states a rule for anyone who holds a revelation. The two acts it forbids — blending the true with the false, and withholding what one knows — are the ordinary operations by which a religious community defends a position it can no longer support from its own sources.

---

The charge is given in the fewest possible words, and every word is a separate offence: the truth is mixed with falsehood, and the truth is then hidden by people who know it. The Book states the same prohibition at the head of the sūrah of the Cow: do not mix truth with falsehood or hide the truth knowingly (2:42), and the sūrah has already entered the same charge twice in this chapter, in the question about disbelief (3:70) and in the covenant to publish the Book that ends in concealment (3:187). The commentators read the two verbs as covering two methods: confusion, which blurs the line between what was revealed and what was invented, and suppression, which removes a clear statement altogether. The address is put as a question — why do you do it? — which leaves the answer to the accused.

## Verse 3:72

> A group among the People of the Book said ˹to one another˺, “Believe in what has been revealed to the believers in the morning and reject it in the evening, so they may abandon their faith

**A Stratagem Reported in Full**

The verse reports what a group among the People of the Book said to one another, and it reports it as a plan rather than as an accusation. The members of the group are not named; the sūrah records the scheme in their own words, which is why the reader meets it as a conspiracy rather than as a charge laid by an adversary.

The Book's decision to disclose the plan has a purpose beyond warning the believers. A community that has been told what is being said about it in private can no longer be surprised by the manoeuvre, and the sūrah has been preparing its readers for exactly this information in the verses that precede it. The disclosure is part of the defence.

**A Record Kept for the Community's Use**

A reader in a later century can take the verse as a model of what to expect rather than as a story about others. Any community whose members are admired by their neighbours will face the same kind of pressure, and the condition for withstanding it is the one the sūrah keeps supplying: knowledge of what one has accepted and of the ground on which it was accepted.

---

The plan's shape is worth stating plainly: the group proposed to accept the new revelation with the crowd in the morning and to renounce it in the evening, so that the believers' confidence would be shaken by the sight of people who knew the Scripture turning away from it. The sūrah has already charged the same group with mixing truth and falsehood (3:71) and with concealing the truth they knew. The answer to the scheme comes in the verse that follows: guidance is Allah's, He grants it to whom He wills, and His bounty is great (3:73–74). The commentators read the stratagem as a tacit compliment to the community's steadiness: a plan built on the hope that others would follow them out of the faith assumed that the community's belief hung on learned men rather than on the revelation itself.

## Verse 3:73

> And only believe those who follow your religion.” Say, ˹O Prophet,˺ “Surely, ˹the only˺ true guidance is Allah’s guidance.” ˹They also said,˺ “Do not believe that someone will receive ˹revealed˺ knowledge similar to yours or argue against you before your Lord.” Say, ˹O Prophet,˺ “Indeed, all bounty is in the Hands of Allah—He grants it to whoever He wills. And Allah is All-Bountiful, All-Knowing.”

**A Second Clause of the Same Plan**

The verse continues the report of what the group said among themselves, and the second part of their plan concerns who deserves to be believed at all. Their proposal is that only members of their own religion should be accepted as credible — a rule that closes the circle of trust around the community that made it.

**The Interjection From God**

The sūrah breaks into the quotation with an instruction to the Prophet: say, guidance is God's guidance. The interruption splits the reported speech and answers it in the middle, and the commentators noticed the grammatical awkwardness and discussed several ways of reading the sentence.

However the syntax is resolved, the content is decisive. The claim that truth is confined to one community is met by the statement that guidance belongs to God, which places the matter outside the jurisdiction of any group. The sūrah has used the same principle earlier in a passage that described the well-grounded in knowledge accepting the whole of what was revealed, and it will return to it again when it states that bounty is in God's hands. What the remark does is to remove the question of credibility from the arena of communal authority and return it to the one who guides.

---

The exclusivity claim is the second half of the scheme: no one is to be believed unless he follows their religion, and no one outside their group is to be given what they have been given, or to dispute with them before God. The verse's answer is a sentence the sūrah states twice: guidance is God's guidance, and He chooses whom He wills for His mercy (3:73–74). The Book answers the claim of a closed revelation elsewhere in the same terms — the Jews and the Christians each say "follow our faith to be rightly guided", and the reply is that the community follows the faith of Abraham the upright (2:135). The chapter's point is that the custodianship of truth was never a property right of any community: what they were given was given to be published, and they had hidden it (3:187).

## Verse 3:74

> He chooses whoever He wills to receive His mercy. And Allah is the Lord of infinite bounty

**The Comfort and the Caution in One Sentence**

For the believers who had been warned that a group wished to mislead them, the sentence is a reassurance about the ground they stand on. What they hold was given, and it can neither be earned away from them nor taken by the manoeuvres described a few verses earlier.

For those who had made the plan, the sentence is a caution. The gifts they were guarding belong to someone else, and the choice of recipients has never been theirs. The Book states both readings in one sentence without differentiating its audience, which is how it usually handles a matter that concerns everyone in the argument, and it leaves the reader to work out which side of the sentence he is standing on.

There is one more thing the verse does, and it is easy to overlook because it is small. It describes the mercy as something God chooses someone *for*, rather than something a person qualifies for by rank, learning, or lineage. A community that has spent generations curating its standing will find the sentence uncomfortable for the same reason the disputants of this sūrah found it uncomfortable: it makes the criterion invisible from outside. No one can inspect another person's selection, and no one can certify his own, which is why the Book's readers are left with the practice the sūrah has been describing rather than with a status they can point to.

---

## Verse 3:75

> There are some among the People of the Book who, if entrusted with a stack of gold, will readily return it. Yet there are others who, if entrusted with a single coin, will not repay it unless you constantly demand it. This is because they say, “We are not accountable for ˹exploiting˺ the Gentiles.” And ˹so˺ they attribute lies to Allah knowingly

**The Test of a Trust**

The verse describes a community by its behaviour with property, and it uses a comparison to make the description exact. There are those among the People of the Book who return a large deposit without being pursued, and there are those who refuse to return even a small sum unless the creditor stays at their door.

**The Small Sum and the Large Deposit**

The contrast between the two cases is not between honesty and theft but between honesty that costs nothing and honesty that requires effort. Returning a great deposit to a man who trusts you costs you nothing if you were never going to keep it; returning a single coin to a man who has been asking for it costs you a decision.

**A Large Deposit and a Small One**

The two sums in the sentence are not close together. The word rendered as a stack of gold names a quantity many times larger than a single coin, though al-Rāzī noted that it is not a precise measure. The Book uses the same word elsewhere for heaps of gold and for a great sum handed over at the end of a marriage (3:14; 4:20). The contrast is therefore between a sum worth protecting and a sum hardly worth pursuing, and the test is which of the two each person will honour. The commentators explained the thing entrusted in the widest sense: it can be a loan, the price of a purchase, or property left for safekeeping, since all three involve a claim that the holder is obliged to satisfy.

**The Claim and Its Correction**

Al-Qurṭūbī and al-Rāzī recorded explanations of the excuse the verse reports. On one account, some Jews held that agreements made with members of their own community who later accepted the message were void, and they justified the position from their teaching; on another, the attitude was a matter of tribal loyalty rather than of law. The word the verse puts in their mouths describes non-Jews, and the Book uses the same term elsewhere for those it calls unlettered, which is how the community is described when the argument concerns the possession of scripture (2:78; 3:20; 7:157–58; 62:2). The sūrah's own later passage supplies the corrective, since it states that the People of the Book are not all alike and names an upright group among them whose conduct the verse before the reader has just illustrated (3:113).

**The Correction Ibn ʿAbbās Gave**

The tradition preserved a report in which Muslims came to Ibn ʿAbbās and made a claim of the same shape against a non-Muslim community living under treaty, arguing that nothing prevented them from taking what belonged to those people. He rejected the claim and told them that no wealth might be taken from the treaty peoples beyond what they had undertaken to pay, the indemnity mentioned in the sūrah of Repentance (9:29). The report shows how the community's teachers used this verse: the argument about one group's misuse of a rule became a warning against the same misuse on the other side. Al-Qurṭūbī attached a further report to the passage, one that traces a chain from resentment to betrayal, from betrayal to the loss of compassion, and from there to the departure of faith from a person's heart.

---

## Verse 3:76

> Absolutely! Those who honour their trusts and shun evil—surely Allah loves those who are mindful ˹of Him˺

**The Word for What Must Be Honoured**

The term rendered "their trusts" covers more than a deposit held for safekeeping. It describes everything that has been placed in a person's care, including property, agreements, and the obligations of a role. The Book uses it for duties of office, for the treatment of dependents, and for the whole of a community's inheritance of revelation.

**Mindfulness as the Second Condition**

The verse names two things: the fulfilment of trusts and mindfulness of God. The second is not a decoration on the first. In the Book's usage, *taqwā* is the guardedness that keeps a person aware of his accountability; it is what makes the fulfilment of a trust a matter of conscience rather than convenience.

The pairing appears in the sūrah's earlier description of the people who inherit the Garden, and the same two elements recur in the Book's portraits of the righteous. What the sūrah is doing here is placing the conduct it has been describing against a standard the community itself would have recognised — honest dealing and awareness of God — and pointing out that the two belong together. A person who fulfils a trust because he fears consequences is performing the act; a person who does it because he is mindful of God has understood what the act is for, and it is the second kind of fulfilment that the verse attaches to God's approval.

---

## Verse 3:77

> Indeed, those who trade Allah’s covenant and their oaths for a fleeting gain will have no share in the Hereafter. Allah will neither speak to them, nor look at them, nor purify them on the Day of Judgment. And they will suffer a painful punishment

**The Covenant and the Oaths**

The two things sold are named separately, and the distinction is worth marking. A covenant with God is the standing obligation of a community that has received a revelation and pledged itself to it; an oath is a particular promise made by a particular person, often in the course of a dispute.

Putting the two together covers both the general and the particular. A community's failure is not usually a single lie; it is a practice of making promises it does not keep, which erodes the general pledge along with each specific one. The Book elsewhere states the requirement in the positive form when it says that those who honour their trusts and are mindful of God are the ones He loves, and the same sūrah has already placed the conduct of the earlier community under that standard. What this verse adds is the mechanism: the pledges are traded, and they are traded for something, which means the person making the exchange has made a calculation about what his word is worth.

---

The verse names two things that are sold, and the difference matters: the covenant with God is the standing obligation accepted by a community that received revelation, while an oath is the particular pledge of an individual, often in a dispute over property or a debt. What buys them is a small gain — the phrase elsewhere describes the scribes who produced their own text for a price: "woe to those who distort the Scripture with their own hands then say 'This is from Allah', seeking a fleeting gain" (2:79), and the hide-and-trade of revelation in 2:174. The verse's catalogue of penalties is as precise as the offence: no share in the Hereafter; God will not speak to them; He will not look at them on the Day of Judgement; He will not purify them; and theirs is a painful punishment. The Prophet ﷺ is reported in the Ṣaḥīḥ collections to have warned that the one who takes a Muslim's property by a false oath meets God while He is angry with him, and the verse's silence — the refusal to speak — is the hardest of its sanctions.

## Verse 3:78

> There are some among them who distort the Book with their tongues to make you think this ˹distortion˺ is from the Book—but it is not what the Book says. They say, “It is from Allah”—but it is not from Allah. And ˹so˺ they attribute lies to Allah knowingly

**The Tongue as the Instrument**

The phrase about the tongue is exact. A distortion made with the tongue can be a change of wording, a shift of emphasis, or an explanation attached to the reading so that the hearer cannot separate the two. What all three share is that the original remains intact behind the performance, and the person performing knows where the difference lies.

The Book's other account of the same conduct uses the language of writing: woe to those who write the book with their own hands and then say that it is from God, seeking a small gain (2:79). The two verses supply the two channels by which a scripture can be made to say what it does not — the pen and the voice — and the charge in each case is the same deliberate substitution of a community's position for the text it holds. What the Book objects to is not interpretation; it is the presentation of interpretation as revelation.

**Reading the Verse Inside a Community That Recites**

The offence named here is available to anyone who speaks about scripture with authority, which means it is available to the community reading the verse. What the Book provides against it is the text itself, preserved and recited in public, along with the repeated instruction not to conceal what one knows. A community that keeps its scripture audible and its explanations of it distinguishable from the text has closed the door this verse describes, and the verse's presence in that community's own book is part of the lock. The Book's own example of the alternative is worth noting as well: it reports the covenant taken from those who were given the Scripture, obliging them to make it known to people and not to hide it (3:187). The obligation to publish stands against the practice of bending, and the two verses describe the same community at the two ends of the same choice.

---

## Verse 3:79

> It is not appropriate for someone who Allah has blessed with the Scripture, wisdom, and prophethood to say to people, “Worship me instead of Allah.” Rather, he would say, “Be devoted to the worship of your Lord ˹alone˺”—in accordance with what these prophets read in the Scripture and what they taught

**A Limit Set on the Gifts of God**

The verse is a statement about what is not appropriate for a human being who has been given scripture, wisdom, and prophethood. The list ascends, and the point of the sentence is that the highest accumulation of divine gifts still does not license the invitation to be worshipped.

**The Word Behind "Sages" and Its Relatives**

The term rendered here as sages belongs to a family of words the Book uses for religious learning. Al-Ṭabarī explained the plural as naming people who are wise, knowledgeable, or devout, and al-Qurṭūbī narrowed it to the most eminent of the scholars, those who understand what is lawful and what is forbidden. The same word appears in the sūrah of the Table, where the rabbis and scholars are described as judging by the Book of God with which they had been entrusted (5:44).

A variant form of the same word occurs earlier in this sūrah, where the Book asks how many *“devotees”* fought alongside their prophets without weakening (3:146), which shows how the term moves between the idea of learning and the idea of devoted service. Al-Ṭabarī also recorded a reading of the verse's final phrase that changes the sense of the last clause: instead of saying that the addressee should be among the devout, it reads as a prohibition of the claim. That the sentence forbids the speaker from asserting that he has knowledge of the Book and has taught it. The verse's point survives either reading, since both of them attach the standing to what a person learns and does rather than to what he is called.

**What the Verse Rules Out**
The verse states that it is not for a man to whom God has given the Book, the wisdom and the prophethood to tell people to be his servants rather than God's, and it gives the words such a man would say instead: be devoted to your Lord because of what you teach of the Book and because of what you study. The Book then adds the second prohibition, about commanding the angels and the prophets to be taken as lords (3:80). The passage answers the claim that a prophet could have asked for his own worship, and it names the two things a teacher of the Book actually does: teaches it and studies it.
**Where the Passage Stands**
The sūrah is in the middle of its address to the People of the Book, and it has just dealt with the covenant taken from the prophets that they would believe in the messenger who confirmed what they had (3:81). The verses after the passage turn to the man who turns away after all of it, and the sūrah keeps the two halves of the covenant in view, the knowledge and the obligation. The verse in front of us gives the limit on the teacher, which is the theme the sūrah has been building since it addressed the family of ʿImrān.
**The Community's Use of It**
The commentators read the verse as a rule for everyone who teaches the religion, and the phrase about being devoted to the Lord became one of the Book's names for the people of knowledge, the rabbis who study and teach. The Prophet's report in the collections about the rank of the men who teach the Book is read with it, and the schools of law used the verse in the chapters about the manners of a teacher and the limits of what a student may be asked to do. The passage's key sentence is the one about the Book and the study, since both halves are named as the grounds of the rank.

---

## Verse 3:80

> And he would never ask you to take angels and prophets as lords. Would he ask you to disbelieve after you have submitted

**The Second Prohibition**

The verse continues the description of what a prophet would never ask, and it names the objects: angels and prophets as lords. The previous verse excluded the invitation to worship the speaker; this one excludes the invitation to worship other venerable beings, and it does so in the same construction.

**Angels and Prophets Named Together**

The choice of these two categories is not arbitrary. They represent the two classes of being that the Book treats as the closest to God, and the communities the sūrah addresses had strong traditions about each.

The angels are described in the Book as servants who carry out God's commands without disobedience, and the prophets as the most honoured of humanity. Naming them together as the objects the prohibition covers removes the possibility of a religion of elevated intermediaries, and it does so without denigrating the beings themselves. The Book elsewhere records a conversation in the Hereafter in which Jesus is asked whether he told people to take him and his mother as gods apart from God, and his answer is a declaration of his own servitude. The verse under discussion is that answer stated in advance as a rule about every prophet and every angel.

---

## Verse 3:81

> ˹Remember˺ when Allah made a covenant with the prophets, ˹saying,˺ “Now that I have given you the Book and wisdom, if there comes to you a messenger confirming what you have, you must believe in him and support him.” He added, “Do you affirm this covenant and accept this commitment?” They said, “Yes, we do.” Allah said, “Then bear witness, and I too am a Witness.”

**A Covenant Binding Every Prophet**

The verse reports an undertaking that God made with the prophets as a class. They were given a book and wisdom, and when a messenger came to them confirming what they had, they were to believe in him and support him. The covenant is then ratified: God asks whether they agree and take up the obligation, they answer that they do, and God calls them to witness while declaring Himself a witness as well.

The scope of the sentence is what gives it weight. It is not an agreement made with one people at one time; it is a standing obligation attached to the office of prophethood itself, so that every messenger is bound by it as a condition of his mission. The Book states the same idea here in the form of a documented undertaking, complete with offer, acceptance, and witnesses.

**Who Is Bound by the Covenant**

The commentators differed over the parties addressed, and al-Rāzī set out the options. On one view the command is given to the prophets themselves, who would be obliged to affirm the messenger if he appeared during their lifetimes. On another, the verse addresses the followers of the prophets, since the messengers themselves would never turn away from what God sent, while their communities might; al-Qurṭūbī and al-Rāzī both recorded this reading. The question that follows, asking whether the parties agree and take up the undertaking, was read by al-Rāzī as spoken by God to the prophets or by the prophets to their followers. The closing words of the verse, the command to bear witness, were taken by the commentators to be God's own speech.

---

The commentators took two views of who was addressed, and the difference shows in the verse's words. On the majority reading the prophets themselves were bound: God gave each of them a book and wisdom, and when the final messenger came confirming what they had, they were to believe in him and support him — a covenant to which every prophet subscribed on behalf of the messenger who would follow. A second reading, reported from ʿAlī and others, takes the covenant to have been taken from the prophets' communities. The Book states the same gathering in the sūrah of the Combined Forces, where God recalls taking the covenant from the prophets along with Noah, Abraham, Moses and Jesus, son of Mary (33:7). What the verse establishes either way is the unity of the message: no prophet was sent as a rival to the one who would come after him.

## Verse 3:82

> Whoever turns back after this, they will be the rebellious

**What Turning Back Means**

The Arabic describes a turning away and a reversal of course, and the Book uses the same root for apostasy, for the refusal of a summons, and for the conduct of a community that agrees to something and then abandons it.

The word therefore covers conduct rather than a mental state, which fits the verse's setting. The covenant was to believe in and support a messenger; turning back from it is not the failure to reach a conclusion but the abandonment of an undertaking. The Book's legal vocabulary is consistent on this point: an agreement obliges the one who accepted it, and the seriousness of a breach is proportionate to the standing of the party who was given the assurance. A covenant witnessed by God is the most serious kind, and the description of the violator follows from that fact rather than from any additional accusation.

**The Severity and Its Source**

The sūrah has already noted that a deposit may be returned faithfully by one member of a community and withheld by another, and it will shortly say in explicit terms that they are not all alike. The verse above states a rule about those who turn back, and the qualification runs through the surrounding verses: the Book names conduct and states its outcome without converting the statement into a verdict on an entire people. The next verse raises the question of what a person leaving such a covenant is actually looking for.

---

## Verse 3:83

> Do they desire a way other than Allah’s—knowing that all those in the heavens and the earth submit to His Will, willingly or unwillingly, and to Him they will ˹all˺ be returned

**A Question About Desire**

The verse asks whether the people just described are looking for a way other than God's, and the question is phrased in desire. What is at issue is not only what they do but what they want, and the Book's habit of naming desire as the engine of doctrinal position is on display here.

The question also exposes the impossibility of what is wanted. The sentence continues by stating that everyone in the heavens and the earth submits to God's will, willingly or unwillingly, and that all of them will be returned to Him. A way other than God's is therefore not an alternative route but a contradiction of the only order there is, and the verse's question is designed to be felt as unanswerable rather than answered.

**Submission Willing and Unwilling**

The Book uses the distinction in more than one place. The sūrah of the Thunder declares that everything in the heavens and the earth prostrates to God, willingly or unwillingly, as do their shadows morning and evening (13:15). In the sūrah of Fussilat, when God addresses the heaven and the earth and calls them to submit, they answer together that they submit willingly (41:11). The sūrah here gives the general case; elsewhere the Book gives the same subject in its particular instances, and the verses about the prostrating sun, moon, mountains, and trees belong to the same theme.

---

## Verse 3:84

> Say, ˹O Prophet,˺ “We believe in Allah and what has been revealed to us and what was revealed to Abraham, Ishmael, Isaac, Jacob, and his descendants; and what was given to Moses, Jesus, and other prophets from their Lord—we make no distinction between any of them, and to Him we ˹fully˺ submit.”

**A Confession of Faith That Names Its Contents**

The verse instructs the Prophet to state what the community believes, and the statement begins with God and then lists the revelations given to a series of named prophets. The list runs from Abraham through his descendants to Moses and Jesus and the other prophets, and it ends with a declaration that no distinction is made among them and a statement of submission.

**Abraham and His Descendants Named First**

The list begins with Abraham and continues through Ishmael, Isaac, Jacob, and the tribes descended from him. The order follows the Book's habit of naming the patriarchs together, and the inclusion of Ishmael alongside Isaac is significant in a sūrah that has been arguing about who has the better claim to Abraham.

**Moses, Jesus, and the Other Prophets**

The second half of the list names Moses and Jesus and then generalises: and the other prophets from their Lord. The two names carry the two books the sūrah has discussed at length, and the general phrase covers the rest of the messengers without requiring an inventory.

**The Counterpart Passage About Partial Faith**

The Book states the creed in nearly the same words in the sūrah of the Cow, where the community is told to believe in what was revealed to Abraham, Ishmael, Isaac, Jacob, and the descendants, and to Moses, Jesus, and the other prophets, making no distinction among them (2:136). The same confession is reported once more among the qualities of the believers, together with their declaration that they make no distinction between any of the messengers (2:285).

The rejection of partial acceptance is stated in the sūrah of Women, where the Book describes those who wish to separate God from His messengers, saying that they believe in some and reject others and hoping to find a middle position. Then declares that those who accept all of them and reject none will receive their reward (4:150, 4:152). Read beside that passage, the clause in this verse about making no distinction is not a formality. It is the line the Book draws between a faith that takes the whole message and one that takes what suits it, and the community's confession is placed on the first side of that line.

---

## Verse 3:85

> Whoever seeks a way other than Islam, it will never be accepted from them, and in the Hereafter they will be among the losers

**A Statement About the Accepted Way**

The verse declares that whoever seeks a way other than Islam will not have it accepted from him. The word rendered Islam is the same term the sūrah has used since early in its argument, and its meaning in the Book is submission to God rather than membership in a community.

**Where the "In the Hereafter" Is Placed**

The verse ends by saying that the person who seeks another way will be among the losers in the Hereafter, which locates the outcome of the choice after death.

The Book places this theme at the end of several of its arguments with the communities of the Book, and the reason has to do with the nature of the dispute. The parties who disagreed about Abraham and Jesus were not disputing material advantages, at least not only those; the disagreement concerned what would be accepted from them at the end. The verse states the criterion and states the sphere in which it operates, and it leaves the world in which the argument is being conducted as it is. Nothing in the sentence tells the community to enforce the criterion, and nothing in it describes a punishment in this life. It speaks about the accounting that the whole sūrah has been describing since its opening verses.

---

## Verse 3:86

> How will Allah guide a people who chose to disbelieve after they had believed, acknowledged the Messenger to be true, and received clear proofs? For Allah does not guide the wrongdoing people

**Who Is Being Described**
The commentators offered two main identifications for the people in view. Some took them as apostates who left the community after having accepted the message, and they connected the verse with a report about a group that departed for Makkah and later returned. Others took the passage as continuing the sūrah's address to the People of the Book, who had received revelations and had then refused the message that confirmed them.
**Why Guidance Is Related to Conduct**
The connection runs in both directions in the Book's account, and the sūrah has already stated it in the negative: those who persistently deviated had their hearts caused to deviate, as the Book says of the people of Moses (61:5). The Book's language of guidance describes a process rather than a verdict, and its warnings are addressed to people who still have the ability to turn. What the verse above states is what happens when the turning is unrepented: the guidance that was available is not offered again in the same way, and the condition the verse describes is that of people who have used the means of knowledge to settle their decision.

**The Question and the Stories Behind It**
*Kayfa yahdī Allāhu qawman kafarū baʿda īmānihim* — how shall God guide a people who disbelieved after their belief? The question is a refusal rather than an enquiry, and it introduces the hardest case in the sūrah's argument with the People of the Book. The verses before it describe a group who had believed, then turned back (3:70-73), and the passage names the reason: they had seen the proof, born witness that the Messenger was true, and then turned away. The Book does not treat that as confusion. It treats it as a decision made in daylight.
**What the Verses Around It Add**
The next two verses state the sentence — the curse of God and the angels and all people upon them — and then immediately open the door again: except those who repent after that and mend their ways, for God is forgiving and merciful (3:87-89). The passage then warns that those who disbelieve and go on increasing in disbelief will not have their repentance accepted, and they are the ones who have gone astray (3:90). The whole sequence is a scale: the sin is grave, the door is open, and the door closes only when a person stops walking towards it.
**What the Reader Is Meant to Take**
The verse is about a kind of person rather than a kind of community. The one who has never known the truth is judged by a different standard than the one who believed and testified and then undid it for a price. The Book uses the same language for whoever takes the same route from any starting point, and the sūrah of the Bee places a person who denies after believing in a worse position than the one who never heard at all (16:106). The warning is meant for the person reading it, because the route is available to anybody who has said he believes.

## Verse 3:87

> Their reward is that they will be condemned by Allah, the angels, and all of humanity

**A Reward That Is a Curse**

The verse states the compensation for the conduct described in the previous lines, and it uses the vocabulary of cursing. The condemned are cursed by God, by the angels, and by all of humanity, and the sentence names the three parties in ascending order of generality.

**What the Curse Means**

The condemnation described in this sentence was explained by the commentators as distance from God and removal from His mercy rather than as an act of verbal abuse. The same joint condemnation by God, the angels, and humanity is discussed in the sūrah of the Cow, where the state of those who die in rejection is described in the same terms (2:161). The verse before the reader therefore repeats a formula the Book uses for a condition rather than for an event, and the company of the condemning parties shows how complete the separation is: the created order itself is described as participating in the verdict.

**The Voices of the Hereafter**

The Book describes the people of the Hereafter speaking to one another in ways that belong to this theme. Communities entering the fire are said to curse their sister communities as each arrives (7:38), and those who had followed false leaders are told that on the Day of Resurrection they will disown one another and curse one another (29:25). The sentence before the reader names the outcome without the scene, and the passages that supply the scene show what the curse looks like when it is spoken aloud. The community that recites the verse is being told that a condition entered in secret in this life becomes public speech in the next.

---

## Verse 3:88

> They will be in Hell forever. Their punishment will not be lightened, nor will they be delayed ˹from it˺

**The Condition Described as Lasting**

The verse states two things about the people whose conduct the passage has been tracing: their condition will last, and the punishment will neither be eased nor postponed. The two clauses cover duration and intensity, and together they remove the two hopes that a person naturally entertains about a penalty.

The Book's descriptions of the Hereafter often pair a duration with a detail about the experience within it, and the pairing here is unusually bare. Nothing is said about the nature of the punishment, and nothing is said about a possible change in the arrangement. What the reader is given is a sentence about what will not happen: no lightening, no delay. The negative form is the Book's way of stating finality without describing the machinery.

**"Nor Will They Be Delayed"**

The Book is consistent in denying this across the descriptions of the Day. It states that no soul can be of help to another, that no ransom will be accepted, and that intercessors will not be heard in the cases it describes. The verse above gives the same denial in two words. The effect is to make the accounting instantaneous in a certain sense: the person arrives and the matter is settled, with no interval between the arrival and the consequence. The Book offers no schedule for the Day, and this verse tells the reader that the arrangements he may imagine for deferring its outcome do not exist.

---

## Verse 3:89

> As for those who repent afterwards and mend their ways, then surely Allah is All-Forgiving, Most Merciful

The verse closes with the declaration that God is Forgiving and Merciful, and the pairing of the two names recurs throughout the Book at the end of sentences about the acceptance of the repentant.

**The Report the Commentators Attached to the Verse**

The classical commentaries connect this verse with a Companion who had left the community and then wrote to his family asking whether a return was still possible, and they report that the verse was revealed in answer.

.

The report, whatever its status, describes the situation the verse is addressed to: a person who knows he has done something serious and wants to know whether the door is shut. The Book's answer is the sentence above, and the answer's form is itself instructive. It does not ask how far the person has gone, and it does not weigh the seriousness of the departure. It names the two conditions and the two divine names and leaves the matter there.

---

## Verse 3:90

> Indeed, those who disbelieve after having believed then increase in disbelief, their repentance will never be accepted. It is they who are astray

**A Second Case Stated After the First**

The previous verse admitted the repentance of those who turned back and repaired the damage. This verse names a group whose repentance will not be accepted, and the difference between the two cases is stated in what happens after the return.

These are people who disbelieved after having believed and then increased in their disbelief. The verse's picture is of a movement with a direction: the departure is not a single act that is then reconsidered but a course that continues. The Book states the refusal of their return in direct terms, and the sentence ends by naming them as the ones who are astray.

**When the Return Is Not Accepted**

The commentators offered a range of explanations for the statement that their repentance will not be accepted. One holds that the sentence refers to the repentance offered at the moment of death, when the Book says elsewhere that no one's return is accepted after the matter has become clear beyond the possibility of choice. Another holds that the return of such a person would not be sincere, since he has already known the truth and turned from it deliberately, and that the sentence describes what he is rather than what God does with him. A third holds that a person who returns and then leaves again has nullified the earlier return, and that the verse describes a state from which the earlier act has been emptied of its effect.

---

## Verse 3:91

> Indeed, if each of those who disbelieve then die as disbelievers were to offer a ransom of enough gold to fill the whole world, it would never be accepted from them. It is they who will suffer a painful punishment, and they will have no helpers

**A Ransom the Size of the World**

The verse describes people who disbelieve and die in that state, and it states that if one of them were to offer, as a ransom, gold enough to fill the whole earth, it would not be accepted from him. The image is built to be impossible, and its function is to state the finality of the matter in terms the reader can picture.

The Book uses ransom language at several points in its descriptions of the Day, and each time it denies the transaction. The sūrah of the Cow states that no ransom will be accepted from those who disbelieve, and the same denial recurs in the accounts of the last gathering. What this verse adds is the measure of the offer: not a sum beyond the reach of most people but a quantity that would suffice to fill the world, which removes the possibility of imagining an amount that would be enough.

---

The image of the earth filled with gold belongs to a small family of verses that all make the same point: on that Day wealth has no exchange rate. Sūrah al-Māʾidah says that even if the disbelievers possessed everything on earth twice over to ransom themselves, it would not be accepted (5:36). Sūrah al-Baqarah says of that Day that no intercession will be accepted, no ransom taken, and no help given (2:48). The verse here is the tail of a two-verse portrait — those who disbelieve after having believed and then die in that state — and its severity answers the size of what was abandoned. The commentator's point is not that God refuses large payments but that the Day is not a market: what is being asked for is precisely the thing that cannot be handed over, a record made clean.

## Verse 3:92

> You will never achieve righteousness until you donate some of what you cherish. And whatever you give is certainly well known to Allah

**A Condition Attached to Righteousness**

The verse states that righteousness will not be attained until a person gives away something he values, and it closes by saying that whatever is given, God knows it. The sentence pairs an unqualified demand with an assurance, and the two halves answer the two questions a person asks about charity: how much, and who is watching.

**The Companions and the Report of a Prized Land**

The commentaries connect the verse with a Companion in Madinah who owned a tract of land he valued above all his property and who wanted to give it in the way of God.

.

The report is a single instance of what the verse requires, and the commentators were careful not to restrict the verse to that instance. The Book's instruction about spending is not addressed to one owner of one garden; the verse's grammar is general, and the practice it demands is one that the community of the Prophet's companions is described as carrying out so frequently that a single story cannot exhaust it.

---

## Verse 3:93

> All food was lawful for the children of Israel, except what Israel made unlawful for himself before the Torah was revealed. Say, ˹O Prophet,˺ “Bring the Torah and read it, if your claims are true.”

**A Statement About Food and a Challenge**

The verse states that all food was lawful for the children of Israel except what Israel forbade for himself before the Torah was revealed, and then instructs the Prophet to call on his opponents to bring the Torah and recite it if their claims are true.

**Who Israel Was**

Israel is the name of Jacob, the son of Isaac, and the third of the figures the sūrah named in its creed several verses earlier. The verse's reference to him as Israel rather than Jacob is deliberate, since the people being addressed are the tribes descended from him and the name is the one their own tradition uses for their ancestor.

The verse's claim about him is a historical one, and its importance lies in what it excludes. If the food laws that the Israelites observed went back to Jacob's own lifetime and were recorded in the Torah, then they form part of a divine law given to him. The verse says instead that he forbade certain foods for himself, which means that the restriction originated with a person rather than with a revealed law, and that it predates the book that the claimants treat as the source of their practice.

---

The verse's challenge presupposes the difference between the food laws as they stood and the restriction Jacob accepted for himself, and the commentators identified the latter with the vow recorded in the biblical account of his wrestling, by which he gave up a particular food. The Qur'an's point is chronological and it is decisive: the restrictions the disputants cited came after the Torah, while Abraham and his household lived before it, so no law that postdates Abraham can define his religion. The same historical argument is made in Sūrah al-Anʿām, where the restriction of certain animals and fats is stated to have been imposed on those who became Jews as a recompense for their wrongdoing (6:146), and in Sūrah al-Nisāʾ with the same wording (4:160). The invitation to bring the Torah and read it is therefore a test of honesty about the record, which is why the section that follows turns from food to the mubāhala.

## Verse 3:94

> Then whoever still fabricates lies about Allah, they will be the ˹true˺ wrongdoers

**A Verdict on Those Who Invent**

The verse states that whoever fabricates a lie against God after the demonstration just given will be among the wrongdoers. The phrase "after that" ties the sentence to the challenge in the preceding verse, and the commentators read it as the point after which the truth about the dietary laws has been made clear.

The Book's phrase for the conduct it names is making something up and ascribing it to God, which is the most serious form of false speech in its vocabulary. What is at issue is not the inaccuracy of an interpretation but the invention of a divine command. The verse places that invention among the wrongs that damage the person who commits them and the people who accept them from him.

**The Evidence Still Standing in Their Hands**

The verse above describes the condition of a person who has such a document in his hands and continues to state the opposite of what it shows. The Book's accounts of the People of the Book repeatedly return to this point, which is that their own scriptures were the source of the case against them. The sūrah will later describe a group among them who twist the scripture with their tongues so that hearers take it for the Book itself, and the conduct described there is the same conduct this verse condemns, in a form that reaches the ear rather than the page.

---

## Verse 3:95

> Say, ˹O Prophet,˺ “Allah has declared the truth. So follow the Way of Abraham, the upright—who was not a polytheist.”

**A Declaration of Truthfulness**

The verse instructs the Prophet to say that God has spoken the truth, and the statement follows the demonstration about the food laws and the verdict on those who fabricated a claim about God. The declaration is short and its placement gives it weight.

**The Phrase the Verse Gave to Recitation**

The declaration that God has spoken true became a formula the community uses at the end of a recitation outside the prescribed prayers, and its origin in this passage is part of what the commentators discuss.
