Al-Ṭabarī explained the sentence as confirming, in this place, what had been said about Jacob and about the food that was made unlawful in the Torah before it was revealed, the matter that the preceding verses had disputed (3:93).

The confirmation therefore answers an argument rather than introducing a new subject. The Book has just told its opponents to bring the Torah and read it if their claims are true, and the sentence here states that the account the sūrah has given is the accurate one. The same confession appears in the sūrah of the Cow, where the community is told to follow the faith of Abraham the upright rather than the claims of the two older communities (2:135). The formula the readers repeat after recitation is so a sentence about a specific dispute that became a general affirmation of the Book.

---

## Verse 3:96

> Surely the first House ˹of worship˺ established for humanity is the one at Bakkah—a blessed sanctuary and a guide for ˹all˺ people

**Bakkah and Makkah**

Bakkah is the name used here, and the commentators identified it with Makkah, treating the difference between the two forms as a common alternation between the sounds m and b. Some held that Bakkah designated the ground on which the House itself stands while Makkah referred to the settlement around it.

**The First House and the Shrine at Jerusalem**

A well-known report gives the verse's priority a concrete shape. Abū Dharr asked the Prophet which mosque was built first on the earth, and was told it was the Sacred Mosque; asked which came next, he was told the Aqṣā Mosque; and when he asked how long separated them, he was told forty years (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī 3366).

**Guidance That Comes From a Place**

The phrase about guidance for all peoples raises a question that the commentators treated at length, which is how a location can be a source of guidance. Their answer was that the House guides by gathering, by directing worship, and by standing as a permanent witness to the worship of one God.

---

## Verse 3:97

> In it are clear signs and the standing-place of Abraham. Whoever enters it should be safe. Pilgrimage to this House is an obligation by Allah upon whoever is able among the people. And whoever disbelieves, then surely Allah is not in need of ˹any of His˺ creation

**The Sanctuary and Its Limits**

The statement that whoever enters the House is safe was read by some as describing the past, since the precinct around the Kaʿbah served in pre-Islamic Arabia as a place where a person fleeing pursuit could take refuge and not be seized.

The commentators then discussed whether the safety continued under the new community, and they reported a range of positions. One holds that the sanctuary no longer shields a person from prosecution and that offences committed in the sacred precinct are punishable like any other, though the punishment is not carried out within it. Another describes a strict regime under which a person taking refuge may not trade, speak, lodge, or be fed, which leaves him no option but to leave and face the case against him. A third holds that the verse is a command to the community to make the place secure, which people may fail to carry out. A further reading, reported from several authorities, takes the safety as deliverance from the Fire for the one who enters to perform the rites.

**The Inviolability of Makkah in the Prophet's Words**

The report of the conquest gives the city's standing in the Prophet's own words. Ibn ʿAbbās related that on the day Makkah was taken he said that God had made the town a sanctuary. That its thorn bushes were not to be cut, its game was not to be chased, and anything dropped there was not to be picked up except by someone announcing it publicly (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī 1587).

---

The verse carries the obligation in a single declarative sentence, and the jurists drew the terms of the duty out of it: pilgrimage to the House is required of every adult Muslim who is able, once in a lifetime, and "ability" was defined as the means of travel, the provision for the household left behind, security on the road, and bodily capacity. The Prophet ﷺ named the pilgrimage among the five pillars on which the religion is built (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī), and the Qur'an attached the rites to Abraham, commanding him to call all people to the pilgrimage so that they come on foot and on every lean camel from every distant path (22:27). The verse's placement is part of its argument: the obligation is stated in the middle of a passage about the signs in the House and the standing-place of Abraham, so the duty is tied to the sanctuary's nature — what is being visited is not a building but the place where the first house of worship was raised.

## Verse 3:98

> Say, ˹O Prophet,˺ “O People of the Book! Why do you deny the revelations of Allah, when Allah is a Witness to what you do?”

**A Rebuke Addressed by Name**

The verse instructs the Prophet to address the People of the Book directly and to ask them why they reject God's revelations. The address is courteous in form and severe in content, which is the Book's characteristic way of handling a community that holds a scripture.

**What Is Being Denied**

The phrase used for what they deny is the Book's ordinary term for God's signs, which covers both the verses of the revelation and the evidences that establish it. In this setting the two senses converge, since the revelation being rejected was itself claimed to be confirmed by what the People of the Book already had.

The commentators understood the denial as directed at the messenger and his message, which the Book treats as denial of the sender. That is why the question's form is a rebuke rather than a request for information: the answer is already known to the ones being asked, and the Book makes plain elsewhere that they recognise the messenger as they recognise their own children while a group among them hides what they know (2:146).

**The Witness Clause**

The sentence does not stop at the question. It adds that God is a witness to what they do, and the clause is what gives the verse its force. A person may deny something and be unknown to everyone but himself; the verse states that the record is being kept.

**Recognition and Concealment in the Sūrah**

The sūrah has been developing a distinction between not knowing and not acknowledging, and this verse depends on it. A community that had received the earlier scriptures was in a position to test the claim being made to it, and the Book's argument throughout the passage assumes the materials were in its hands.

That is why the Book's harshest descriptions are reserved for concealment. The sūrah says of a group among them that they mix the truth with falsehood and hide the truth knowingly (3:71), and another passage states that those who hide the clear proofs after they have been made clear for humanity will be condemned by God and by all who condemn (2:159). The verse above is the shortest form of the same charge, and it leaves the reader with a question that the accused cannot answer without either admitting the recognition or denying the record.

**Making the Way Crooked**

The same description of turning people from God's path appears in several sūrahs, where it is used of those who sought to make the way appear distorted (7:45; 11:19; 14:3). Al-Qurṭūbī and al-Ṭabarī explained that the obstruction described in this passage is not a physical hindrance but a try at misdirection, since the people in question would present the religion as false to create doubt among as many believers as possible, as al-Rāzī put it. A report attached to the passage describes them denying, when they were asked, that the Prophet's coming had been announced in their books (7:157). The sentence's closing clause, that God is a witness to what they do, answers the secrecy of the method rather than the method itself. The sūrah's next verse states what the community is to make of the try: the witness described in this passage is a people conscious of the Messenger among them and of truths they find in their own scriptures.

---

## Verse 3:99

> Say, “O People of the Book! Why do you turn the believers away from the Way of Allah—striving to make it ˹appear˺ crooked, while you are witnesses ˹to its truth˺? And Allah is never unaware of what you do.”

**From Denial to Obstruction**

The previous verse asked the People of the Book about their denial of God's revelations. This verse asks them about something else, which is what they do to the people who believe, and the shift from belief to conduct is the passage's subject.

The charge is that they turn believers away from the way of God and work to make that way look crooked, while they themselves are witnesses to its truth. The sentence therefore names an act, a purpose, and a state of knowledge, and the three together form the accusation. What is being described is not a private conviction but a campaign directed at the community of the believers.

**Turning Away and the Sense of the Verb**

The commentators discussed whether the turning described here is physical or figurative, and the weight of their explanation favours the second. The Book does not accuse these people of standing in the road; it accuses them of misdirection, of presenting the way as something other than it is so that those who hear them begin to doubt.

---

The phrase the verse uses — "turning away from the Way of Allah and striving to make it appear crooked" — is one the Qur'an repeats of this type in three other places: those who hinder others from God's path, striving to make it appear crooked (7:45; 11:19), and those who prefer this life and hinder from His way and seek to make it crooked, who have gone far astray (14:3). The detail that gives the charge its force is the one the verse adds: "while you are witnesses to its truth". The objection is not to their ignorance but to their obstruction of others after knowledge had reached them, which is the offence the chapter charges to the custodians of the Book, and which the next verses take up as the reason their testimony cannot be accepted against the Prophet.

## Verse 3:100

> O believers! If you were to yield to a group of those who were given the Scripture, they would turn you back from belief to disbelief

**What "Yielding" Produces**

The sentence states a consequence rather than an accusation. If the believers were to follow the direction of that group, they would find themselves turned back from their belief, which describes a process with stages rather than a single decision.

**"A Group" and the Refusal to Generalise**

The word used for those in view is one that means a section or a party, not an entire people. The choice matches the sūrah's care elsewhere in the same passage, where it states that the People of the Book are not all alike and that some among them are upright, reciting the revelations of God through the night and prostrating themselves (3:113).

The Book also says in this sūrah that some among them are faithful while most are rebellious (3:110). Near its end it praises those who believe in God, in what was revealed to the believers, and in what was revealed to them, and who do not trade away God's revelations for a small gain (3:199). A warning directed at a group is therefore consistent with the sūrah's own distinctions, and the reader is protected from turning a judgment about conduct into a verdict about a people.

The sūrah states the same outcome near its end in a warning addressed to the same community, telling the believers that if they obey the disbelievers they will be dragged back to disbelief and become losers (3:149). The image of dragging in that verse matches the sense of the sentence here, where the result is reached by following rather than by arguing. A community is not talked out of its faith in one exchange; it is guided away from it in a series of small compliances, and the verse names the end of the road so that the first step can be recognised for what it is.

**Envy as the Plain Motive**

The Book supplies the motive in another passage, where it says of many among the People of the Book that they wish they could turn the believers back to disbelief because of their envy, after the truth has been made clear to them (2:109). The wording is direct about what drives the wish, and it removes the possibility of mistaking the behaviour for a honest disagreement.

**The Remedy the Sūrah Prescribes**

The Book's answer to the danger of being drawn apart is stated a few verses later in this sūrah, where the believers are told to hold firmly to the rope of God all together and not to be divided. To remember the favour by which they were enemies and then became brothers (3:103).

---

## Verse 3:101

> How can you disbelieve when Allah’s revelations are recited to you and His Messenger is in your midst? Whoever holds firmly to Allah is surely guided to the Straight Path

**A Question That Names Two Proofs**

The verse asks how disbelief is possible when two things are present: the revelations of God being recited and the Messenger in the community's midst. The question is not addressed to strangers; it follows a warning to the believers about the pressure they would face.

**The Recitation as Evidence in Itself**

The Book's challenge to its doubters rests on the character of the recitation, not only on its content. Three separate passages invite the sceptics to produce a comparable composition, and one of them escalates the demand through stages, ending with the observation that even if humanity and the jinn combined their efforts they could not produce its like (17:88).

**Holding Fast to God**

The verse's second sentence states the alternative to doubt: whoever holds firmly to God is guided to a straight path. The Arabic image is of a person gripping something so as not to fall, and the commentators identified what is to be gripped in two ways.

---

The charge is not that the audience lacks the truth but that it is present twice over, in the recitation and in the person of the Messenger, which is why the question is put as an astonishment rather than an instruction. The verse that follows gives the remedy in the same terms: hold firmly to Allah, and He will guide you to a straight path (3:103). The sūrah has described the same favour already — God raised a messenger from among them to recite the revelations, purify them and teach them the Book and wisdom (3:164) — so this verse addresses people who have heard the recitation with their own ears in the company of the one who brought it. The commentators read the two proofs as standing together: the text and the living witness, neither of which was available to earlier communities in that form.

## Verse 3:102

> O believers! Be mindful of Allah in the way He deserves, and do not die except in ˹a state of full˺ submission ˹to Him˺

**A Command With a Measure Built In**

The verse calls on the believers to be mindful of God in the way He deserves, and then tells them not to die except in a state of submission. The first command has a measure attached to it, and the measure is the word that means a right or what is due.

Al-Ṭabarī explained the phrase as describing a mindfulness in which God is obeyed and not defied, remembered and not forgotten, thanked and not denied. The formulation answers the question a person naturally asks when told to give God what He deserves, since the answer is not a quantity but a way of living. The same root appears elsewhere in the Book in the rebuke that people have not shown God His proper reverence, a criticism attached to those who denied that He had revealed anything to a human being (6:91).

**The Command That Seemed Too Heavy**

The community's first reaction to the verse is part of its history. Reports record that the Companions found the command difficult and were troubled by it, since no one could claim to have given God everything He deserves. Some then suggested that the command was lightened by another verse that tells the believers to be mindful of God as far as they are able (64:16).

The reasoning is not that the earlier command was cancelled but that the two verses explain each other. The same Book states that God does not require of any soul more than it can bear (2:286), so a command to give God His due cannot mean a demand beyond human capacity. Al-Rāzī drew the conclusion directly: to be mindful of God as He deserves is to be mindful of Him to the extent of one's ability, because the Book has ruled out the impossible.

**The Two Commands Belong Together**

The Book uses the same combination in the advice Abraham and Jacob gave their children, where the dying clause appears in almost identical wording after the statement that God has chosen the faith for them (2:132). In both passages, the point is not to arrange a particular death but to live in a way that keeps the ending what it should be. A person has no power over the moment or the manner of his death, and the command is directed at the part he does control.

---

## Verse 3:103

> And hold firmly to the rope of Allah and do not be divided. Remember Allah’s favour upon you when you were enemies, then He united your hearts, so you—by His grace—became brothers. And you were at the brink of a fiery pit and He saved you from it. This is how Allah makes His revelations clear to you, so that you may be ˹rightly˺ guided

**What the Rope of God Means**

The verse commands the believers to hold firmly to the rope of God together and not to be divided, and then reminds them of what God had done for them. The image of the rope has been explained in several ways, and the explanations are not far apart.

The community of believers is the reading many commentators preferred, since the sentence goes on to forbid division. Others took the rope to be the Book itself, and some traced the image to a report in which the Qur'an is described as a rope stretching between heaven and earth. A further reading, noted by Ibn Kathīr, understands the rope as a covenant or pact, because the same word appears later in this sūrah for the treaties by which a people may be protected.

**Remembering What God Did**

The enmity refers to the tribal wars among the Arabs in the time before Islam. More particularly to the long feud between the two Madinan tribes of Aws and Khazraj, who had fought each other for years and were separated only by the arrival of the Prophet and the faith he brought. The Book states elsewhere that the joining of hearts was God's work and could not have been bought with any amount of wealth (8:63), which makes the memory a statement about divine action rather than about diplomacy.

**The Book's Standing Command Against Division**

The instruction not to be divided is repeated in the Book in ways that show how the theme is handled. The command was given before to Noah, Abraham, Moses, and Jesus, together with the instruction to uphold the faith and make no divisions in it (42:13). Another passage states that those who were given the Scripture did not split into sects out of mutual envy until knowledge came to them (42:14).

That order — knowledge first, then division — is the pattern the Book keeps returning to, and it appears again where the People of the Book are said to have become divided only after the clear proof came to them (98:4). The sūrah's own version of the command follows the same logic: the community has received what it received, and the warning is against coming apart in the presence of the truth rather than in ignorance of it.

**Brotherhood as the Fruit of the Favour**

The verse's reminder ends with the community described as brothers, and the Book elsewhere builds that description into a rule. The believers are described as a single brotherhood, and the instruction that follows is to make peace between brothers, with mindfulness of God named as the condition for receiving mercy (49:10).

---

## Verse 3:104

> Let there be a group among you who call ˹others˺ to goodness, encourage what is good, and forbid what is evil—it is they who will be successful

**The Report That Sets Out How It Is Done**

A well-known report related from Abū Saʿīd al-Khudrī orders that a person who sees something abominable should correct it by his hand. Where he lacks the strength for that, by his tongue, and where he lacks the strength even for that, then in his heart — which the report calls the least of faith. The narration is in Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim (49a) and is among the most quoted texts on this subject.

**Who the Group Is**

The verse's phrase about a group has been read in two ways. On one reading it describes a distinct segment of the community that takes the task upon itself; on another, it is a statement of what the community should be, so that the whole body is addressed rather than a portion of it.

The case for a segment rests on the observation that not everyone can carry out the practice, since it requires knowledge of what is right and the standing to speak about it, and since the community is not composed only of those who can influence the conduct of others. The case for the wider reading rests on the fact that the community is described elsewhere in the sūrah as encouraging what is good and forbidding what is wrong (3:110). The two positions meet when the duty is understood as binding on the community as a whole and satisfied when a qualified part of it acts.

**The Practice and the People Who Field It**

Because the practice requires the ability to distinguish what is right from what is wrong, the commentators connected it with knowledge. A person who does not know what the law requires may end up forbidding what God has permitted, and the scholars therefore held that the obligation rests most heavily on those equipped to judge.

The Book's passage about the distribution of religious learning belongs to this discussion, since it describes a party from each group going out to gain understanding and then returning to warn their people (9:122). The two verses describe one system: expertise is developed in some and used for the benefit of all, and the community is expected to arrange itself so that the function does not lapse.

---

## Verse 3:105

> And do not be like those who split ˹into sects˺ and differed after clear proofs had come to them. It is they who will suffer a tremendous punishment

**A Command Framed as a Comparison**

The verse tells the believers not to be like those who divided and differed after clear proofs had reached them, and it ends by stating that such people will receive a severe punishment. The command is expressed through a comparison rather than a prohibition, and the form gives the instruction its shape.

What is forbidden is not division in the abstract but the imitation of a specific history. The Book uses this method often: it holds up a community's conduct and tells its readers to avoid resembling it. That form of warning is stronger than a rule because it supplies the example, and the sūrah has already used it twice in this passage, once with the people of an earlier nation and once with the fate of those who disbelieve.

**Who Divided and Differed**

The commentators identified the people in view as the communities that had received earlier scriptures. The Book describes the pattern in several places with the same details. In the sūrah of the Cow it states that no one disputed the Scriptures except the very people who had received them, and that the dispute began after the clear proofs had come to them, out of jealousy (2:213).

The same account appears in this sūrah earlier, where it says that those who were given the Scripture did not dispute among themselves out of mutual envy until knowledge came to them (3:19). The Book is consistent about the sequence: the proof arrived, and the division followed it rather than preceding it. That is what makes the conduct the verse warns against so grave, since it is not the disagreement of people still searching.

**Division as a Category in the Book**

The theme recurs in the Book with a fixed vocabulary. One passage states that those who divided their faith and split into sects have nothing to do with the Prophet, and that their affair rests with God, who will inform them of what they did (6:159). Another describes the same people, with each group rejoicing in what it happens to hold (30:32).

Alongside those descriptions stands the command: the Book's charge to uphold the faith and make no divisions in it was given to Noah, Abraham, Moses, and Jesus (42:13). The Book therefore treats unity as a requirement of the religion itself and division as a departure from it, and the passage in this sūrah belongs to that treatment rather than to a general preference for agreement.

---

## Verse 3:106

> On that Day some faces will be bright while others gloomy. To the gloomy-faced it will be said, “Did you disbelieve after having believed? So taste the punishment for your disbelief.”

**A Day That Shows on the Face**

The verse describes the Day of Judgment as the day when some faces become bright and others dark, and it reports what is said to the second group. The image is drawn from everyday experience, where the face carries news of the inner state before any word is spoken.

Arabic uses the darkening of the face for shame, distress, and suppressed anger, and the Book employs the phrase in this world before it applies it to the next. One passage describes a man whose face grows gloomy when he is given news of the daughter he attributes to God, and who suppresses his rage as he does so (43:17). The verse before the reader transfers that familiar reaction to the day when nothing is concealed.

**The Faces That Brighten**

The bright face is the Book's image for joy, relief, and approval, and the spiritual sense the commentators added is that the light on those faces is the light of faith made visible. The Book describes one group of people as receiving the finest reward and even more, and explains that neither gloom nor disgrace will cover their faces (10:26).

**The Faces That Darken**

The dark face belongs to the people the Book elsewhere describes as liars against God. One passage states that on the Day of Judgment those who lied about God will be seen with their faces gloomy, and then asks whether the Fire is not a fitting home for the arrogant (39:60).

**"Did You Disbelieve After Having Believed?"**

The question is put to people who had once accepted the message, and the commentators identified them in several ways. The most natural identification, on the sūrah's own argument, is the people the previous verses described as having been warned about being drawn away from their belief, since the warning and the outcome belong to the same passage.

Others read the verse as addressed to those who broke the primordial acknowledgement of God's lordship, the moment the Book describes when humanity testified that God is its Lord (7:172). A further reading applies it to hypocrites, whose attestation of faith was contradicted by their actions. The three readings converge on the phrase the verse uses, which names faith as something the people had and then lost.

---

## Verse 3:107

> As for the bright-faced, they will be in Allah’s mercy, where they will remain forever

**Mercy Described as a Place**

The verse states that the people with bright faces will be in the mercy of God, and the phrasing treats mercy as an environment rather than as a transaction. What they enter is not a payment but a condition in which they remain.

The Book uses this construction in several places to describe the final state of the blessed. The word for mercy in these passages covers the kindness that surrounds a person and the provision that keeps him, so that the verse does not say simply that God was merciful to them once; it places them inside the mercy and leaves them there. The sūrah's earlier verses already used the same vocabulary, when the well-grounded asked for mercy from God's presence while asking that their hearts be kept, and the two passages belong to one body of language.

**Permanence in the Book's Descriptions of Reward**

The verse states that the people described will remain where they are, and the formula of permanence recurs throughout the Book's descriptions of Paradise. The same sūrah uses it when it describes the reward of those who do good, who receive forgiveness and gardens with rivers flowing beneath them, staying there forever (3:136).

---

## Verse 3:108

> These are Allah’s revelations We recite to you ˹O Prophet˺ in truth. And Allah desires no injustice to ˹His˺ creation

**The Revelations Described as Recited**

The verse states that what has been set out in the preceding passage consists of God's revelations recited in truth, and it adds that God desires no injustice for any of His creation. Both sentences are about the message itself and about the one who sent it.

The formula used here appears elsewhere in the Book in nearly identical words. One passage states that these are God's revelations recited in truth and then tells the Prophet that he is truly one of the messengers (2:252). The pairing of the two statements in that verse shows what the formula does: it identifies the material as revelation and locates the one reciting it within the sequence of those sent by God.

**The Statement About Injustice**

The Arabic word translated as injustice describes the placing of something outside its proper place. The verse's sentence denies that God desires such a thing for any of the worlds, and the commentators offered two explanations that fit the wording.

---

The two sentences are the sūrah's standard of judgement, and the chapter sets it twice, once here and once at the close of the same discussion: this treatment is the reward of what your hands have done, and God is never unjust to His creation (3:182). The same pair of statements closes other passages of the Book: whoever does good, it is to their own benefit, and whoever does evil, it is to their own loss; your Lord is never unjust to His servants (41:46), and the words "My Word cannot be changed, nor am I unjust to My creation" (50:29). The repetition is the doctrine: nothing that comes to a person on that Day is arbitrary. The commentators note that the verse's mention of creation in general, rather than of the believers alone, is what gives the principle its reach — the warning to enemies and the promise to the faithful are two applications of one justice.

## Verse 3:109

> To Allah ˹alone˺ belongs whatever is in the heavens and whatever is on the earth. And to Allah ˹all˺ matters will be returned ˹for judgment˺

**Ownership and the Return of Matters**

The verse states that everything in the heavens and the earth belongs to God, and that all matters will be returned to Him for judgment. The two clauses are a statement of possession followed by a statement of jurisdiction.

The Book pairs them often. One passage states that whatever is in the heavens and the earth belongs to God, and that God is the Self-Sufficient and Praiseworthy (31:26), which makes the point that the ownership of everything carries no benefit for the owner and no limitation on Him. Another passage states that God will certainly gather humanity on a day about which there is no doubt, and then asks whose word could be more truthful than God's (4:87). The verse before the reader stands in that sequence of declarations.

**Why the Formula Settles Disputes**

The sentence about ownership is the Book's standard reply to arguments about authority. Throughout this sūrah, the disputes have concerned lineage, scripture, and standing, with each party claiming a position that the others contested.

The reply is not to adjudicate the claims one by one but to state who owns the terms. If everything belongs to God, then no community owns a claim on Him, and no lineage carries a right against Him. The sūrah has already used the same reasoning when it stated that God is never unaware of what people do and when it described Him as witness over the conduct of the People of the Book, and the sentence here generalises the point to the whole of creation.

**"All Matters Are Returned"**

The clause about matters being returned appears in several contexts in the Book, and one of them is a passage about the battlefield, where the return of matters to God is given as the reason the outcome of a battle was arranged as it was (8:44).

---

## Verse 3:110

> You are the best community ever raised for humanity—you encourage good, forbid evil, and believe in Allah. Had the People of the Book believed, it would have been better for them. Some of them are faithful, but most are rebellious

**The Best Community Raised for Humanity**

The verse addresses the believers as the best community brought forth for humanity, and then names the practices that define the description: encouraging what is good, forbidding what is wrong, and believing in God. The rank is stated first and the content of it immediately after.

The same title appears in the sūrah of the Cow, where the community is described as made upright so that it may bear witness over humanity (2:143).

**The Witness the Community Carries**

The role attached to the community in the parallel passage is that of a witness over humanity, with the messenger a witness over it (2:143). That role explains why the description of the community's practices follows its title, since a witness must be visible and its conduct is part of what it witnesses to.

**"Some of Them Are Faithful, Most Are Rebellious"**

The word rendered as rebellious is used in the Book for those who abandon an obligation or step outside a limit, and the commentators noted that it is contrasted here with belief rather than with disbelief.

**Believers Named Among Them**

The verse's statement that some among them are faithful is confirmed elsewhere in the sūrah. A later passage says that they are not all alike and describes an upright group among the People of the Book who recite God's revelations through the night and prostrate themselves (3:113). The same sūrah's closing passages speak of those among them who believe in God, in what has been revealed to the believers, and in what was revealed to them (3:199).

The Book's own categories therefore prevent its readers from converting a judgment about majority conduct into a verdict on a whole people, and the tradition records that individuals of the People of the Book accepted the message and were counted among the community's most respected members.

**The Readings of the Verb and What They Change**

The verb in the sentence can be read as either present or past, and al-Rāzī set out what follows from each. Read in the past, the words describe something already written: the community was named the best of peoples in the Preserved Tablet, the register the Book mentions at the end of the sūrah of the Constellations, *“˹recorded˺ in a Preserved Tablet”* (85:22). The sentence then calls on the community to live up to what it was named. Read as addressed to the people of the community once their lives are over, the words describe them in the garden, looking back at what they were in the world.

---

## Verse 3:111

> They can never inflict harm on you, except a little annoyance. But if they meet you in battle, they will flee and they will have no helpers

The verse tells the believers that the people in question cannot harm them beyond a limited irritation, and that if it comes to fighting they will retreat and find no help. The sentence sets a ceiling on what the community has to fear.

The claim is carefully measured.

**The Annoyance the Verse Names**

The word translated as annoyance covers insult, mockery, and the kind of speech that wears a community down without defeating it. The commentators included blasphemous claims about God among the things the term describes.

The sūrah returns to this theme later when it tells the believers that their patience and mindfulness will prevent the schemes of their opponents from harming them at all (3:120). The two verses fit together: one describes the category of harm that remains possible, and the other describes the conduct that neutralises even that. The Book's habit is to name the residual risk rather than to promise absolute immunity, which keeps the urging to patience meaningful.

**The Prediction About Fighting**

The second sentence of the verse states that if the opponents meet the believers in battle, they will turn and flee, and that they will have no helpers. The wording is a prediction about armed confrontation, and it matches another passage in the Book that says the same people would certainly flee if they were fought, leaving the disbelievers with no help (59:12).

**What It Means to Have No Helpers**

The phrase about having no helpers is one of the Book's fixed descriptions for those who are cut off, and it appears in several contexts. It is used of people whose deeds are wasted in this world and the next (3:22), of those subjected to punishment in both lives (3:56), and of those whom God has condemned, for whom no helper exists (4:52).

**The Distinction Between Harm to Persons and Harm to the Message**

The verse's careful limit points to a distinction the Book draws throughout its accounts of persecution. What a hostile group can do to individuals is real and is described plainly, and what it can do to the message is nothing.

That distinction explains the calm of the verse. The community is not told that its people will not suffer; the sūrah's later passage describes the losses at the battle of Uḥud without softening them. What is told is that the opposition cannot make the message fail, and the sentence that they will flee states the outcome of the try in the plainest terms.

---

## Verse 3:112

> They will be stricken with disgrace wherever they go, unless they are protected by a covenant with Allah or a treaty with the people. They have invited the displeasure of Allah and have been branded with misery for rejecting Allah’s revelations and murdering ˹His˺ prophets unjustly. This is ˹a fair reward˺ for their disobedience and violations

**A Condition Described as Lasting Abasement**

The verse states that the people in view will be struck with humiliation wherever they are found, unless they are protected by a covenant from God or a treaty with people, and it explains the condition by naming their offences. The sentence is about the circumstances in which such a community lives.

The Book describes the same condition in an earlier passage about the Israelites, where they are said to have been struck with disgrace and misery for rejecting God's signs and unjustly killing the prophets (2:61). The wording of the two verses is close, and the repetition shows that the Book treats the state it describes as the working out of a long-standing pattern rather than as an isolated event.

**The Offences the Verse Names**

The verse gives two reasons for the condition: rejecting God's signs, and killing the prophets without right. Both are acts committed by communities that had received messengers, and the Book attaches them to the history of the Israelites in particular.

---

The two exceptions the verse names are the two legal grounds a community of non-believers could hold in the Muslim polity, and the jurists read them precisely: a covenant with Allah means the acceptance of Islam, and a treaty with the people means the agreement by which a non-Muslim community lives under protection — the dhimmah, whose terms the Qur'an assumes when it speaks of the covenant of protection (9:29). The verse's charge is laid out in sequence: they killed the prophets without right, and they transgressed, which is the pattern the sūrah has traced from the beginning of the people's history. The condition described is so not a fate imposed on a people but the consequence of what they have done, and the exception is left open: humiliation is the rule, but a treaty is a door out of it, as the constitution of the community in Madinah — the agreement with the Jewish tribes — itself demonstrated in practice.

## Verse 3:113

> Yet they are not all alike: there are some among the People of the Book who are upright, who recite Allah’s revelations throughout the night, prostrating ˹in prayer˺

**The Qualification That Prevents a Blanket Judgment**

The verse begins by stating that the People of the Book are not all alike, and it then describes one part of them: an upright community that recites God's revelations through the night while prostrating themselves. The sentence follows the previous verse's account of a people struck with humiliation.

**What the Word for Upright Carries**

The word describing the group means standing, and it carries the senses of firmness, justice, and the standing posture of prayer. Al-Ṭabarī noted its association with fairness, and others explained the upright as those who maintain the limits God has set and do not distort what they hold.

**Night Worship in the Book's Vocabulary**

The Book returns often to the practice of praying in the dark. One passage describes people who abandon their beds to call on their Lord in hope and fear and to give from what they have been provided (32:16). Another places among the righteous those who are patient, truthful, obedient, and who seek forgiveness before dawn (3:17).

This sūrah itself contains the instruction to rise in the last part of the night for additional prayer (17:79), and the command to recite the Book in measured tones comes in the same context. The group described in the verse under discussion is therefore performing a practice the Book commends to its own community, and the passage is acknowledging the practice wherever it is found.

**Why the Night Is Named**

The night carries a significance in these passages beyond the hour. Prayer in the dark is hidden from people, and the Book's descriptions of it repeatedly pair hope with fear, which is the state of a person alone with his Lord.

**The Reports About the Occasion**

The commentaries record several occasions for the verse's revelation. One describes the believers waiting in the mosque for the Prophet to lead the night prayer, and his telling them that no people from any religion were remembering God at that hour besides them. Other reports name groups from Najrān, from Abyssinia, and from Byzantine territory who followed the religion of Jesus and affirmed the truth of the message. A further report describes a people who prayed between sunset and the end of twilight.

.

---

## Verse 3:114

> They believe in Allah and the Last Day, encourage good and forbid evil, and race with one another in doing good. They are ˹truly˺ among the righteous

The verse continues the description of the group named in the previous verse and gives four elements: belief in God and the Last Day, encouraging what is good, forbidding what is wrong, and competing with one another in good deeds.

**Why Belief Is Paired With the Last Day**

The pairing of God and the Last Day is the Book's shortest creed and one of its most frequent formulas. When the Book wants to describe belief in a way that covers both the present and the future, it names these two.

The reason is the difference the two make to conduct. Belief in God establishes who is being obeyed; belief in the Last Day establishes that obedience has a destination and that no deed is lost. The Book uses the pair when it speaks about the reward of those who do good, stating that whoever believes in God and the Last Day and acts rightly will have his reward with his Lord, without fear or grief (2:62). A verse in the sūrah of the Table repeats the same formula in the same terms (5:69).

**Practices the Sūrah Has Already Required**

The two practices in the middle of the list are the ones the sūrah commanded the believers to maintain. It told them to have a group that calls others to good, that encourages what is right and forbids what is wrong (3:104), and later it described what this community should be in the same words (3:110).

The vocabulary is not community-specific. The Book applies it to the Muslim community as its own obligation and to the upright among the People of the Book as their description, which shows that the terms name conduct rather than membership. What the sūrah presents here is the recognition of the same conduct in another community, and the recognition is stated as a fact rather than as a concession.

**"Racing With One Another in Doing Good"**

The final element of the description is the language of competition. The Book uses the same verb for the conduct of the righteous in another passage, where it says of Zachariah's family that they used to race one another in good deeds, calling on God in hope and fear and humbling themselves before Him (21:90).

**Who These Verses Describe**

The identification of the people praised in this passage depends on two other decisions the commentators had to make. The first is who the upright community of the previous verse is, and the second is how the statement in the earlier verse that some of the People of the Book are believers is to be taken. On the reading that the upright community consists of the People of the Book, the sentence before the reader continues the same description; on the reading that it refers to the Muslims, the passage moves from the community to the group it has been discussing. The content of the passage resembles the earlier statements about those who believe in God and the Last Day and do right (2:62; 5:69), and the same questions about identity arise in each place. What the verse names is a set of qualities rather than a group, and the qualities are those the sūrah has been commending throughout.

---

## Verse 3:115

> They will never be denied the reward for any good they have done. And Allah has ˹perfect˺ knowledge of those mindful ˹of Him˺

**A Promise That No Good Is Lost**

The verse states that whatever good such people do will not be denied to them, and it adds that God has full knowledge of those who are mindful of Him. It closes the three-verse description of the upright group with an assurance about their reward.

The assurance is stated in the strongest form the language provides, since the verb of denial carries the sense of refusing something that is due. The Book uses the same idea in other places when it assures people that their charity and their vows are known to God, and that nothing given has gone unrecorded (2:270). The sentence in this verse is the shortest version of that reassurance.

**What the Word for Denial Implies**

The word translated as denied shares its root with the Book's word for ingratitude, which is the refusal to acknowledge a benefit that has been received. Applying that word to the reward means that the good done by such people will not be treated as if it had never happened.

---

## Verse 3:116

> Indeed, neither the wealth nor children of the disbelievers will be of any benefit to them against Allah. It is they who will be the residents of the Fire. They will be there forever

**What Lies Between the Two Statements**

The distance between the two sentences is filled with the sūrah's discussion of communities that received revelations, disputed about their ancestors, contracted covenants, and then divided. Some of that material concerned the People of the Book and some concerned the believers, and all of it concerned people who had things they trusted.

Read with that in mind, the repeated sentence gains a specific sense. The wealth and children it names are not only money and offspring; the passage has been treating standing, lineage, and scripture as possessions of the same kind. What the verse denies is that any of them interposes between a person and his Lord, and the intervening argument has supplied the evidence for the denial.

**The Delusion the Book Describes**

The Book elsewhere gives the psychology of the condition. One passage describes a person who turns away when the revelations are recited to him, and it explains that he does so because he has been given wealth and children (68:14). Another tells the Prophet not to be impressed by the wealth and children of such people, because God's purpose with those gifts is a trial that ends with their death in disbelief (9:85).

**Why Children Are Named With Wealth**

The two items appear together throughout the Book because they cover both of a person's horizons. Wealth is what he has, and children are what continues him, and together they represent the whole of what he might want to secure.

The Book's verdict on the pair is not a condemnation of either. The same Book describes them as the adornment of the present life (18:46) and states that they are a test in which many people are tried (64:15). What is denied is their use as a defence: a person may love his children and have his wealth and still answer for his conduct, and the sentence treats the belief that these things can stand in his place as the error that ends badly.

**Residents of the Fire, and the Difference From the Earlier Wording**

The earlier statement described such people as the fuel of the Fire, which named what they become. This verse calls them its residents and states that they will remain there, which names where they live and for how long.

---

## Verse 3:117

> The good they do in this worldly life is like the harvest of an evil people struck by a bitter wind, destroying it ˹completely˺. Allah never wronged them, but they wronged themselves

**A Parable About Outlay Without Return**

The verse compares what such people spend in this life to a crop destroyed by a bitter wind, and it ends by stating that God did not wrong them and that they wronged themselves. The image is agricultural, and its subject is labour that produces nothing.

The sentence is addressed to people whose generosity was part of their standing, and the parable does not deny that the labour occurred. A harvest is the result of work, and the verse describes the destruction of something that had grown. What it denies is the survival of the result, and the denial is stated with an image that any farmer in the audience would have recognised.

**Wind in the Book's Picture of Effort**

The Book uses wind in several passages to describe the fate of deeds, and the variations on the image carry different emphases. One passage describes a garden with palms, vines, and every kind of fruit, whose owner grows old with young dependents while a fire-laden whirlwind strikes and burns the whole of it (2:266). Another compares the deeds of those who disbelieve to ashes blown away by a fierce wind on a stormy day, so that nothing they earned remains available to them (14:18).

The same Book describes wind elsewhere as the bearer of good news ahead of rain and as a means by which provision reaches people. The two uses belong to a single doctrine: the wind does not discriminate, and what it destroys is destroyed because of where it blew, not because wind is cruel.

---

## Verse 3:118

> O believers! Do not associate closely with others who would not miss a chance to harm you. Their only desire is to see you suffer. Their prejudice has become evident from what they say—and what their hearts hide is far worse. We have made Our revelations clear to you, if only you understood

**The Advice the Commentators Took From It**

The classical commentaries read the verse as a caution about relying on outsiders in matters where a disclosure could harm the community. Ibn Kathīr and al-Qurṭūbī mention contracts, correspondence, and the conduct of important affairs, and al-Qurṭūbī adds a complaint from his own lifetime about the employment of Jews and Christians as scribes and secretaries.

**"They Would Not Spare You Any Corruption"**

The second sentence of the verse states that such people will not miss a chance to harm, and their wish, the verse says, is that the believers should suffer. The wording describes a disposition rather than an incident.

The Book attaches the same disposition to the hypocrites elsewhere, whose speech changes with their company: they tell the believers that they believe, and when they are alone with their own people they say that they were only mocking (2:14). The verse before the reader assumes that condition as the reason for its instruction and describes what it produces when the opportunity arises.

**Hatred That Has Reached the Tongue**

The Book uses the same approach in the sūrah of the Table, where it describes people who say that they believe with their tongues while their hearts are in disbelief (5:41). The point in both places is that a person's public statements are the least of what he carries, and the instruction in this verse is drawn from that fact: the community is being told what to expect from what it can already hear.

**What the Verse Is Warning Against**
The verse tells the believers not to take as close confidants people outside their own community, since such people spare no effort to corrupt them and are pleased at what troubles them. The Book does not name a group, and the commentators read it in the context of the years in Madinah, when the community was living among groups who had treaties with it and interests against it. The verse ends with a statement about the hearts of the men who feel this way, and it says that the signs have been made clear to the community if it will understand them. The Sūrah of the Table gives the same instruction with a warning about the two earlier communities (5:57), and the Sūrah of the Women names the same caution about a group that mixed with the believers and worked against them (4:139).
**What the Commentators Took From It**
The older commentaries drew a rule about the difference between dealing with people equitably and taking them into the inner counsels of the community. The sūrah commands the believers in another place to deal kindly and justly with those who are not fighting them (60:8), and the verse in front of us restricts the sharing of confidential matters. The jurists applied the rule to contracts, to advising, and to the places where a disclosure could damage the community, and they held that the prohibition is about the trust rather than about the person.
**What Comes Next in the Sūrah**
The verse after this one gives the community the test of the relationship in two signs: the grief of these people at the good that comes to the believers and their joy at the harm. The sūrah then states the remedy, which is patience and God-consciousness, and the passage belongs to the same section as the account of Uḥud. The Book gives the community the diagnosis before the lesson, and it leaves the judgement of intention to the One who knows what the hearts conceal, in the words the verse uses at the end.

---

## Verse 3:119

> Here you are! You love them but they do not love you, and you believe in all Scriptures. When they meet you they say, “We believe.” But when alone, they bite their fingertips in rage. Say, ˹O Prophet,˺ “˹May you˺ die of your rage!” Surely Allah knows best what is ˹hidden˺ in the heart

**Love That Is Not Returned**

The verse addresses the believers as people who love those who do not love them back, and the commentators set out several ways of understanding the love in view. It could mean the affection that believers are expected to hold for all people as fellow creatures of God; it could mean the wish that others should be guided, which is a form of good will; or it could mean that the believers do not wish their opponents to fall into difficulty.

**Biting the Fingertips in Rage**

The same double speech appears elsewhere in the Book, where it is said of such people that they tell the believers they believe and then tell their own associates that they were only mocking (2:14). The verse before the reader puts the reaction in the body rather than in words, and the physical image conveys a rage that is visible to the onlookers and useless to the one who feels it.

---

The verse belongs to a passage warning the believers against taking the disaffected of Madinah into their confidence: "do not associate closely with others who would not miss a chance to harm you" (3:118). What this verse adds is the asymmetry that makes the demand painful: the believers are told they love them — the ordinary goodwill owed to neighbours and kin — while the others do not return it. The rage described at the end, biting the fingertips, is the chapter's picture of a resentment that cannot be acted on openly, and the commentators noted that the verse does not tell the believers to answer hate with hate. The instruction that follows is to hold to what the sūrah has already prescribed, and the next verse states the rule plainly: whatever good the believers do cannot harm them, and any harm their enemies intend turns back on those who intend it (3:120).

## Verse 3:120

> When you ˹believers˺ are touched with good, they grieve; but when you are afflicted with evil, they rejoice. ˹Yet,˺ if you are patient and mindful ˹of Allah˺, their schemes will not harm you in the least. Surely Allah is Fully Aware of what they do

**The Portrait of a Settled Enmity**
The verse reports that when good comes to the believers their opponents grieve, and when harm comes to them they rejoice, and it then gives the community the condition under which such reactions will not harm it. The pairing of reactions is the summary of everything the passage has been describing.
**Moses' Plea in the Same Key**
The Book records the same sentiment in the words of Moses, who pleaded with God not to let his enemies rejoice over his misfortune and not to count him among the wrongdoers (7:150). The scene is one of distress, after the people had taken a calf in his absence.
**The Context in Which the Community Heard It**
That setting explains the practical tone of the sentence. A community under pressure needs something it can do, and the verse gives it patience and mindfulness rather than information about the intentions of its enemies. The Book's guidance in such passages is consistently about the conduct of the believer and consistently silent about the details of the plot.

**The Two Reactions and the Cure**
The verse closes with a condition rather than a promise: if you are patient and mindful of God, their plotting will not harm you, and God surrounds what they do. The verse before it has already named the two reactions — grief at the community's good fortune and joy at its trouble — and the sūrah will name them again later, telling the Prophet not to grieve over those who rush into disbelief (3:176). The cure given is the same in both places. *Ṣabr* is the refusal to answer a provocation on the provocateur's terms, and *taqwā* is the guard that keeps a person from making the reply that would be wrong even when the complaint is right.
**Where the Believers Were Hearing This**
The passage belongs to the period after Uḥud, and the community was doing what a wounded community does: counting its losses and looking for someone to blame. The Book does not tell them that their opponents will stop. It tells them that the opponents' reactions are not the thing that decides their future. Trouble comes by God's arrangement, and the test is whether a person's conduct holds when the news is bad (57:23). That is a lesson about steadiness rather than about enemies, and the sentence is built so that the reader checks himself first: how much of the grief in the verse has been in his own heart?

## Verse 3:121

> ˹Remember, O Prophet,˺ when you left your home in the early morning to position the believers in the battlefield. And Allah is All-Hearing, All-Knowing

**A Morning Recalled by Command**

The verse opens with a command to remember, and what is to be remembered is a specific morning: the Prophet leaving his household at daybreak to assign the believers their positions in the battlefield. The sentence preserves a piece of military history inside the revelation itself.

**The Withdrawal of a Portion of the Force**

.

The withdrawal is the background to the verse's attention to positioning. A force that has already lost part of its strength is a force whose commander must place the remainder with care, and the sūrah's reminder of the dawn placement is a reminder of that care.

**Positioning the Believers**

A narration from al-Barāʾ ibn ʿĀzib in Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī (3039) reports that the Prophet appointed ʿAbdullāh ibn Jubayr over fifty archers and told them to hold their position, not to move even if they saw the enemy defeated and fleeing, until he sent for them. The instruction and its precise conditions became the crux of the day's events, and the sūrah's account of what followed will turn on what happened on that hill.

---

## Verse 3:122

> ˹Remember˺ when two groups among you ˹believers˺ were about to cower, then Allah reassured them. So in Allah let the believers put their trust

**Two Groups Named in the Tradition**

The verse recalls a moment when two groups among the believers nearly lost their resolve, and it adds that God was their protector and that the believers should place their trust in Him. The community remembered which groups were meant.

A narration from Jābir ibn ʿAbdullāh identifies them as the two Madinan tribes of Banū Ḥārithah and Banū Salimah, and adds a detail that has the flavour of the first generation's way of reading the Book. Jābir said that he would not have wished the verse had not been sent down, precisely because of the clause in which God is named as the protector of the two groups. The report is in Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī (4051) and Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim (2505).

**What It Means to Be About to Cower**

The verb describes an inclination rather than an act. The two groups considered faltering, and the Book records the thought without describing it as a deed, which keeps the verse's accusation at the level of temptation.

**The Book's Honesty About Its Community**

The verse records a moment of weakness among the believers, and the Book records such moments repeatedly. It describes the community's fear at Badr, its hesitation before the battle of the Trench, and its afflictions in between, and it never presents the first generation as a body of people who were never afraid.

---

## Verse 3:123

> Indeed, Allah made you victorious at Badr when you were ˹vastly˺ outnumbered. So be mindful of Allah, perhaps you will be grateful

**A Victory Recalled Before a Trial**

The verse reminds the believers that God gave them victory at Badr when they were few, and it tells them to be mindful of God so that they may be grateful. The reminder is placed between the recollection of the march to Uḥud and the promises of angelic aid that follow.

The position is the point. The sūrah is about to describe a day of loss, and it first recalls a day of victory in which the same community was vastly outnumbered. A reader who holds the two together learns that neither outcome was determined by numbers, and the instruction at the end of the verse tells him what does determine it.

**The Disproportion at Badr**

The sūrah has already described the two armies at Badr and has stated that the believers saw their enemy at twice their number (3:13). The report of the Prophet's supplication on that day, recorded in Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim (1763), describes him calling on God with the words that if the small band with him were destroyed, none would remain to worship God on the earth.

The community's later memory placed its own numbers at three hundred and thirteen against roughly a thousand of the enemy. The verse's phrase about their being few matches that memory, and its use of the plural verb addresses the whole community rather than the Prophet alone. What happened at Badr is presented as something the believers did and something God gave.

**Why the Earlier Victory Is Named**

The sūrah's use of Badr in this passage is not nostalgia. It is the provision of a case that the community could check against its own experience. The men being addressed had been at Badr; many of them had been at Uḥud as well, and the two events together were the material from which the sūrah's lessons were drawn.

**One Battle Mentioned Twice**

The sūrah refers to Badr in two places. The first presents the two armies as a sign and states that the believers saw their enemy at twice their number (3:13). The second, in the verse before the reader, names the same event as a reminder of help already given.

---

## Verse 3:124

> ˹Remember, O Prophet,˺ when you said to the believers, “Is it not enough that your Lord will send down a reinforcement of three thousand angels for your aid?”

**Angels in the Accounts of Badr**

The Book's other account of angelic support at Badr states that the believers cried out to their Lord for help and were answered with the promise of a thousand angels sent in succession (8:9). The promise in the verse before the reader is of three thousand, and the difference belongs to the different occasions of the two passages.

**Where the Promise Belongs**

Al-Rāzī set out the disagreement over whether these words were spoken to the community in connection with the battle of Badr or the battle of Uḥud. If the promise belongs to Badr, the sentence joins the passage that recalls the community's weakness before the fighting and the help that followed it; if it belongs to Uḥud, the account runs on from the verse that describes the Prophet leaving his household on the morning of the battle. The commentators were equally divided over how the help of the angels was to be understood. Most held that the angels fought at Badr, where the Book's own account describes the rain, the sleep that came over the believers, and the hosts that were not seen (8:9–12). Some reports describe the participation in physical terms, with strange deaths in the enemy ranks and unknown fighters on the field. Others read the help as the strengthening of the believers' hearts, the sharpening of their senses, and the courage their belief in the promise gave them, which is how al-Rāzī treated the question.

---

## Verse 3:125

> Most certainly, if you ˹believers˺ are firm and mindful ˹of Allah˺ and the enemy launches a sudden attack on you, Allah will reinforce you with five thousand angels designated ˹for battle˺

**A Promise With Its Condition Attached**

The verse states that if the believers hold firm and remain mindful of God, and the enemy attacks them suddenly, God will reinforce them with five thousand angels sent with distinguishing marks. The condition is stated before the promise, and the order is the verse's teaching.

The condition has two parts: patient firmness and awareness of God. Both have already appeared in this passage, and both belong to the community's own conduct rather than to anything that can be known about the enemy. What the verse does is attach the promised help to a state that lies within the believers' control.

**The Increase in the Number**

The previous verse recalled the promise of three thousand angels, and this one speaks of five thousand. The increase answers the added condition, and it answers the situation the verse describes, which is an assault launched without warning.

The commentators discussed the word describing the angels, which is usually rendered as marked or designated. Some explained it as describing horses with distinctive marks; others took it as a reference to the angels themselves being distinguished from others. The point of the word, on any reading, is that the reinforcement is a specific and prepared force rather than a general expression of support.

---

## Verse 3:126

> Allah ordained this ˹reinforcement˺ only as good news for you and reassurance for your hearts. And victory comes only from Allah—the Almighty, All-Wise—

**The Purpose Behind the Promise**

The verse states that the reinforcement was intended as good news and as a means of reassurance for the believers' hearts, and it ends by attributing victory to God alone. The sentence explains why the promise of angels was given rather than describing what the angels did.

The same explanation appears in the other account of the battle, where the aid is called a sign of victory and a reassurance, followed by the statement that help comes only from God (8:10). The Book's consistent position is that the language of aid is addressed to the inner state of the people who receive it, and the verse before the reader states that purpose in its opening words.

**Victory Attributed to God Alone**

The clause that victory belongs to God alone is the sentence's centre. The Book states it elsewhere in its most direct form, telling the believers that if God helps them none can defeat them, and that if He withholds help nobody else can supply it (3:160).

**Whether the Angels Fought or Strengthened**

The commentaries discuss what the angels did at the two battles. Most hold that they fought at Badr and that their participation is described in the sūrah of the Spoils; on the accepted view they did not fight at Uḥud, and their mention in this passage is understood as moral support. Ibn Kathīr and others record this position, and al-Ṭabarī notes that God does not need angels to help the believers.

---

## Verse 3:127

> to destroy a group of the disbelievers and humble the rest, causing them to withdraw in disappointment

**A Purpose Stated in Verbs**

The verse sets out what the arrangement was meant to achieve: the cutting off of a faction among the disbelievers, or their humiliation, and their withdrawal in disappointment. The sentences complete the explanation that the previous verse began.

**The Historical Shape of the Two Battles**

The two battles that the sūrah has been describing ended in opposite ways on the surface. At Badr the believers, though few, broke the opposing force and killed a number of its leaders; at Uḥud the believers suffered casualties including prominent Companions, and the enemy withdrew without pressing its advantage.

The verse's two clauses fit both events. A part of the enemy's strength was cut off at Badr, and the enemy went home disappointed after Uḥud. The Book describes the same purpose across two days that felt very different to the community that lived through them, and that is part of what the passage is teaching. A reader who compares the two accounts notices that the sūrah never presents the losses at Uḥud as a reversal of the promise recorded before them; it presents them as part of the same arrangement, in which the community's own conduct and the divine purpose both had their place.

---

## Verse 3:128

> You ˹O Prophet˺ have no say in the matter. It is up to Allah to turn to them in mercy or punish them, for indeed they are wrongdoers

**A Limit Placed on the Messenger**

The verse tells the Prophet that he has no share in the decision, and it explains that the matter belongs to God, who may relent toward the people in question or may punish them, since they are wrongdoers. The sentence defines the boundary of the messenger's role at a moment when his feelings were most engaged.

**Mercy and Punishment Reserved to God**

The Book holds this position consistently. One passage states that God's mercy encompasses everything, while the same passage affirms that punishment falls where He wills (7:156). Another passage, addressed to those who have gone to extremes against themselves, commands them not to despair of God's mercy and states that He forgives all sins (39:53). The verse before the reader applies the same framework to a specific group.

**The Accounts of What Prompted the Verse**

Al-Ṭabarī recorded the report that the Prophet was wounded at Uḥud and that one of his front teeth was chipped or broken, and that his face bled from the fighting. The words attributed to him on that occasion were a question about how a people could prosper when they wounded their prophet while he was calling them to their Lord, and the verse was revealed in connection with that moment. Another account places the revelation in relation to a supplication made during the prayer, in which the Prophet asked God to curse certain people and the verse told him that the matter was not his to decide. Ibn Kathīr reports that some of those named in the second account later accepted the message, which is the point the verse makes: the outcome for any person is open until the end.

**What the Book Says About Guidance**

The sentence before the reader is joined by several passages that make the same point. One tells the Prophet that he is not responsible for the guidance of the people and that God guides whom He wills (2:272). Another tells him that if they turn away, his duty is only to convey the message (3:20); a third states that he will not guide whom he loves, since guidance is God's to give (28:56). The verses together mark the boundary of a messenger's work. He delivers the message and cares about the outcome, and the outcome itself is not in his hands, which is why the sentence can speak of the matter having been settled elsewhere. The sūrah's position in its account of the battle makes the point sharper, since the sentence arrives at the moment when the Prophet's grief was greatest.

---

## Verse 3:129

> To Allah ˹alone˺ belongs whatever is in the heavens and whatever is on the earth. He forgives whoever He wills, and punishes whoever He wills. And Allah is All-Forgiving, Most Merciful

**Not Despairing and Not Presuming**

The verse occupies the middle ground between two errors, and the Book addresses both elsewhere. One passage addresses people who have wronged themselves and forbids them to despair of God's mercy, telling them that He forgives all sins (39:53). Other passages warn those who presume on forgiveness while persisting in their conduct.

The verse before the reader states the fact on which both warnings rest: the decision is God's. A person who understands the sentence will not conclude that his outcome is fixed by anything in his own control, and he will not conclude that it is arbitrary. What he is given is the knowledge that the matter rests with a forgiver who is also a punisher, and that both descriptions are true.

The passage that precedes this verse dealt with a battle and with the fate of its opposing parties. This sentence takes that material out of the hands of the community and places it with God.

---

The verse follows the Prophet's own instruction not to intervene in the punishment of those who fought at Uḥud: "you have no say in the matter" (3:128). Sovereignty over forgiveness and punishment is God's alone, and the Qur'an states the same formula in several places — He forgives whom He wills and punishes whom He wills (5:18; 2:284) — which rules out both human despair about God's mercy and human presumption on it. The chapter uses the same closing words here and in the passage about the People of the Book: Allah is All-Forgiving, Most Merciful. The commentators read the verse as comfort for the Prophet after the setback: he had been told he could not decide who would be forgiven, and the sentence assures him that the mercy is not small. The wrongdoers of Uḥud were still within reach of it, and the chapter will show men who were pardoned after they had fled (3:155).

## Verse 3:130

> O believers! Do not consume interest, multiplying it many times over. And be mindful of Allah, so you may prosper

**A Command About Money in a Passage About War**

The verse addresses the believers about interest and forbids them to take it in multiplied form, and it closes by telling them to be mindful of God so that they may prosper. It follows a passage about battles, aid, and the fate of opponents.

**What the Term Covers**

The Arabic word for interest describes an addition charged on a loan, and the Book's treatment of the practice is worked out at length in the sūrah of the Cow. There it states that God has permitted trade and forbidden interest (2:275), and then commands the believers to give up outstanding interest if they are believers, warning them that persistence means a war with God and His Messenger (2:278-279).

**"Multiplying It Many Times Over"**

The phrase describes a debt that grows by doubling when it is not repaid, and the practice was one that left borrowers in a position they could not escape. Commentators discussed whether the qualifier limits the prohibition to the compounded form or describes the practice as it was known.

The second reading was the one favoured by most of the classical scholars, who held that the prohibition covers any guaranteed addition for the mere passage of time, and the Book's later verses in al-Baqarah state the rule in general terms. The description in this verse records a shape the practice often took rather than a boundary of the prohibition.

**The Book's Contrast Between Interest and Charity**

The Book sets the two practices against each other in a way that makes the prohibition a matter of principle rather than of amounts. One passage states that whatever a person lends seeking interest will not increase with God, while whatever is given as charity seeking His pleasure multiplies its reward (30:39).

Another states directly that God makes interest fruitless and makes charity fruitful (2:276). The pair of sentences treats the two uses of wealth as producing opposite results, and the result is measured not in the world's terms but in the accounting the Book has been describing throughout the sūrah.

---

## Verse 3:131

> Guard yourselves against the Fire prepared for the disbelievers

**A Command to Guard Oneself**

The verse commands the believers to guard themselves against the Fire, and the word it uses is the one the Book employs throughout for the awareness that keeps a person within limits. It carries the senses of fear, care, and standing watch.

**The Fire Described as Prepared**

The sentence states that the Fire has been prepared and names those for whom the preparation is made. The Book uses the same image in its earlier description of the Fire, which it calls a blaze fuelled by people and stones and states to be prepared for the disbelievers (2:24).

Another passage addresses the believers and tells them to protect themselves and their families from a fire whose fuel is people and stones, guarded by severe angels who carry out every command they are given (66:6). The image of preparation suggests something made ready in advance, and the Book uses it to remove the sense that the destination is a metaphor for consequences in general.

**Prepared for the Disbelievers, Addressed to the Believers**

The passage immediately before this verse concerned the prohibition of interest, and the passage immediately after concerns obedience to God and the Messenger. The warning is placed between two instructions, which is where the Book usually puts it, and the community reading it is given the choice the passage is designed to present.

---

## Verse 3:132

> Obey Allah and the Messenger, so you may be shown mercy

**Two Obediences Named in One Command**

The verse commands obedience to God and to the Messenger, and it attaches a promise of mercy to the command. The pairing of the two objects of obedience is one of the Book's most frequent constructions.

The Book's logic in pairing them is that the messenger conveys what God has commanded, and obedience to the message is therefore obedience to the sender. The sūrah elsewhere states the connection from the other side, telling the Prophet to say that those who love God should follow him, and that God will then love them and forgive their sins (3:31). The two verses together describe a single relationship with two faces: love toward God and obedience toward the one He sent.
