**Where the Obligation of Obedience Falls**

The Book states the principle in another passage, where it tells the Prophet to consult his companions in matters and then to put his trust in God once he has decided (3:159). The two verses describe the same structure: consultation, decision, and an obedience that no longer reopens the question. The community's health depended on that structure, and the losses of Uḥud were the price of the break in it.

**Obedience in the Passages About the Messenger**

The command to obey the Prophet appears throughout the Book, and it covers the ordinary business of the community's life: settling disputes, taking part in its undertakings, accepting its judgments. The Book states in one place that whoever obeys the messenger has obeyed God, which is the ground of the command in this verse.

---

## Verse 3:133

> And hasten towards forgiveness from your Lord and a Paradise as vast as the heavens and the earth, prepared for those mindful ˹of Allah˺

**A Command to Hasten**

The verse tells the believers to move quickly toward forgiveness from their Lord and toward a garden described as wide as the heavens and the earth, prepared for those who are mindful of God. The verb used for the movement is the one the Book employs for racing.

The same command appears in another sūrah in almost the same form, where the believers are told to compete with one another for forgiveness from their Lord and for a garden as vast as the heavens and the earth, prepared for those who believe in God and His messengers (57:21). The Book's habit of repeating the instruction shows how it treats the pursuit of the Hereafter: as a task that requires speed rather than a conclusion that can be reached at leisure. The order of the two objects is significant, since forgiveness is named before the garden; what the race reaches first is the pardon that opens the way to the reward.

---

## Verse 3:134

> ˹They are˺ those who donate in prosperity and adversity, control their anger, and pardon others. And Allah loves the good-doers

**The Portrait of the People Described**

The verse continues the description of the people for whom the garden is prepared, and it gives three practices: giving in ease and hardship, restraining anger, and pardoning others. It ends by stating that God loves those who do good.

The three items cover the three situations in which a person's character is tested. The first concerns wealth, the second concerns provocation, and the third concerns power over someone who has done harm. Each of them is a place where most people fail in a predictable way, and the verse names the practice that constitutes success in each.

**A Report About Strength**

The Prophet's definition of strength takes up the same theme. A narration from Abū Hurayrah reports him saying that the strong person is not the one who overpowers others, but the one who controls himself while in a state of anger (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī 6114, Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim 2609).

The report gives the verse's instruction the weight of a redefinition. A community that measured strength by the ability to dominate is told that the quality it admires belongs to the person who governs himself. The Book's praise of those who restrain their anger is the same valuation stated in the language of the Hereafter, where the reward is described as a filling of the heart with security and faith.

**Pardoning Those Within One's Power**

The Book states the same ethic in other passages. One describes the righteous as those who, when they are angered, forgive (42:37). Another states that whoever pardons and seeks reconciliation will find his reward with God (42:40). A third commands the believers to respond to evil with what is best, and observes that the person with whom one is in a feud may then become a close friend (41:34).

The statement is the highest form of praise the Book offers, and its placement at the end of a list of difficult practices is deliberate. The community is told that the behaviour described is not only advisable but beloved, and the sentence supplies the motivation that the preceding items require.

**What "In Ease and in Hardship" Was Taken to Mean**

The pair of conditions named in the sentence received two explanations. Al-Ṭabarī read the first term as naming a time when wealth is plentiful, so that the contrast is between giving out of abundance and giving when there is little to give. Al-Rāzī recorded a second reading in which the pairing describes the giver's own state rather than his means: whether he is content or distressed, whether the act of giving pleases him or costs him something, he still spends in the way of God.

The second reading is the more demanding of the two, and the Book's other descriptions of the practice support it. The people praised in an earlier verse of this sūrah are described as charitable without any qualification attached to the giving (3:17), and a passage in the sūrah of Consultation describes those who forgive when they are angered (42:37). Both passages treat conduct as the evidence of the disposition, which is why the two conditions are named in a single phrase. What the sentence asks for is not generosity in one mood but a practice that survives the change of circumstances.

---

## Verse 3:135

> ˹They are˺ those who, upon committing an evil deed or wronging themselves, remember Allah and seek forgiveness and do not knowingly persist in sin—and who forgives sins except Allah

**The Question About Forgiveness**

The sentence interrupts its own description with a question: who forgives sins except God? The question is rhetorical, and its function is to remove the possibility that the person might seek the remedy elsewhere or despair of finding it.

The Book's statements about forgiveness support the question on both sides. One passage states that God does not forgive the association of others with Him but forgives anything else for whoever He wills (4:48). Another addresses those who have exceeded all limits against themselves and tells them not to lose hope in the mercy of God, who forgives all sins (39:53). The question in this verse stands between the two statements and confirms both, since it locates the remedy with the one who has set the terms and rules out any other source of pardon.

**What the Book Says About the Outcome of Such Repentance**

The Book's other statements about repentance describe its effects on the person who turns back. One passage promises that those who repent, believe, and do good will have their evil deeds changed into good deeds, and describes God as forgiving and merciful (25:70). Another states that whoever commits evil or wrongs himself and then seeks God's forgiveness will find Him forgiving and merciful (4:110).

**What Persistence Means**

Al-Ṭabarī explained the phrase rendered as persisting in sin as describing those who continue in an act knowingly, whether the knowledge is of the act's prohibition or of its character as disobedience. The qualification is significant because it excludes the person who falls into a fault and regrets it. Some commentaries connect the verse with a report about a man who bought dates from a woman, embraced and kissed her, and then came to the Prophet regretting what he had done. The report illustrates the situation the sentence describes, and the sūrah's answer to it is the description of the people who remember God, ask forgiveness, and do not continue. The words for workers of righteousness in the following verse were read by al-Rāzī as evidence that the reward of the garden involves what a person does as well as the grace that makes his work possible, and the two passages are read together for that reason.

---

## Verse 3:136

> Their reward is forgiveness from their Lord and Gardens under which rivers flow, staying there forever. How excellent is the reward for those who work ˹righteousness˺

The verse states the reward of the people described in the two verses before it, and it names two things: forgiveness from their Lord, and gardens with rivers running beneath them. The pairing matches the two orders of the passage that has just ended.

**The Gardens and the Rivers Beneath Them**

The phrase about rivers running below the gardens is one of the Book's most frequent descriptions of the Hereafter, and the earlier sūrahs use it in the same words. The image gathers what a garden needs in the lands from which the first audience heard the message: water that never fails.

The Book uses the picture to describe a place of continuous provision rather than an estate with fixed borders. Elsewhere the description is expanded with details of greeting and speech, as in the passage where the people of the garden are welcomed with peace and their closing words are praise of God, the Lord of all worlds (10:10). The rivers are the opening line of a description that the Book extends in many places.

**Abiding In it**

The statement that they remain there is part of every extended description of the garden in the Book. It answers the fear that has shadowed human hopes about good things: that they end.

The Book contrasts the permanence of the next life with the passing character of this one in several places, and it uses the contrast to order its instruction about what deserves effort. A reward that could be lost would not settle the account of people who gave up what they had for it. The sentence in this verse closes that question, and the description of the garden that appears when the believers are admitted states that they stay there by the will of their Lord (14:23).

**The Exclamation That Closes the Verse**

The same form appears elsewhere in the Book at the point where the reward is named, as in the declaration of the righteous in the Hereafter that God has fulfilled what He promised them and that excellent indeed is the reward of those who work (39:74). The Book puts this kind of sentence at the end of a description to leave the listener with the value of what has been described, and this verse does so in a single clause.

**What the Two Collections Say About Deeds and Mercy**

The tradition records a statement that no one enters the garden by his deeds alone, that the most beloved conduct is what is done consistently even if it is little. That the Prophet himself answered in the same terms when he was asked whether the rule applied to him (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī 6464, Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim 2818).

---

## Verse 3:137

> Similar situations came to pass before you, so travel throughout the land and see the fate of the deniers

**The Word Behind "Wonts"**

The verse tells the community that patterns of conduct have passed away before it and directs people to travel and consider how those who denied the truth ended. The word rendered here as wonts is the plural of a noun that covers an established practice, a way of doing things that has been set up and followed.

**A Report About Establishing a Practice**

A report from Jarīr ibn ʿAbdullāh records the Prophet's statement that whoever establishes a good practice in Islam has its reward and the reward of those who act on it after him, without anything being taken from their reward. That whoever establishes an evil practice carries its burden and the burden of those who act on it after him (Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim 1017).

**The Verse's Place After the Battle**

Some commentators connected this verse with Uḥud, reading it as a statement that the enemies of the believers would meet a fate like that of the enemies of earlier messengers. The community that had just lost seventy of its men was being told to look at the wider pattern.

---

## Verse 3:138

> This is an insight to humanity—a guide and a lesson to the God-fearing

**The Verse's Work in the Passage**

The verse stands between the command to consider the ends of earlier peoples and the two commands that follow it, which tell the believers not to weaken and not to grieve. Placed where it is, the sentence explains why the community should be able to absorb the lesson of its own losses.

**The Word Behind "Exposition"**

The verse describes what has been sent down as an exposition for mankind, and the noun behind the rendering is one of the names the tradition has attached to the Book itself. Its root carries the sense of making a matter clear and separating it from confusion.

The losses at Uḥud were severe enough to invite the conclusion that the community had been abandoned. The verse answers that conclusion in advance by stating what the message the community carries actually is: a clarification for everyone, and a guidance and an urging for those who take it seriously. The Book's own presence among them was the reason for confidence, and the next verses build on that ground.

---

## Verse 3:139

> Do not falter or grieve, for you will have the upper hand, if you are ˹true˺ believers

**Two Commands Joined in One Sentence**

The verse forbids two things at once and then gives a reason for the prohibition. The community is told not to weaken and not to grieve, and it is told why: its position is one of ascendancy, given the condition the sentence names at its end.

**Grief and What It Was About**

Al-Ṭabarī read the prohibition of grief in this verse as directed at mourning over the losses of the battle, the men killed and the ground given up on the day of Uḥud. The grief the verse addresses is not sorrow in general but sorrow over the specific cost of the fighting.

The distinction is worth keeping. The Book does not forbid the community to feel the deaths of its people, and it does not treat the memory of a defeat as a subject that must be avoided. What the verse forbids is a grief that settles into the conclusion that the cause has been lost, and the reason that follows in the next clause explains why that conclusion is wrong.

---

## Verse 3:140

> If you have suffered injuries ˹at Uḥud˺, they suffered similarly ˹at Badr˺. We alternate these days ˹of victory and defeat˺ among people so that Allah may reveal the ˹true˺ believers, choose martyrs from among you—and Allah does not like the wrongdoers—

**The Wording About God Knowing**

The statement that God acts so that He may know those who believe is one of the Book's many expressions of this kind, and the tradition of interpretation explains it as knowledge that becomes evident rather than knowledge newly acquired. God's knowledge of a person's faith does not change, but the moment of trial makes the faith visible to the community and to the person himself.

**What the Refining Produces**

The word rendered as assay names the process of separating impurities from metal, and al-Ṭabarī explained it as the trials through which patience is tested. The word rendered as blight names reduction and shortage, the dwindling of something until nothing of it can be seen, and it appears again in the sūrah of the Cow (2:276). The two words describe the same operation from different angles: what is lost is gone, and what remains has been clarified. The statement that God may know the believers is also stated in the verse that follows, which asks whether the community imagined it would enter the garden without God knowing those among them who strove and those who were patient. The knowledge in question is therefore displayed rather than learned, and the sūrah's discussion of the point, which the commentators connected with the verse about the change of direction in prayer, keeps the reader from reading the phrase as a statement about information God acquires (2:143).

---

## Verse 3:141

> and distinguish the ˹true˺ believers and destroy the disbelievers

**The Two Battles in the Commentators' Reading**

The commentators read the reference to a people who had suffered a like wound as pointing to the enemies of the believers who had been defeated at Badr the year before. The wound at Uḥud answered an earlier wound, and the same community that had won the first engagement was present in both.

**Blight and What It Means**

The second half of the clause says that God blights the deniers, and the word describes a reduction or a shortage, a diminishing so thorough that nothing of the thing can be seen. Al-Rāzī explained it in those terms, and the same root appears in the earlier sūrah where God states that He destroys interest and causes charity to grow.

This reading gives the verse its historical anchor and explains why the sūrah refers to the alternation of days. The tradition of interpretation added an Arabic proverb that captures the sense of the passage: one day for you, and one day against you. The Book uses that rhythm as a teacher, and both battles belong to the lesson.

---

## Verse 3:142

> Do you think you will enter Paradise without Allah proving which of you ˹truly˺ struggled ˹for His cause˺ and patiently endured

**Striving and Patience Named Together**

The two qualities named in the verse are the ones the sūrah has been testing since its account of Uḥud began. Striving covers the effort that a person makes, including the effort of fighting, and patience covers the endurance of what the effort costs.

The Book habitually pairs them, and the pairing appears in other passages about trial: the community is told that God will test it until the truth about those who strive and remain steadfast becomes known. It is told that the entry into the garden depends on proving which of its members struggled and endured. Neither quality is presented as an alternative to the other. Effort without endurance collapses at the first hardship, and endurance without effort is passive.

**Comparison With Other Passages**

The Book states the same principle in several other places. One passage tells the believers that God will test them until He shows who among them strive and remain steadfast. Another asks whether people think they will be left alone once they have said they believe, without being put to the test. A third describes earlier communities afflicted with hardship and shaken to the point where the messenger and those with him asked when God's help would come.

---

## Verse 3:143

> You certainly wished ˹for the opportunity˺ for martyrdom before encountering it, now you have seen it with your own eyes

**Who the Verse Addresses**

Al-Ṭabarī reports that the verse is understood to address believers who had not taken part in the Battle of Badr and who had wished for an opportunity to fight the idolaters in the way of God. The verse reminds them that the wish they had expressed was now being answered in the hardest possible form, and it does so without any softening of the contrast between the hope and the event.

**Wishing for Death in the Book's Other Passages**

The Book addresses the subject of wishing for death in other places as well, and in those passages the wish is treated as a claim that a person is invited to substantiate. It tells those who assert that the next life is theirs alone to wish for death if their claim is true, and it tells others who spoke about their fallen brothers that they should try to avoid death if what they say is true.

The pattern is consistent across the Book. A claim about one's standing is tested by whether the person is prepared to accept the consequences of it. The verse before the reader belongs to the same family of statements, and its application to the community at Madinah was immediate.

---

## Verse 3:144

> Muḥammad is no more than a messenger; other messengers have gone before him. If he were to die or to be killed, would you regress into disbelief? Those who do so will not harm Allah whatsoever. And Allah will reward those who are grateful

**The Name Used in the Sentence**

The verse names the Prophet directly and states that he is nothing more than a messenger, and other messengers had passed away before him. The sūrah uses his name only a few times, and the use of it here is part of the sentence's teaching.

**Messengers Who Have Passed Away**

The statement that other messengers have gone before him places the Prophet within a sequence, and the Book states the mortality of the messengers in several places. One passage asks whether, if the Prophet were to die, his people would live forever, and another declares that everything is bound to perish except God Himself.

**The Day the Prophet Died**

Abū Bakr recited this verse, beginning with the statement that whoever had worshipped Muhammad should know that Muhammad had died, and that whoever worshipped God should know that God is alive and does not die (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī 1241, 1242). ʿUmar later said that it was as though he had never read the verse before that day. The tradition preserved the incident because it shows the verse carrying the community through the moment it had been preparing them for.

---

The verse was heard at Uḥud, when the rumour spread through the ranks that the Prophet had been killed and the shock of it turned men back toward the position they had held before Islam. The argument is put in a form the community could not escape: he is a messenger, and messengers have died before him; the faith was never a contract with a person. The Book stated the same fact twice more for the Prophet's own hearing: "you will certainly die, and they will die too" (39:30), and the command to the Prophet in the sūrah of the Cave to say that he is only a man receiving revelation (18:110). The section on this verse records the moment the fact became public among the community, when Abū Bakr stood in the mosque and recited the verse while ʿUmar and others could not believe what had happened. The closing words, that whoever turns back does not harm God in the least, put the loss where it belongs.

## Verse 3:145

> No soul can ever die without Allah’s Will at the destined time. Those who desire worldly gain, We will let them have it, and those who desire heavenly reward, We will grant it to them. And We will reward those who are grateful

**The Hypocrites' Argument Silenced**

The commentary tradition noted that the verse also closes an argument that the hypocrites among the community were using. Their claim was that the men who died at Uḥud would not have died if they had stayed home, and they used it to discourage the believers from taking part in further fighting.

The Book answers the argument directly elsewhere by telling the men who said it about their brothers to try to avoid death themselves if what they said was true. The principle in this verse is the deeper answer: the term is fixed, and remaining behind provides no more protection than going out does. The two statements work together to remove the calculation from the community's thinking.

**A Report About Three Men**

The tradition preserves a report that bears on the verse's contrast between motives. Abū Hurayrah related that among the first people whose cases are decided on the Day of Judgment is a man who died as a martyr, who is made to recount the blessings he received and admits them. Who is told that he fought so that it might be said he was brave, and that it was said (Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim 1905a).

**What the Verse Says**
The verse states that no soul dies except by God's permission, at a term that has been written, and it turns then to what a person wants in exchange: whoever wants the reward of this world, he is given of it, and whoever wants the reward of the Hereafter, he is given of it, and God will reward the grateful. The sentence is one of the Book's clearest statements that the length of a life is not something a person protects by caution, and the words about the two rewards cover every person's motive. The Sūrah of the Family of ʿImrān puts it right after the account of Uḥud and immediately before the passage about the messengers who were killed among their followers (3:146).
**The Argument It Closes**
The report of Uḥud included men who said that their brothers would not have died if they had stayed home, and the sūrah answers them in the verses around this one: what was written had to happen, and the men who died did not die by someone else's decision. The Prophet's own practice of caution in a battlefield — the armour, the guard, the night watch — belongs with the verse, since the Book elsewhere commands preparation and does not treat foresight as a lack of trust (8:60). The verse's point is sharper than either: the appointment is settled, and what a person chooses is what he is living for.
**The Hadith and the Community's Use**
The scholars of the community used the verse in two ways: against those who distrusted God's provision, and against those who exposed themselves needlessly. The Prophet's word in Muslim that no one dies until his provision is complete encouraged the pursuit of livelihood, and this verse is the scriptural ground under it. The verse ends with the grateful, which is the quality the sūrah has been asking of the community since its opening, and the gratitude is attached to the One who gives each person what he asked for by the way he lived.

---

## Verse 3:146

> ˹Imagine˺ how many devotees fought along with their prophets and never faltered despite whatever ˹losses˺ they suffered in the cause of Allah, nor did they weaken or give in! Allah loves those who persevere

**The Two Readings of the Verb**

The verb in the opening clause can be read as referring to fighting, or as referring to being killed. On the second reading the sentence says that many devoted men stood alongside a prophet who was killed, which is why al-Ṭabarī preferred it: the reading fits the plain sense of the preceding verses about the Prophet's mortality.

Both readings support the same lesson. Whether the prophet fought and his companions fought with him, or the prophet was killed and his companions held their ground, the sentence presents people who continued in their duty while the outcome was in doubt. The difference between the readings concerns which aspect of the earlier history is being held up, and the community's own recent experience answers to both.

**The Construction That Sets Present Losses Inside a Pattern**

The verse opens with a question about how many, and the same figure of speech carries encouragement elsewhere in the Book. When the companions of Saul looked at the army of Goliath and lost heart, the ones who were certain of meeting God answered that a small force has often overcome a great one by God's will, and the verse closes by naming God's company with the steadfast (2:249).

---

## Verse 3:147

> And all they said was, “Our Lord! Forgive our sins and excesses, make our steps firm, and grant us victory over the disbelieving people.”

**The Restriction in the Sentence**

The verse reports that the only words of these people were a prayer, and the restriction is the point of the sentence. Faced with a defeat, the devoted men did not debate, did not cast blame, and did not announce what the community should have done differently; they asked God for four things.

**The Request That Steps Be Made Firm**

The third petition asks God to make the community's steps firm, and the request uses the image of standing and walking on the ground. The Book uses the same image when it promises the believers that if they support God's cause, He will support them and steady their footing.

The request has a particular fitness at this point in the sūrah. The community's failure at Uḥud was a failure of footing, and the men whose prayer is recorded here ask for the remedy in the words that describe the problem. The Book presents such prayers as the place where people take the weakness they have seen in themselves and ask for what would correct it.

---

## Verse 3:148

> So Allah gave them the reward of this world and the excellent reward of the Hereafter. For Allah loves the good-doers

**Both Rewards Given**

The verse reports the result of the conduct and the prayer described in the two verses before it: God gave those people the reward of this world and the best reward of the Hereafter. The Book's promises of this kind appear in several places, and the phrasing here covers the whole of what a person can receive.

The Book does not usually promise both to those who seek only one. It tells those who want the harvest of the next life that it will be increased for them, and it tells those who want only the present one that they will receive some of it while having no share in the next. The sentence in this verse belongs to a different group, and the community at Madinah was being shown what their position could yield.

**The Link With the Verses Before It**

The arrangement gives the community a way of reading its own experience. The losses at Uḥud were real, and the sūrah does not promise that the community will recover every loss in the same currency. What it promises is that the conduct it has described is not wasted, and that the reward reaches into the situation the community is in as well as into the one that follows it.

---

## Verse 3:149

> O believers! If you yield to the disbelievers, they will drag you back to disbelief—and you will become losers

**The Address and Its Warning**

The verse addresses the believers directly and warns them against obeying those who deny, describing the consequence in physical terms: they would be turned back on their heels and would end up as losers. The warning is stated as a certainty in the form the Book uses for imminent dangers.

The community had come through a period in which advice was plentiful and its sources were mixed. The sūrah has already described a party among the People of the Book who wished to mislead the believers, and it has described the conduct of the hypocrites during the fighting. The verse gathers such advice into a single category and addresses the community's habit of listening to it.

**The Continuity With the Previous Verses**

The sūrah's account of the battle has shown what happens when a community departs from the instructions it has been given. The warning in this verse generalises the lesson by naming the source of such departures as the counsel of those who deny. The community is told, in practice, that the danger it faces after the battle is the same as the danger it faced during it, in a different form.

---

## Verse 3:150

> But no! Allah is your Guardian, and He is the best Helper

**A Short Verse With Weight**

The verse is among the shortest in the passage, and its brevity suits its function. It stands at the end of the section that dealt with the aftermath of Uḥud, and it supplies the conclusion the whole section has been building toward.

The Book uses such sentences as conclusions. After the account of a loss, the warnings about apostasy, and the reminder that death comes at an appointed term, the community is given a sentence it can carry: its master is God, and no helper is better. The sūrah does not add qualifications to the promise, and the brevity is part of what makes it memorable.

**What the Community Needed to Hear**

The verse tells them that the security they were looking for was not going to come from a rearrangement of their alliances. The Book does not forbid the making of treaties, and the sūrah refers elsewhere to covenants the Prophet had with other groups. What the sentence does is establish the order of reliance: the community's protection is from God, and any arrangement it enters into operates within that protection rather than replacing it.

---

## Verse 3:151

> We will cast horror into the hearts of the disbelievers for associating ˹false gods˺ with Allah—a practice He has never authorized. The Fire will be their home—what an evil place for the wrongdoers to stay

**The Terror Cast Into Hearts**

The verse announces that terror will be thrown into the hearts of those who deny, and the form of the sentence presents the event as settled. The Book describes this casting of horror among the opponents of the prophets in several passages, and the wording is consistent across them.

One passage reports the instruction given to the angels on the day of Badr, in which God states that He will cast horror into the hearts of those who disbelieve. Another describes the expulsion of a group from their homes and the horror placed in their hearts, so that they destroyed their own houses with their own hands. A third describes the same treatment of those who supported an alliance against the community. The sentence in this verse belongs to that family.

**The Report From the Return to Makkah**

The commentators connect the verse with an event after the fighting at Uḥud. The idolaters, on their way back to Makkah, regretted that they had not finished the community off, and they resolved to return and complete what they had begun. The reports state that God then cast terror into them, and they lost their resolve and continued home.

---

## Verse 3:152

> Indeed, Allah fulfilled His promise to you when you ˹initially˺ swept them away by His Will, then your courage weakened and you disputed about the command and disobeyed, after Allah had brought victory within your reach. Some of you were after worldly gain while others desired a heavenly reward. He denied you victory over them as a test, yet He has pardoned you. And Allah is Gracious to the believers

**Two Desires in One Community**

The verse states that among the community were people who desired this world and people who desired the next, and the distinction is offered as the explanation of the quarrel. The community assembled on the field was not of one mind about what the day was for.

Al-Ṭabarī records a report that Ibn Masʿūd said he had not known, before this verse was sent down, that any of the Prophet's companions could be described as desiring this world. The report conveys the seriousness with which the earliest community received the diagnosis. The verse does not divide the community into two parties of fixed membership; it states that the two motives were present among people who were fighting side by side.

**Turned Aside to Be Tested**

The sentence continues by stating that God turned the community away from its opponents to test it. The Book describes the same arrangement in the account of Badr, where it made each army appear small in the eyes of the other to establishing what had been decreed.

---

The verse explains the setback at Uḥud by the two desires present in the ranks: some wanted this world's goods, the spoils, and others wanted the Hereafter, and the dispute over the command followed from the difference. The commentators read the quarrel back to the order given to the archers to hold their position, which a section of them left when the fighting appeared to be over — a reading drawn from the passage's own sequence, since the verse says "then your courage weakened and you disputed about the command and disobeyed after He had shown you what you like". The sūrah then states the mercy hidden in the withdrawal in judgment: "then He turned you away from them so that He might test you, and He has already pardoned you" (3:152). The same correction is stated at the chapter's close: why did you protest when a loss struck you after you had taken twice as much, and the answer given is that it was because of disobedience (3:165).

**What the Verse Explains**
The verse states that God fulfilled His promise to the community when they were cutting the enemy down by His permission, and then that the community lost heart and argued about the order and disobeyed, after He had shown them what they desired, and it names the reason: among them were men who wanted this world and men who wanted the next. The verse then states the outcome of the day, that God turned them away so that He might test them, and that He forgave them. The passage is the sūrah's account of the setback at Uḥud in its own words, and it names both the obedience and the failure in one sentence.
**What the Commentators Named**
The older commentators named the men who left their position on the pass as the archers, and the promise as the earlier part of the battle when the field was won. They read the argument about the order as the dispute over whether to hold the position or to go down for the spoils, and the two desires as the reason the dispute happened. The Sūrah of the Family of ʿImrān's account comes immediately before the verse about the death of every soul (3:145), and the two are read together as the sūrah's explanation of what a community does when its appetite and its duty pull in different directions.
**The Doctrine the Verse States**
The sentence about the two desires is one of the Book's clearest statements that a community may hold the same belief and still be divided by what its members want. The Sūrah of the Spoils tells the believers the same thing about the day of Badr, where the two groups argued about the spoils (8:7-8), and the Sūrah of the Family of ʿImrān itself returns to the point when it tells the community that they were longing for the meeting and the meeting came (3:143). The verse in front of us does not remove the forgiveness from the day, and the Book's arrangement keeps the failure and the pardon in the same passage.

## Verse 3:153

> ˹Remember˺ when you were running far away ˹in panic˺—not looking at anyone—while the Messenger was calling to you from behind! So Allah rewarded your disobedience with distress upon distress. Now, do not grieve over the victory you were denied or the injury you suffered. And Allah is All-Aware of what you do

**The Verb and the Terrain**

The verb describing the movement has the basic sense of going up, and al-Ṭabarī records a difference of opinion about what it means in this form. Some held that the form used here means to travel along level ground, which would fit the account of the men coming down from the high ground to the field. Others took it as describing flight up the slopes of the mountain.

Both readings describe the same disorder. On the first, the community is depicted streaming across open ground with the enemy behind it; on the second, it is scattered up the hillside. The disagreement is a reminder that the commentators were working with detailed reports of the terrain and the positions, and that they cared about getting the picture right before drawing lessons from it.

**The Discipline of Fear**

The verse's account of the collapse also treats the fear that produced it as a condition to be governed. The Book's commands about courage in this sūrah rest on the same ground as its commands about patience: the person who knows that the outcome is in the hands of God is not ruled by the prospect of loss.

---

The verse's phrase *ghamman bi-ghammin*, distress upon distress, names the sequence: the defeat in the field, and then the report that reached the men that the Prophet had been killed, which was harder to bear than the loss itself. The mercy that follows is given in the next verse, where sleep was sent down over one company of them (3:154). The commentators note the detail that the Messenger was calling them "from behind": the men had run past the point where the Prophet stood, and the call is part of the account's severity, since the community was being called back to the position it had abandoned. The correction is stated at the chapter's close, where the community is asked why it protested when a loss struck it after it had already taken twice as much from its enemy, and the answer given is that it was the result of disobedience (3:165).

## Verse 3:154

> Then after distress, He sent down serenity in the form of drowsiness overcoming some of you, while others were disturbed by evil thoughts about Allah—the thoughts of ˹pre-Islamic˺ ignorance. They ask, “Do we have a say in the matter?” Say, ˹O Prophet,˺ “All matters are destined by Allah.” They conceal in their hearts what they do not reveal to you. They say ˹to themselves˺, “If we had any say in the matter, none of us would have come to die here.” Say, ˹O Prophet,˺ “Even if you were to remain in your homes, those among you who were destined to be killed would have met the same fate.” Through this, Allah tests what is within you and purifies what is in your hearts. And Allah knows best what is ˹hidden˺ in the heart

**The Sleep That Came After the Wound**
The verse describes what God sent down on the men who held their ground after the fighting at Uḥud: a security that settled on them as sleep, and it names the group that received it. A part of the community was overcome by drowsiness and lay down in the middle of a battlefield, and the Book treats that sleep as a gift sent down, not as exhaustion. The Sūrah of the Spoils describes the same gift before Badr, where God sent down sleep to make the believers feel secure (8:11), and the parallel tells the reader how the Book reads a night's rest in the middle of a crisis.
**The Other Group and What They Were Thinking**
The same verse names the second group: those who were anxious about themselves, thinking thoughts that were not true, thoughts of the days of ignorance. They were saying, in effect, that the community had been given a choice and would have avoided the ground where the dead fell. The verse answers them without softening the words: say that even if you had stayed in your houses, those who were destined to be killed would have gone out to the places where they were to die. The Book puts the doctrine of the decree inside the account of a battle that went wrong, and it does so to take the sting out of the recriminations. If the men who died had been walking towards an appointment, then the ones who survived were not the ones who had managed their lives better.
**What the Verse Is Testing**
The sentence then says why: so that God might test what is in your hearts and purify what is in your breasts. The Book uses the same language for every trial it records, and the test in this passage is about the inward reaction to a defeat. The Sūrah of Āl-ʿImrān has been doing this for several pages: it explains the causes of the setback (disobedience of the archers, the desire for the spoils), it forgives the men who held on, and it refuses to let the community turn a defeat into a reason for doubt. The verse's comfort and its warning come from the same sentence, and the reader who has just lost a battle is told to look inside himself before he looks for somebody to blame.

**The Morning After the Battle**
The verse belongs to the aftermath of Uḥud, when the Muslim lines had broken and the Prophet was wounded in the face and the mouth. The reports say that he stood with the blood on him and asked forgiveness for his people, and the Book records the community in two groups on that same afternoon: the ones whom sleep carried off and the ones who lay awake worrying. The sleep is named in the verse as a gift sent down, and the Book uses the same word for the calm that came before Badr (8:11), which makes the quiet in the middle of the disaster part of the same pattern of help.
**What the Anxious Group Was Saying**
The men who could not sleep were talking about the decisions that had put them on that ground, and the Book gives their words: if we had any say in the matter, we would not have been killed here. The answer in the verse is one of the plainest statements of the decree in the Book: you would have gone out to the ground where death was written for you. The point is not to remove human responsibility — the sūrah argues the causes of the defeat for several pages around this verse — but to remove the second error that a defeated community makes, which is to believe that a different decision could have made death impossible.
**The Test Inside the Test**
The verse closes by naming what was being examined: not the courage of the men or the tactics of the day, but what was in the hearts when the news was bad. The Sūrah of Āl-ʿImrān returns to the same ground later, telling the believers that these are the days that God alternates among people so that He may know who struggles and so that the patient may be distinguished (3:140-142). The verse is therefore not a comfort offered after the event. It is the doctrine that makes the event bearable and still leaves the community accountable, and the two halves are held together in a single sentence.

## Verse 3:155

> Indeed, those ˹believers˺ who fled on the day when the two armies met were made to slip by Satan because of their misdeeds. But Allah has pardoned them. Surely Allah is All-Forgiving, Most Forbearing

**The Day the Two Hosts Met**

The verse refers to the day the two armies met, which the commentators identify as Uḥud, and it addresses those who turned away during the fighting. The reference places the sentence within the same narrative the previous verses have been unfolding.

**Why the Reports About Individuals Matter**

The commentators connect the case with the Book's account of Adam, whose repentance was accepted after his error (2:37). The comparison supports the sūrah's larger purpose in this passage, which is to teach a community recovering from failure that its mistakes do not have to define it, and that the remedy has been provided.

**What the Community Learned From the Verse**

That combination is the heart of the sūrah's treatment of Uḥud. The community had to be able to continue after a day that exposed its weaknesses, and the Book gives it both the diagnosis and the pardon. The verses that follow turn from the battle to the lessons the community was to carry out of it, and the passage keeps the two movements in that order.

---

The verse names the cause of the withdrawal plainly, and it is a cause the Qur'an assigns to Satan without excusing the men: they slipped "because of some of what they earned", and the Book's rule is that whatever good befalls a person is from God and whatever evil befalls is from the person's own doing (4:79). Satan's agency is real in the Qur'an but never coercive; he suggests, and the deed remains the doer's. What the verse does with the slip is characteristically this sūrah: it closes the matter with pardon, "but Allah has pardoned them", the same turn the chapter took at 3:152. The Book's most-quoted statement of that mercy is addressed to people who had gone much further than fleeing a battlefield — those who exceeded the limits against their own souls are told not to despair of God's mercy, for He forgives all sins (39:53).

## Verse 3:156

> O believers! Do not be like the unfaithful who say about their brothers who travel throughout the land or engage in battle, “If they had stayed with us, they would not have died or been killed.” Allah makes such thinking a cause of agony in their hearts. It is Allah who gives life and causes death. And Allah is All-Seeing of what you do

**The Prohibition and the Model Rejected**

The verse addresses the believers and forbids them to resemble those who deny in what they say about their dead. The comparison is the point of the sentence: the community is shown a way of speaking about loss and told that it does not belong to people of faith.

The Book often teaches by means of such contrasts, and the instruction here is about speech rather than about belief. What the community is told not to imitate is a habit of recording outcomes as though human decisions had controlled them, and the habit is presented as characteristic of the people who have not accepted the message.

**The Grammar Behind "On Account of Their Brethren"**

The commentators also identified the speakers in three ways: the disbelievers taken as a body, the hypocrites taken as a body, or ʿAbd Allāh ibn Ubayy in particular, whose conduct earlier in the sūrah the passage has already described (al-Quṭūbī; al-Rāzī). The Book does not settle which of the three is intended, and the omission suits the way the sūrah has handled similar remarks: the conduct is named, and the reader is left to recognise it in his own circle.

The prohibition also comes before the premise is answered. The sentences that follow turn to those slain in God's way, and the answer to the claim about staying behind is supplied there rather than in this verse.

---

The verse forbids a way of speaking about the dead, not the sorrow of loss, and it names the sentence precisely: that if the travellers and fighters had stayed behind, they would not have died. The sūrah answers the fatalism in the next verses on its own ground. No soul dies except by God's permission at an appointed time, and whoever desires this world is given it while whoever desires the Hereafter is given that (3:145); and the Book says elsewhere that wherever a person may be, death will reach him even in a fortified tower (4:78). What the verse removes is the implication that the dead died because they had gone out — the reasoning that turns a duty into a cause of death. The community is being taught that its grief is not to be expressed in a sentence that also accuses the men who obeyed.

## Verse 3:157

> Should you be martyred or die in the cause of Allah, then His forgiveness and mercy are far better than whatever ˹wealth˺ those ˹who stay behind˺ accumulate

**The Qualifier "In the Way of God"**

The promise attaches to a person who dies in the way of God, and the qualifier is the important element of the sentence. The Book uses the phrase for conduct undertaken for God's sake, and it uses it in the same way in its instructions about wealth and fighting.

**What the Word for Amassing Covers**

The sentence closes with a comparison between what God gives and what people gather, and Ibn Kathīr read the word for what is gathered as covering wealth and, more broadly, the accumulation of the world's vanities. The comparison is stated as a fact rather than as a recommendation: the believer is not told to despise what others collect, only to measure it against what is promised to those who die in God's cause. The verse's own subject is the death of the community's members, and the sentence answers the fear that those deaths were a loss. The fuller account of the state of the slain is given a dozen verses later, where the Book states that they are alive with their Lord and provided for (3:169), and the two passages are read together as a single answer to the same anxiety.

---

## Verse 3:158

> Whether you die or are martyred—all of you will be gathered before Allah

**The Book's Recurring Statement**

The Book repeats the sentence about the return to God in passages that speak about death. One states that every soul will taste death, that humanity is tested with good and with evil, and that all will be returned to God. Another states the same sequence in fewer words: every soul will taste death, and then to God all will be returned.

The sūrah of the Family of ʿImrān makes the same teaching in its own way in a later verse, where it states that every soul will taste death and that the full reward will be given on the Day of Judgment. The claim in this verse therefore belongs to the Book's basic instruction about human destiny, and the sūrah restates it at the point where the community needed to hear it most clearly.

**What "Amassing" Covers, and What Is Promised Instead**

The content of the promise is stated a few verses later, where the same people are described as alive with their Lord and provided for (3:169). The two statements work together: the sentence before the reader names what is better than the world's goods, and the later passage states what the better thing consists of. The community that had lost men at Uḥud is so answered twice, once by a comparison and once by a description, and the description is the one the passage dwells on.

---

## Verse 3:159

> It is out of Allah’s mercy that you ˹O Prophet˺ have been lenient with them. Had you been cruel or hard-hearted, they would have certainly abandoned you. So pardon them, ask Allah’s forgiveness for them, and consult with them in ˹conducting˺ matters. Once you make a decision, put your trust in Allah. Surely Allah loves those who trust in Him

**The Gentleness Attributed to Mercy**

The verse states that it was by a mercy from God that the Prophet was gentle with the community, and the sentence places his treatment of the men who had disobeyed within the working of divine mercy. The Prophet had every ground for harshness after the events of the day, and the verse names the quality that he showed instead.

**Consultation in the Battle's Own History**

The instruction to consult arrives after the community's most conspicuous failure, and the sūrah had described the consultation that preceded the battle. The Prophet had asked the Muslims of Madinah whether to remain in the city or to march out, and the decision to go out had prevailed.

The commentators note that the outcome of the fighting did not lead to the abandonment of the practice. The verse instructs the Prophet to seek the community's judgment in the very kind of matter that had just gone wrong, which teaches the community that a defeat is not a reason to abandon a sound method.

**The Other Sentences About His Gentleness**

The gentleness described in this verse is a theme the Book states in connection with the Prophet in several places. The sūrah of Repentance tells the community that a messenger has come to them from among themselves, one who is concerned by their suffering, anxious for their well-being, and gracious and merciful to the believers (9:128). The sūrah of the Heights gives the command in its shortest form: be gracious, command what is right, and turn away from those who act ignorantly (7:199).

**What the Verse Commands**
The verse gives four instructions in one sentence: the gentleness that came from mercy, the command to pardon the community and ask forgiveness for them, consultation with them in the matter, and the commitment to trust God once the decision is made. Each one belongs to the aftermath of Uḥud, and the commander who had been disobeyed was being told what to do with the men who disobeyed him. The Book names mercy as the source of the gentleness, and it makes the gentleness the reason the men stayed with him: had he been harsh and hard-hearted, they would have dispersed from around him.
**The Consultation Before the Battle**
The sūrah has already described the consultation. The Prophet called the companions together and asked whether they should meet the enemy outside Madinah or wait, and the younger men pressed for the open ground, and he went with their view against his own. The next verse names the men who broke ranks, and this one tells the leader what to do with the result. The community drew from the passage the practice of consultation in matters of public decision, and the Sūrah of the Consultation names it as one of the marks of the believers, who decide their affairs by counsel among themselves (42:38).
**What the Schools Took From It**
The jurists used the verse as a ground for the ruler's consultation with the people of knowledge and experience, and they noted the order in the sentence: pardon, then ask forgiveness for them, then consult, then decide and trust. The Shāfiʿī and other writers held that the leader may act on his own decision after the consultation, since the verse's last clause hands the outcome to God. The Prophet's own practice was mixed, taking counsel in the campaigns and in matters of the community, and the Book's instruction here is the only place where a messenger is commanded to consult those he is leading.
**The Trust at the End**
The last clause, that those who put their trust in God are the ones He loves, is repeated in the same words in the Sūrah of the Family of ʿImrān at 3:159 and in the Sūrah of the Spoils (8:61), and it closes the sequence. The community had decided, acted and suffered; the Book gives them the next decision and the frame to make it in. The verse is one of the sūrah's clearest statements that a defeat does not suspend the work, and that the same men who had to be pardoned were the ones whose advice was to be taken.

---

## Verse 3:160

> If Allah helps you, none can defeat you. But if He denies you help, then who else can help you? So in Allah let the believers put their trust

**The Two Conditions Stated Together**

The verse sets the help of God against His withholding of help, and it states the consequence of each. If God helps the community, nothing can overcome it, and if He abandons it, no one else can take His place.

**The Truth of the First Clause**

The community had been helped at Badr with everything the two clauses describe, and it had been shown at Uḥud what happens when the connection is broken. The sentence does not promise that every engagement will end in victory; it states who controls the outcome, and it leaves the community's responsibilities where the earlier verses placed them.

**The Reading That Sees the Two Battles**

The commentators frequently read the two clauses of this verse as a description of the community's recent history: God helped the believers at Badr, and He withheld His help at Uḥud. The verse would then be a statement of the difference between the two days rather than a conditional promise.

The reading gives the sentence a particular force for its first listeners, who had lived through both days within a year. The sūrah had already explained the change in the community's own conduct, and this verse states the result in help received or withheld, without adding any qualification that would soften the contrast.

**The Second Reading of the Closing Phrase**

The phrase rendered as after Him, or after that, can be read in two ways. Some take it as referring to time, so that the question is who could help after God has withheld His help. Others take the pronoun as referring to God Himself, so that the sense is who could help apart from Him.

**The Chain of Statements About Help in This Sūrah**

This sentence is the last in a series of statements about divine assistance that runs through the chapter, and reading them together shows how the argument has been built. The passage about Badr promises reinforcement from the angels and attaches it to the community's patience (3:124–25). The statement that God helped at Badr when the community was weak follows shortly after (3:123), and the reminder that victory comes from God alone closes the same section (3:126). Later in the account of Uḥud, the community is told that God had fulfilled His promise to them and that some of them wavered (3:152).

---

## Verse 3:161

> It is not appropriate for a prophet to illegally withhold spoils of war. And whoever does so, it will be held against them on the Day of Judgment. Then every soul will be paid in full for what it has done, and none will be wronged

**The Statement About the Prophet**

The verse declares that it is not for any prophet to defraud, and the sentence is a defence of the Prophet's integrity in a matter where it had been questioned. The sūrah addresses the subject in the aftermath of a battle, when property had changed hands and accusations were circulating.

The commentators connect the verse with the distribution of the spoils of war and with reports that an item of value had gone missing, after which some people suggested that the Prophet himself might have taken it. Al-Ṭabarī's preferred reading is that the verse was revealed simply to inform people that such a thing does not happen with a prophet.

**What the Verb Covers**

The verb rendered as defraud is used specifically for taking part of what should be shared out, and the commentators treated the verse as dealing with the spoils above all. Some held that it concerned a valuable piece of cloth lost after one of the battles, and others that it concerned the failure to distribute spoils equitably.

---

The offence the verse names is *ghulūl*, taking something of the spoils before the leader has distributed them, and it carries weight because the community had just disputed over property after Uḥud. The ruling that emerges from the verse and the sūrah of the Spoils together is that the spoils belong to the community: they are for Allah and His Messenger to distribute (8:1), and the Qur'an tells the Prophet to take from them and to eat "what you have taken, lawful and good" (8:69) once the apportionment is made. The verse's sentence about the Day of Judgement adds the sanction: what is taken improperly is not simply unpaid but returned, and the person carries it with him into the account. The defence of the Prophet's personal integrity is the verse's other work — the accusation that had been whispered is answered not by an assurance about his wealth but by a statement about what is not possible for the office he holds.

## Verse 3:162

> Are those who seek Allah’s pleasure like those who deserve Allah’s wrath? Hell is their home. What an evil destination

**A Question That Places Two Figures Side by Side**

The verse asks whether a person who pursues the contentment of God is like a person who earns a burden of anger from God and whose refuge is the fire. The question expects a negative answer, and the whole sentence is built to make the difference visible.

**The Aim as a Way of Life**

The verse describes a life in what it is pursuing, and the description applies to the ordinary conduct of a person as much as to a decisive choice. The Book's word for contentment covers the approval that a person seeks in what he does, and the pursuit of it gives shape to the ordinary details of a person's dealings.

The community at Madinah had just been through an examination of exactly this kind. The spoils, the positions on the hill, and the question of who took what were all part of an afternoon in which the community's aims had shown themselves, and the verse that sets the two aims side by side is written for people who had seen them both at work.

---

## Verse 3:163

> They ˹each˺ have varying degrees in the sight of Allah. And Allah is All-Seeing of what they do

**The Statement of Rank**

The verse states that the two groups are ranked in degrees with God, and it closes with the statement that God sees what they do. The sentence answers the question posed in the verse before it by denying the equality the question had proposed.

**Ranks and the Book's Teaching About Reward**

The Book states repeatedly that people will be dealt with according to what they have done, and the language of degrees is one of its ways of expressing the principle. One passage states that each will be assigned ranks according to his deeds, and another states that God will pay every soul in full for what it has earned.

**The Two Verses That State Graduation Plainly**

The principle the sentence assumes is stated in two other places without the image of rank. The sūrah of the Cattle says that each person will be assigned ranks according to his deeds, and it closes with the reminder that God is not unaware of what people do (6:132). The sūrah of the Sand Dunes states that the two groups will be ranked according to what they have done, so that every one may be repaid in full, and it ends with the declaration that no one will be wronged (46:19).

Those sentences use the vocabulary of degrees for reward and punishment alike, which shows that the Book does not divide humanity into two flat categories. The verse before the reader applies the same scale to the contrast it has just drawn, and the reading recorded by al-Ṭabarī takes the ranking at its simplest level: even in this world, the two groups are not on the same footing. The Book's consistent claim is that the difference people see now is a shadow of the difference that will be assigned later.

---

## Verse 3:164

> Indeed, Allah has done the believers a ˹great˺ favour by raising a messenger from among them—reciting to them His revelations, purifying them, and teaching them the Book and wisdom. For indeed they had previously been clearly astray

**The Favour Named at the Outset**

The verse states that God conferred a favour on the believers by sending them a messenger from among themselves, and the sentence places the coming of the Prophet at the head of everything the community had received. The word for favour covers a gift and an act of kindness, and the Book uses it for the blessings that carry the greatest weight.

**A Messenger From Among Themselves**

The phrase describing the messenger as one of the community's own was the point that the community's opponents found hardest to accept. The Book reports the objections of those who asked whether they should follow a human being like themselves, and the reasoning behind their refusal is recorded in several passages.

The verse presents the very fact they objected to as the favour. A messenger drawn from the community could speak its language, know its conditions, and share its life, and the Book elsewhere describes the Prophet as concerned for the suffering of the believers and merciful toward them. The gift consisted partly in the closeness of the messenger to the people he was sent to.

**What the Favour Is**
The verse names the sending of a messenger from among the community as the favour of God to those who believe, and it names what the messenger did among them: he recites the signs, he purifies them, and he teaches them the Book and the wisdom, and before that they were in plain error. The Book states the same sequence in the Sūrah of the Cow in the prayer of Abraham for a messenger from among his descendants (2:129), and the Sūrah of Friday quotes the passage when it addresses the community with the same account (62:2). The four verbs give the whole work of the prophet: recitation, purification, teaching of the Book and teaching of the wisdom.
**What the Sūrah Does With It**
The passage comes a little after the account of the men of Badr and a little before the account of Uḥud, and the Book is telling the community what it had been given before it reminds them of what it had done. The verses that follow warn against hoarding (3:180) and state the alternations of fortune among people (3:140), and the verse in front of us sits among them as a statement of the favour that none of the losses could take away. The sūrah's arrangement gives the reader the gift and then the duty, which is the order the Book uses whenever it is about to correct a community.
**The Community's Own Reading**
The scholars took the phrase about purifying as the base of the discipline of the self, and the phrase about the wisdom as the ground for the traditions of the Prophet used alongside the Book. The report in the collections about the Prophet's teaching the companions, and the practice of the community's teachers of reciting the Book to the people and explaining it, are both read from this verse. The passage tells the community that the recitation, the teaching and the formation of character are one work, and it puts the Book and the wisdom in the same sentence.

---

## Verse 3:165

> Why is it when you suffered casualties ˹at Uḥud˺—although you had made your enemy suffer twice as much ˹at Badr˺—you protested, “How could this be?”? Say, ˹O Prophet,˺ “It is because of your disobedience.” Surely Allah is Most Capable of everything

**The Conduct That Brought the Loss**

The commentators identify the conduct in various ways that all point to the same events. Some held that the community was afflicted because it had ransomed the prisoners of Badr rather than putting them to death, obtaining worldly wealth in exchange for a military advantage. Others held that the cause was the disobedience of the archers, whether by leaving their positions, pursuing the spoils, or fleeing later in the day.

Al-Rāzī and al-Ṭabarī record these views without settling on one. What the explanations share is that the loss followed from decisions the community had made, and the verse's answer holds all of them: the affliction came from the community's own side.

**The Arithmetic Behind the Claim of Twice Over**

The comparison in the sentence was given numbers by the commentators.

The commentators also connected the verse with two explanations offered for why the loss came when it did. Some held that the community was afflicted at Uḥud because it ransomed the prisoners of Badr instead of settling the matter there, and others because of the disobedience described in the preceding passages. The verse itself states the comparison and leaves those reports as reports.

**The Verse and the Argument It Answers**
The verse asks the community why, when a wound touched them, they said that this had come from where. The Book's answer is that it came from themselves, and the whole passage that follows explains what that means. The sūrah has already named the fault of the archers who left their post and the desire for the spoils in some of the men, and it has forgiven the men who held. The verse in front of us closes the argument by refusing the community the comfort of blaming God for a loss it had brought on itself.
**What the Commentators Named**
The older commentators listed the causes, and they began with the choice to leave Madinah and meet the army in the open, against the advice of the Prophet. Ibn Kathīr and al-Qurṭubī add the division among the men, the men who said the Prophet should have listened to ʿAbdullāh ibn Ubayy, and the archers who abandoned the pass. The Book uses the same construction elsewhere when it tells the community that what comes to it of good is from God and what comes of evil is from itself (4:79), and the Sūrah of Consultation names the same rule about disasters (42:30).
**What the Verse Is Not Saying**
The Book does not say the men who died were being punished. The sūrah names them as martyrs in the same passage (3:169-171), and the Prophet's word about the martyrs of Uḥud names a reward that the community still repeated in its books. The verse's sentence about the wound is addressed to the survivors and to the questions they were asking in the mosque, and the Book answers the questions and keeps the honour of the dead in the same passage.

---

## Verse 3:166

> So what you suffered on the day the two armies met was by Allah’s Will, so that He might distinguish the ˹true˺ believers

**The Wound Attributed to God's Permission**

The verse states that what befell the community on the day the two hosts met happened by God's permission, and it adds that the event served the purpose of making the believers known. The sentence is the sūrah's summary of the battle's place in the divine arrangement.

**The Day the Two Hosts Met**

The phrase naming the two hosts is the sūrah's standard way of referring to Uḥud, and it appears in the passage about those who turned away. The wording keeps both sides of the engagement in view and presents the battle as a meeting of two bodies rather than as a disaster that struck the community alone.

The Book's naming of the event in this way keeps its readers from reading the day as an isolated misfortune. What happened was a battle between two gathered forces, and the fact that one of them suffered more than it expected is stated in the same passage that explains why.

---

## Verse 3:167

> and expose the hypocrites. When it was said to them, “Come fight in the cause of Allah or ˹at least˺ defend yourselves,” they replied, “If we had known there was fighting, we would have definitely gone with you.” They were closer to disbelief than to belief on that day—for saying with their mouths what was not in their hearts. Allah is All-Knowing of what they hide

**How Their Answer Has Been Read**

Al-Rāzī considered two ways of taking the group's statement. On one reading they genuinely believed that no fighting would take place and expected only a standoff. On the other, they expected that if anything happened it would not be a battle but a massacre, and their departure was prompted by the certainty of defeat.

Both readings, al-Rāzī observes, describe an attitude that the verse calls hypocritical rather than sincere. The group had joined the expedition and then left it on the basis of a private calculation, and the sūrah places that calculation among the things the day had brought to light.

**The Withdrawal and the Account Around It**

The verse's naming of the group connects two points in the sūrah's narrative. The withdrawal it refers to is the departure of ʿAbd Allāh ibn Ubayy with about three hundred men before the fighting began, the event the passage on the morning of the battle has already described (al-Quṭūbī; al-Rāzī). Their absence thinned the community's ranks at the moment when numbers mattered most.

---

The verse records the excuse of the men who stayed behind, asking by which party they meant their words (3:166): that if fighting had been certain they would have joined. The Qur'an's answer stands in the words that follow: they were present, the danger was visible, and the statement is answered by the next verse's charge that they said of their brothers while they sat at home that they would not have died had they obeyed the Prophet, which is why the sūrah tells the Prophet to say that they should prevent death from themselves if it is true. The commentators noted that the man who says such a thing about his dead brothers has exposed more about himself than about them, and the verse's portrait is completed by Al-Rāzī's two readings of the sentence: either they had expected no battle at all, or they had expected a slaughter they wanted no part of. Both readings make the same point, that their judgement was shaped by where they had decided to stand.

**What the Group Did and What They Said**
The verse describes the men who stayed behind at Uḥud and told their brothers that if they had obeyed them they would not have been killed, and it answers them with a command to the Prophet: then prevent death from yourselves, if you are truthful. The verse before it names the same men as the ones who were told to fight and did not, and the passage that follows turns to the martyrs and their living state with God (3:169-171). The Book puts the doubters and the dead in the same stretch, and the arrangement is the answer.
**What the Commentators Discussed**
Al-Rāzī considered the two ways of taking the group's statement, and the two are worth setting out. On one reading the men genuinely believed that a different decision would have saved the dead, and their words were an error of judgement rather than a lie; on the other, the words were a way of criticising the Prophet's decision after the fact, which the sūrah answers directly. The reports connect the group with ʿAbdullāh ibn Ubayy and with the men who turned back before the battle, and the Book does not identify them because the words outlived the men.
**What the Verse Settles**
The command to prevent death by their own efforts closes the argument, since the men were claiming that a safer decision would have preserved lives that God had already appointed. The Sūrah of the Family of ʿImrān answers the same claim in the verse about the term that is written (3:145), and it states the honour of the dead in the passage that follows. The Book's method in the passage is to answer a claim about tactics with a claim about the decree, and it does not leave the dead to be used as an argument.

## Verse 3:168

> Those who sat at home, saying about their brothers, “Had they listened to us, they would not have been killed.” Say, ˹O Prophet,˺ “Try not to die if what you say is true!”

**The Description of the Speakers**

The verse describes the speakers as sitting at home while they spoke about their brothers, and the participle marks their position throughout. They were not among those who had gone out.

The commentators identified them as the hypocrites, and some connected the verse with the leader of that group and his companions. The comment on this passage also notes that the brothers in question could be blood relatives as well as brothers in the faith, and the grief of the community at Uḥud touched many households. The claim the verse reports was therefore being made about people who were close to the speakers as well as about fellow believers.

**The Answer That Comes Two Verses Later**

The Book uses the same method elsewhere. It tells people that death will overcome them wherever they are, even in fortified towers, and then takes up their habit of attributing good and evil to the wrong source (4:78). The point in both passages is that the try to control an outcome by avoiding a place or a decision is an illusion, since the term is set and the location does not change it. The talk of the group in this verse is answered, therefore, not by denying that men die but by stating what death delivers them into.

---

## Verse 3:169

> Never think of those martyred in the cause of Allah as dead. In fact, they are alive with their Lord, well provided for—

**The Statement That They Are Alive**

The verse states that they are alive with their Lord, and the commentators discussed what kind of life is meant. The Book's language is used in the passage about the martyrs in another sūrah, where the believers are told not to say that those killed in God's cause are dead, since they are alive, though the community does not perceive it.

The two verses together give the teaching its shape. The life is real and it is not perceptible to the community that remains, and the phrase about being with their Lord locates it in a state that the Book describes without setting out its stages. The community is not told how to picture the state; it is told the fact that governs how it should speak.

**The Requests of the Slain**

The Book's account of those who died in God's cause includes the report that they ask to be sent back to the world so that they may die again in His way, a report that al-Rāzī and al-Ṭabarī record from the traditions attached to this passage. The request is the clearest evidence of the state the verse describes, since a person content with what he has received would not ask to exchange it for a repetition of the experience that brought him there. What the next verse adds, that they rejoice in those who have not yet joined them, belongs to the same picture. The community in this world is told that its dead are aware of it, and the warning about the bars between the living and the dead is answered by the two sentences rather than by any claim about how the awareness works.

---

## Verse 3:170

> rejoicing in Allah’s bounties and being delighted for those yet to join them. There will be no fear for them, nor will they grieve

**The Pair About Fear and Grief**

The verse closes with the statement that no fear shall come upon them and no grief shall be theirs. The pair of terms appears throughout the Book as the description of the condition of those who are secure with God, and it answers the two anxieties that the community at Madinah was living with.

The Book repeats the promise to the believers, to those who follow guidance, and to those who do good in several passages. The two terms cover the future and the past: fear is the apprehension of what is coming, and grief is the weight of what has already happened. The verse announces that the men in question have been delivered from both.

**The Book's Uses of the Pair**

The promise that no fear will come upon a group and that they will not grieve appears in the Book's descriptions of the believers, of those who follow the guidance of God, and of the people of the earlier scriptures who believed and did good. The pairing is one of the Book's standard forms of assurance.

---

## Verse 3:171

> They are joyful for receiving Allah’s grace and bounty, and that Allah does not deny the reward of the believers

**The Continuation of the Description**

The verse continues the sentence about those who were killed in the way of God, and it adds a further element to the state described in the two verses before it.
