The men are said to be rejoicing in a blessing and a bounty from God, and the sentence closes with a statement about the reward of the believers in general.

**The Report About the Slain of Badr**

The commentary tradition records that after the battle of Badr the Prophet addressed the enemy's dead and asked whether they had found what their Lord had promised them to be true. That when he was asked why he spoke to the dead, he replied that they could not hear him any better than the dead could.

The report is part of the tradition's evidence for the awareness of the departed, and it is placed alongside these verses in the commentaries. What the verse before the reader establishes is the state of the believers who died in God's cause, and the reports belong to the same subject in the community's teaching.

---

The rejoicing is the mark of the martyrs' state in the Qur'an, and this verse is the second panel of the picture begun three verses earlier: they are alive with their Lord, provided for, rejoicing in what God has given them, and glad for the believers who have not yet joined them. The Book states the same elsewhere — "never say that those martyred in the cause of Allah are dead; in fact, they are alive, but you do not perceive it" (2:154) — and holds the whole matter in one of the sūrah's most sweeping promises, that God has bought from the believers their lives and their wealth in exchange for the Garden (9:111). The verse's closing sentence, that Allah does not deny the reward of the believers, is the sūrah's way of extending the promise beyond those killed: the same account is kept for everyone who works.

## Verse 3:172

> ˹As for˺ those who responded to the call of Allah and His Messenger after their injury, those of them who did good and were mindful ˹of Allah˺ will have a great reward

**The Call and the Place of Assembly**

The reports describe the community assembling despite its condition, with the Prophet himself among the wounded, and marching a few miles from Madinah to a place called Ḥamrāʾ al-Asad. The force was smaller than the one that had fought the day before and in worse condition. The reports note that the men who answered the call included those who had been injured the day before, and that the place they reached lay several miles out on the road the enemy army had taken.

**The Arrangement That Prevented Another Battle**

The reports describe the steps the Prophet took to prevent the Makkans from turning back toward Madinah. A man from a tribe with which the Prophet had a treaty was sent to tell the Makkans that the Madinans were pursuing them in far greater numbers than before, now including all those who had stayed behind during the fighting.

The community also lit a great many campfires at night within sight of the Makkan camp, so that the enemy would judge its numbers from the fires. The Makkans abandoned their intention to return, and the sūrah reports the outcome in the verse that follows this one. The arrangement shows how much of the affair turned on what each side believed about the other, and the community's own uncertainty was answered by the success of the ruse.

---

## Verse 3:173

> Those who were warned, “Your enemies have mobilized their forces against you, so fear them,” the warning only made them grow stronger in faith and they replied, “Allah ˹alone˺ is sufficient ˹as an aid˺ for us and ˹He˺ is the best Protector.”

**The Warning That Reached Them**

The verse reports what people said to the community on the road: that a force had gathered against them, and that they should be afraid. The commentators identify the speakers in two ways, either travellers who had passed the Makkan contingent and carried the news on to Madinah, or the hypocrites who had been discouraging the community from fighting all along.

The content of the message is a report of the enemy's strength. The men receiving it were wounded, fewer in number than before, and in no condition to meet a renewed attack, and the report was designed to produce the reaction the verse's next clause denies.

**The Prayer in the Community's Practice**

The declaration that God suffices and is an excellent guardian has been used by Muslims at the outset of difficulties, and the Book contains it in more than one place. The sūrah of the Prophets and the sūrah about Repentance both use the same formula in different situations.

---

The verse belongs to the aftermath of Uḥud, when word reached the Muslims that the Makkan force — or Abū Sufyān's party on the road — was gathering a larger army to finish the campaign, and the warning was intended to frighten them into inaction. Their answer is the sentence the verse preserves: "Allah is sufficient for us, and He is the best disposer of affairs." The phrase occurs in the Qur'an only here. The sequel is given two verses later: they returned with grace and bounty from God, no harm touched them, and they followed what pleased Him (3:174). The confidence was not a soldier's bravado. It rests on the principle the sūrah states elsewhere in the Book of the same community: "Nothing will ever befall us except what Allah has destined for us; He is our Protector, and in Allah let the believers put their trust" (9:51).

## Verse 3:174

> So they returned with Allah’s favours and grace, suffering no harm. For they sought to please Allah. And surely Allah is ˹the˺ Lord of infinite bounty

**The Return Described**

The verse states that the community returned with blessing and bounty from God and that no harm had touched it, and it adds that the men had pursued the contentment of God. The sentence describes the outcome of the expedition that the previous verses introduced.

Al-Ṭabarī explains that the community came back unharmed by the enemy, since no second battle took place. The sentence supplies the outcome of the decision reported in the previous verse, and it does so in the language of gift rather than of achievement. On the reading that connects the passage with the appointment at Badr a year later, the return was from that meeting, where no fighting occurred at all. Both readings describe a homecoming without losses.

**Pursuing the Contentment of God**

The phrase about pursuing God's contentment appears earlier in the sūrah, where the verse contrasts the person who seeks it with the one who earns anger, and the two verses are connected by the same words. The men who went out after Uḥud are described by their aim, and the aim is the one the sūrah has named as the mark of the righteous.

---

## Verse 3:175

> That ˹warning˺ was only ˹from˺ Satan, trying to prompt you to fear his followers. So do not fear them; fear Me if you are ˹true˺ believers

**The Warning Traced to Its Source**

The verse identifies the fear that had been stirred up in the community as the work of Satan, who frightens his friends. The report that reached the community on the road was presented as information about the enemy, and the verse states what was behind it.

The commentators read the sentence as a statement about how the warning worked rather than only about who delivered it. Al-Qurṭūbī and al-Ṭabarī explain that Satan inspires the disbelievers to frighten the believers, so that the community's resolve would fail before its opponents had done anything. Naming the source is the Book's way of reducing a threat to its true size, and the sentence does this by identifying the influence behind the report rather than by disputing the details of the report itself.

**Satan's Friends**

The phrase describing the friends of Satan identifies those whose cause the fear serves. The Book uses the expression in its description of the people who follow their impulses and forget the remembrance of God, and it describes them as a party that is bound to lose.

---

## Verse 3:176

> ˹O Prophet!˺ Do not grieve for those who race to disbelieve—surely they will not harm Allah in the least. It is Allah’s Will to disallow them a share in the Hereafter, and they will suffer a tremendous punishment

**The Consolation Addressed to the Prophet**

The verse tells the Prophet not to let those who rush toward disbelief cause him grief, and it then states that their conduct does not harm God in the least. The instruction follows a long passage about the community's own failures, and it turns the Prophet's attention to another group whose opposition was personal to him.

The Book addresses the Prophet's distress at the disbelief of others in several places, and the commentators gathered the passages together. One sūrah tells him not to let the conduct of those who deny cause him grief, another tells him not to let their speech cause him sorrow, and a third asks whether he will consume himself in regret over people who will not believe. The verses form a body of consolation addressed to the same experience.

**Who the Verse Concerns**

The commentators identified the group in more than one way. Al-Rāzī read the phrase as referring to the idolaters of Makkah, who were the Prophet's own kin and who worked hardest against him. Al-Qurṭūbī included the People of the Book, who had a scripture of their own and were expected to recognise what came after it, and the hypocrites, who claimed adherence while working against the community.

---

The consolation is repeated in the next verse with the same words, that those who trade disbelief for faith will not harm God in the least (3:177), and the chapter has already used the pattern for the Prophet's grief twice: he is told not to lose heart and not to grieve for the men who had turned back (3:139, 3:153). The promise attached to this verse — that God intends not to give them a share in the Hereafter, and theirs is a great punishment — is the same statement the sūrah makes about the people of Uḥud who abandoned the field and the community, and the two cases are handled the same way: the offence is named, the loss is placed with the offender, and the Prophet is told his sorrow is not owed to people who will not accept it. The men who raced toward disbelief were not harming God's cause but their own record.

## Verse 3:177

> Those who trade belief for disbelief will never harm Allah in the least, and they will suffer a painful punishment

**The Image of a Trade**

The verse describes those who have purchased disbelief at the price of belief, and it states that they will not harm God and that a painful punishment awaits them. The commercial image is one the Book uses frequently for moral choice.

The commentators drew attention to the pattern. One sūrah describes people who trade guidance for misguidance and observes that the trade is profitless. Another describes the same exchange and adds that the traders are persistent in pursuing the fire. A third, on the same image, describes those who purchase the present life at the price of the next. The Book's use of the market as a picture of a person's decisions is one of its characteristic ways of explaining consequences.

The verse's exchange is between belief and disbelief, and the order of the words is important. What the group had is named as belief, and what they took in its place is named as disbelief.

The Book's descriptions of the same transaction usually mention what was given up in the process: guidance, forgiveness, the next life. The sentence in this verse is brief, and the brevity makes the loss clear without adding anything. What was given up was the more valuable of the two, and the sentence assumes that any reader knows which is which.

---

## Verse 3:178

> Those who disbelieve should not think that living longer is good for them. They are only given more time to increase in sin, and they will suffer a humiliating punishment

**The Respite and the Misreading**

The verse warns those who deny not to suppose that the delay they are granted is good for them, and it states that the delay allows them to increase in sin. The sentence addresses a conclusion that people naturally draw when they observe the prosperous among the wicked.

The community had watched its opponents return home in good order while its own dead were being buried, and the sight raised the question the verse answers. The verse explains what such survival amounts to, and it does so in terms that remove the advantage a person might read into it.

The commentators explain the respite as the granting of more life, in place of dying or being killed. The Makkans who had escaped the fighting were given additional years, and the sentence treats those years as the subject of the warning.

**The Reading About the Battle's Outcome**

One of the interpretations recorded in the commentaries applies the verse to the outcome of the fighting. On that reading, those who deny should not think that their survival at Uḥud was better than the deaths of the men who fought in God's way, since all their survival achieved was further life in a corrupt state.

**The Punishment That Is a Condition, Not Only a Consequence**

The Book describes the matter in those terms elsewhere. It states that God turns the hearts and eyes of such people away as they refused to believe at first, leaving them to wander in their defiance (6:110), and it describes a sealed heart as the state of those who have chosen what they chose (2:7). The sentence in this verse supplies the purpose clause that makes the outcome explicit: the respite is given so that they may increase in sin.

The verse that follows this one repeats the judgment in another form, saying that God's will is to deny such people any share in the Hereafter (3:176). Read together, the two sentences leave no room for the reading the verse is warning against, since the advantage they appear to have in this world is described as a subtraction from what comes after it.

---

## Verse 3:179

> Allah would not leave the believers in the condition you were in, until He distinguished the good from the evil ˹among you˺. Nor would Allah ˹directly˺ reveal to you the unseen, but He chooses whoever He wills as a messenger. So believe in Allah and His messengers. And if you are faithful and mindful ˹of Allah˺, you will receive a great reward

**The Unseen Not Disclosed**

The verse states that God will not inform the community of the unseen, and the commentators explain this as a statement that God does not reveal what lies in a person's heart. The community would not be given a list of names; it would be given occasions on which character showed itself.

**The Book's Teaching About Testing**

The verse's statement that the separation happens is amplified elsewhere in the sūrah. The Book asks the community whether it imagined that it would reach the garden with no one having been shown to have struggled and endured, and the account of the battle shows the sorting that the sentence describes happening in the course of one afternoon.

Both al-Rāzī and al-Ṭabarī tie the sentence to the campaign itself, where the pressure of the day brought out what people were. The Book's way of dealing with a community is to let conduct bring to light what a report from the unseen would have settled only by coercion.

---

The verse answers a hope that the community might have held, that God would simply disclose who among them was sincere, and it denies that they will be given a list of names. The next sentence explains the method instead: God chooses from His messengers whom He wills, and the rest are tested. The Book states the same law at the testing of the community: "Do people think once they say, 'We believe' that they will be left without being put to the test? We certainly tested those before them, and in this way Allah will clearly distinguish between those who are truthful and those who are liars" (29:2–3). Uḥud was the instrument of the distinction this verse describes: it exposed the men who would fight and the men who would turn back, and it did so in the open, before the community's eyes rather than in a book about hearts.

## Verse 3:180

> And do not let those who ˹greedily˺ withhold Allah’s bounties think it is good for them—in fact, it is bad for them! They will be leashed ˹by their necks˺ on the Day of Judgment with whatever ˹wealth˺ they used to withhold. And Allah is the ˹sole˺ inheritor of the heavens and the earth. And Allah is All-Aware of what you do

**The Warning to the Miserly**

The verse tells those who are miserly with what God has given them not to suppose that their holding back is good for them, and it states that it is evil for them. The sentence deals with a calculation that people make about their property, and it reverses the calculation's conclusion.

**Material Wealth and Spiritual Knowledge**

The commentators read the verse as covering both material wealth and the knowledge that a person is given, and al-Ṭabarī applied it specifically to the People of the Book who refused to teach what they had received. The verse's wording about the bounty of God supports the wider application.

The two kinds of withholding share a structure. In each case a person keeps something that was given for a purpose beyond his own comfort, and in each case the holding is presented in the Book as a loss to the holder rather than as a gain. The passage about the earlier community's scholars is the clearest instance the sūrah's first readers would have recognised. The sūrah's readers are expected to examine their own case along both lines.

---

The image at the end is exact: what was withheld is made into a leash and fastened to the neck that refused to give it, so that the wealth a person hoarded becomes the thing that drags him. The Prophet ﷺ is reported to have described that treatment and to have recited this verse in that connection (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī). The same threat is stated in the sūrah of Repentance for those who hoard gold and silver and do not spend them in God's way: the metals are heated in the fire of Hell and pressed to their foreheads, flanks and backs (9:34–35). The verse's place in the chapter adds a second edge. It comes directly after the charge against the People of the Book who sold their covenant for a price, so the warning covers more than money: any bounty held back from its purpose — knowledge, position, wealth — is the withholding the verse has in view, and its owner is the one who will carry it.

## Verse 3:181

> Indeed, Allah has heard those ˹among the Jews˺ who said, “Allah is poor; we are rich!” We have certainly recorded their slurs and their killing of prophets unjustly. Then We will say, “Taste the torment of burning

**The Punishment Named**

The sentence closes with a command addressed to the group on the Day of Judgment: taste the punishment of the burning. The Book uses the imperative form in its descriptions of the treatment of those who denied, and the command is spoken by the one whose words they had mocked.

**The Answer the Book Gives Elsewhere**

The claim that God is poor and the community rich is answered directly in another sūrah, where the Book states that God is the Rich and the people are the poor (47:38). The two statements use the same vocabulary, which is why the commentators treated the taunt as a deliberate reversal of a verse the community had been reciting. The words in question came from the passage that asks who will lend God a goodly loan, and the loan language had been used by the revelation itself; the taunt took that language literally and turned it into mockery. The sentence before the reader records it as speech God has heard, and the answer to it is not a correction of the claim about wealth but the statement of what the speakers have earned.

**What the Verse Answers**
The verse follows the one before it, where the men who had been given the Book are described as saying that God is poor and they are rich (3:181 continues from the same passage), and it reports that God has heard them. The Book then names their other offence: they killed the prophets without right, and it answers both with the sentence the verse carries, that they will taste the punishment of the burning. The passage is one of the sūrah's hardest addresses to a group it names, and the Book records the words that provoked it.
**What the Commentators Recorded**
The older commentators connected the sentence about God being poor with the community's need in Madinah and with the appeals that had been made to the Jewish community for help, and they reported the exchange that followed. The Book's answer is not an argument but a record, and the next verses state that God has written it down and that the day will come. The sūrah then turns to the mercy of God and the entry into the gardens (3:182 in the same passage), and the arrangement keeps the warning and the invitation in the same stretch.
**Where the Passage Sits**
The verses around it deal with the men who broke their covenant, who distorted the Book, and who claimed a special standing with God, and the sūrah has been answering each of them in turn. The verse in front of us closes the passage with the sentence the condemned will hear, and it uses the words the men themselves had used, which is the Book's habit: the answer to a claim arrives in the same language the claim was made in.

---

## Verse 3:182

> This is ˹the reward˺ for what your hands have done. And Allah is never unjust to ˹His˺ creation.”

**What the Hands Have Sent Ahead**

The phrase about what the hands have sent ahead appears in other passages of the Book, and its image is of deeds that precede the person who will meet them. One sūrah states that a person will be told that the treatment received is for what his hands have done, and another uses the same formula in a context of punishment.

**The Reason Stated**

The verse explains the punishment announced in the sentence before it by pointing to what the people in question had themselves sent ahead, and by stating that God does not wrong His servants. The sentence is short and functions as the justification of what has just been described.

The Book uses the image to make a person's responsibility concrete. What is done in this life is not left behind when a person moves on; it goes ahead of him, and the sentence in this verse treats the punishment of the group as something they had dispatched with their own conduct.

---

The sentence the verse quotes is a formula of judgement, and the Qur'an places it almost identically at two other points — "This is the reward for what your hands have done, and Allah is never unjust to His creation" (8:51; 22:10) — each time over the people of the Fire. The phrase "what your hands have sent ahead" pictures deeds walking in front of the person to the meeting with their record. The verse is the last word given in the chapter to the men who said that nothing they did could bring them harm and that God had not warned them (3:181), and the answer is that the account is exact: nothing is added to a punishment and nothing is taken from a reward. The same standard protects the believers, and the chapter has already applied it to the trials of the community itself (3:165).

## Verse 3:183

> Those ˹are the same people˺ who say, “Allah has commanded us not to believe in any messenger unless he brings us an offering to be consumed by fire ˹from the sky˺.” Say, ˹O Prophet,˺ “Other prophets did in fact come to you before me with clear proofs and ˹even˺ what you demanded—why then did you kill them, if what you say is true?”

**The Historical Challenge**

The commentators describe the occasion as a challenge that prominent members of the Jewish community in Madinah put to the Prophet. They asked him to produce a sacrificial offering that fire from heaven would consume, presenting it as the criterion they had always required.

The commentaries observe that fire descending on an offering is described in their own scriptures in connection with a number of prophets, and they list the occasions. The challenge therefore drew on a tradition the group knew, and the verse's reply treats it as a criterion that had been applied selectively.

**The Verse's Connection With the Earlier Statements**

The verse follows the taunt about God's poverty and the general statement about what deeds are recorded, and it continues the sūrah's account of the community's opponents. The two passages together describe the same quarter in the same posture.

One of the readings recorded in the commentaries applies the verse to the inner life, describing the soul's refusal to follow the way and its production of excuses designed to satisfy itself. On this reading the demand for a spectacular sign is the mind's way of postponing a decision it has already made.

The reading is offered as an application rather than as the verse's plain sense, and it has been useful in the community's teaching about self-deception. The verse's own reply is a matter of history, and the application draws the same lesson from it for anyone who sets conditions on what he will accept, since a delay in accepting what is evident is usually a matter of the will rather than of the evidence.

**The Fire From Heaven in the Earlier Books**

The sign the group demanded has a history in their own scriptures, and the commentaries mention the passages that contain it. Fire came down from heaven on the offering of Elijah on Mount Carmel, when the people looked on and fell on their faces (1 Kings 18). Fire fell on the threshing floor that David purchased and on the altar Solomon dedicated (1 Chronicles 21:26; 2 Chronicles 7:1). Fire came up from the rock at the offering of Gideon, and the angel of the Lord went up in the flame of the altar of Manoah (Judges 6:21; 13:20).

Naming those passages sharpens the reply recorded in the verse. A sign of that kind was granted to particular prophets at particular moments, and not to every messenger who came to Israel. The commentators also observe that the practice of animal sacrifice in the temple had ended by the time the demand was made. The group was therefore asking for a sign that its own tradition did not require of every prophet, and the verse's answer draws attention to what their ancestors had done with the messengers who did come.

---

## Verse 3:184

> If you are rejected by them, so too were messengers before you who came with clear proofs, divine Books, and enlightening Scriptures

**The Consolation Repeated**

The verse tells the Prophet that if the people deny him, they had already denied messengers who came before him with the proofs their Lord had given them. The sentence is the sūrah's fourth address to the Prophet's grief, and it uses the same remedy as the others.

The argument is addressed to the experience of rejection rather than to the arguments of the rejecters. The Prophet is told that the response he was receiving had a history, and that the men who preceded him had been met with the same treatment from the people they came to. The sentence's force comes from the fact that the Prophet is not being told to expect anything unusual.

**The Earlier Messengers' Experience**

The Book describes the treatment of the earlier messengers in several passages. One states that every time a messenger came to the people of Israel with what they did not want, they rejected some and killed others. Another describes the covenant that was taken from them and the messengers who were sent and rejected.

---

The consolation is drawn not from the Prophet's own situation but from a chain: the messengers who came before him carried the same proofs, received the same rejection, and endured. The verse names the gifts they carried in a sequence — clear proofs (*bayyināt*), books (*zubur*), and an enlightening Scripture (*kitāb munīr*) — which is the sūrah's way of saying that what the Prophet brings is neither new nor improvised but the same message in its final form. The Book states the pattern elsewhere in nearly the same words: "messengers before you were rejected but patiently endured rejection and persecution until Our help came to them" (6:34), and the community itself has been told earlier in this sūrah that it will not enter Paradise without being tested as those before it were, shaken until the Messenger and the believers with him asked when God's help would come (2:214).

## Verse 3:185

> Every soul will taste death. And you will only receive your full reward on the Day of Judgment. Whoever is spared from the Fire and is admitted into Paradise will ˹indeed˺ triumph, whereas the life of this world is no more than the delusion of enjoyment

**The Sentence About Death**

The verse opens with the declaration that every soul will taste death, and the sentence appears in two other sūrahs in almost the same words. The commentators note that the Arabic construction also permits the sense that every soul is a taster of death, which suggests that death is not only an event at the end of a life but a condition that touches a person throughout it.

**The Verse in the Community's Memory**

The verse has been among the most quoted in the community's life, and al-Qushayrī observed that the cup of death has been placed in the hand of every living being, so that a person either drinks it well and finds bliss or drinks it with a grimace and a wail. The recitation of the verse at funerals is a long-established practice.

The sentence is short enough to be carried in memory and complete enough to be used on its own. The community that recites it on such occasions is reminded of the destination of every soul and of the standard by which the outcome is measured, and the two ideas are given in the order the verse presents them.

**The Sentence and Its Two Other Homes**
The declaration that every soul will taste death appears in the same words in the Sūrah of the Prophets (21:35), where the verse adds that the testing is by evil and good, and in the Sūrah of the Spider (29:57), where it introduces the promise of a gathering to the best of homes. The commentators noted the order in this sūrah as well, since the verse follows the account of Uḥud and the men who were killed on that field. The community that had just lost seventy men is told that the appointment applies to everyone, and the Book does not soften the sentence to make the loss bearable.
**What Is Paid in Full**
The second half of the verse says that whoever is drawn away from the Fire and admitted to the garden has succeeded, and it puts the full payment on the Day of Resurrection. The word for the payment is the one used for satisfying a debt, and the Book's point is that nothing is settled before that day. The Prophet's own report gives the same picture in the reverse direction, describing the man who is brought out of the Fire and given what he wishes for, so that no human being could imagine it (Bukhārī). The Book closes the verse by naming what the life of this world is worth, and the two words it uses are the enjoyment of delusion.
**The Verse in the Sūrah's Argument**
The passage around it deals with the men of Uḥud, with the believers who feared death and asked why, and with the hypocrites who said their brothers would not have died if they had stayed (3:168). This verse answers all of them at once. Death is not a risk that the community can avoid by caution; it is an appointment that every soul keeps, and the only question the Book allows is what a person does before that appointment arrives. The verse is therefore a ground for courage rather than a reason for fear, and the sūrah follows it with the instruction to bear patiently and to hold firm (3:200).

---

## Verse 3:186

> You ˹believers˺ will surely be tested in your wealth and yourselves, and you will certainly hear many hurtful words from those who were given the Scripture before you and ˹from˺ the polytheists. But if you are patient and mindful ˹of Allah˺—surely this is a resolve to aspire to

**The Hurt From the Two Groups**

The verse states that the community will hear much that is painful from those who were given the Book before it and from the idolaters, and the word used covers both physical harm and verbal attacks. Al-Ṭabarī explains that it includes mockery, slander, falsehoods, and blasphemous statements about God and His prophets.

**What the Reference to Souls Covers**

The phrase about being tried in one's soul can be read as referring to the person himself, and al-Ṭabarī records another reading on which the word means one's own kin and the members of one's household. On that second reading the verse predicts the loss and the suffering of the people closest to the believer.

Both readings describe what the community at Madinah had already experienced. Its members had lost relatives on the field, and the survivors had borne the strain of the losses in their own persons. The verse's phrasing gathers the different forms of the trial into two words, and the commentators' disagreement reflects how much those two words were expected to carry.

The community's situation in Madinah involved constant dealings with both groups, and the taunts came in the course of ordinary conversation and argument rather than only in open hostility.

**The Two Groups the Verse Names**
The verse tells the community that it will be tested in its property and its lives, and that it will hear much that is painful from those who were given the Book before it and from the idolaters who associate others with God. The order is deliberate, and the commentators noted that the hurt from these groups is placed beside the loss of wealth and life, which puts a verbal attack in the same category as a wound. The Sūrah of the Cow states the same expectation earlier, telling the believers that they will be tried in their property and their persons, and the Sūrah of Muḥammad states the same expectation in one line, that the testing is a settled part of the road (47:31).
**The Political Background**
The passage belongs to the years of Madinah, when the community lived beside the tribes of the city, the tribes of the desert and the Jewish communities with whom it had made treaties that were then broken. The reports behind the verse concern the mocking and the slander that the Muslims met from the surrounding groups, and the Book neither minimises it nor gives the community leave to answer in kind. The verse that follows gives the instruction: if you are patient and mindful of God, their plotting will not harm you, and the sūrah repeats that remedy after the two mentions of loss (3:120; 3:186).
**The Ruling the Scholars Took**
The jurists and the commentators used this verse to set the standard for how the community is to bear insult, and they joined it to the command in the Sūrah of the Private Apartments about not answering a foolish speaker in the same manner (49:11) and to the instruction to repel the evil deed with the better one (41:34). The Book does not tell the community to keep silence about the truth. It tells them that the patience and the God-consciousness are the protection, which is a promise about the outcome rather than about the absence of the offence.

---

## Verse 3:187

> ˹Remember, O Prophet,˺ when Allah took the covenant of those who were given the Scripture to make it known to people and not hide it, yet they cast it behind their backs and traded it for a fleeting gain. What a miserable profit

**The Covenant Recalled**

The verse recalls the time when God took a covenant from those who had been given the Book, and it states the content of the obligation: to make the revelation clear to people and not to conceal it. The sentence reports a commitment that the recipients had accepted.

**The General Application**

Al-Qurṭūbī read the verse as a general condemnation of anyone who is given knowledge and does not teach it, even though the sentence speaks specifically of one group. On this reading the obligation to teach what one has received applies to every community that has it.

The tradition supports the reading with a report in which the Prophet states that whoever conceals knowledge from his people will be bridled with one of the bridles of the fire. With a statement attributed to ʿAlī that God does not require an ignorant person to learn until He requires a learned person to teach. The community's scholars have applied the verse in that sense ever since.

---

The concealment the verse describes is a distinctive offence in the Qur'an, because what is hidden is the guidance that the custodians of the Book were appointed to publish. The Book denounces it in nearly identical terms twice: those who hide the clear proofs after God has made them clear "will be condemned by Allah and all those who condemn" (2:159), and those who hide His revelations, trading them for a small gain, "consume nothing but fire into their bellies" (2:174). The image here is the book thrown behind the back, which the sūrah uses again of those who turned away from what they had been given (2:101). The verse's place in the chapter is deliberate: it comes after the demand that the People of the Book accept the truth about Abraham and before the passage on the family of ʿImrān, so the charge of concealment is the reason their testimony cannot be accepted on its own terms.

## Verse 3:188

> Do not let those who rejoice in their misdeeds and love to take credit for what they have not done think they will escape torment. They will suffer a painful punishment

**The Warning About Self-Satisfaction**

The verse warns against supposing that people who exult in what they have brought about, and who want to be praised for what they have not done, are safe from punishment. The sentence describes a combination of satisfaction with oneself and a desire for credit that is not deserved.

The Book's warnings about hypocrisy usually concentrate on one of these elements, and the verse before the reader names both of them in a single sentence. The result is a portrait of people who are pleased with their own record and who also solicit approval for accomplishments that are not theirs, and the Book treats the combination as a single condition of the heart. The warning also addresses the community that recites it, since the desire for credit is not a condition confined to the group at which the verse was first aimed.

**The Repetition of the Prohibition**

The construction is unusual even by the standards of the Book, and the commentators noted it as a mark of the seriousness with which the conduct described is regarded. The effect is to make the sentence a warning about the people described rather than a statement about them, and the hearer is placed on guard before the description has been completed.

---

The combination the verse names is one the Book treats as a single disease: to be glad of what one has done and to want praise for what one has not done. The sūrah of the Women states the other half of the standard, that God elevates whom He wills and no one is wronged by the width of a date-stone thread (4:49). The passage was read against the People of the Book's claim to a status they no longer lived up to — a claim to be loved by God while they concealed the descriptions of the Prophet in their books — and the commentators noted that the verse is not directed at wrongdoing alone but at the inner accounting that goes with it. The verse's closing denial, that they should think themselves safe from punishment, is the same denial of false security that the sūrah enters at 3:185, where every soul is told it will taste death and no one will be paid in full except at the end.

## Verse 3:189

> To Allah ˹alone˺ belongs the kingdom of the heavens and the earth. And Allah is Most Capable of everything

**The Ownership Stated**

The verse states that to God belongs sovereignty over the heavens and the earth and that He is powerful over all things, and the sentence supplies the fact that governs everything the passage has been discussing. The Book states the same ownership in many places, and it usually does so where claims of power or standing are in question.

The sentence's position in the passage is significant, since it follows a description of people who were pleased with their own standing. It follows a warning to people who took pleasure in their position and expected credit for what they had not done, and it states where the position they valued actually belongs. The sovereignty over the heavens and the earth is not distributed among the people who believe they hold it.

**The Position of the Oppressed**

The community at Madinah had seen its enemies gain ground and had been told about the fate of the groups whose conduct the sūrah has been describing in the passages about the People of the Book and the idolaters. The verse's statement of ownership supplies the frame in which all of that is to be read, and the sentences that follow turn to the signs of God in the creation.

---

## Verse 3:190

> Indeed, in the creation of the heavens and the earth and the alternation of the day and night there are signs for people of reason

**The Signs Named**

The verse states that in the creation of the heavens and the earth and in the variation of night and day there are signs for those who have understanding. The sentence opens the sūrah's closing movement, which is built on the observation of the world.

The Book invites this observation in several passages. One lists the creation of the heavens and the earth, the alternation of day and night, the ships that sail the sea, the rain, the creatures scattered throughout the earth, the shifting winds, and the clouds, and concludes that in all of it there are signs for people of understanding. Another passage states that in the alternation of night and day and in what God has created there are signs for those who are mindful. A third states that in the creation of the heavens and the earth there are signs for the believers.

**The Word Behind Creation**

The word rendered as creation can refer both to the act by which the heavens and the earth were brought into being and to the character of what was created. The commentators noted that the verse can therefore direct attention both to the making and to the nature of what was made.

---

The verse opens a passage that runs to the end of the sūrah and is built on the observation of the world: those who remember God standing, sitting and lying on their sides, and reflect on the creation of the heavens and the earth, and end by saying that God has not created this in vain (3:191). The Prophet's own practice was attached to these verses. ʿĀʾisha reported that when he rose for the night prayer he would recite the closing ten verses of Āl ʿImrān (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī), which gives the passage its place in the community's worship: the reasoning of 3:190–194 is not private reflection but night recitation. The sūrah so closes by returning to the theme it began with — the Book itself as guidance for people of understanding (3:7) — and the argument from creation is the ground on which the sūrah's closing prayer stands.

## Verse 3:191

> ˹They are˺ those who remember Allah while standing, sitting, and lying on their sides, and reflect on the creation of the heavens and the earth ˹and pray˺, “Our Lord! You have not created ˹all of˺ this without purpose. Glory be to You! Protect us from the torment of the Fire

**The Three Postures of Remembrance**

The verse describes the people of understanding as those who remember God while standing, while sitting, and while lying on their sides, and the three positions cover the ordinary conditions of a human life. The sūrah about Women uses the same three modes in its instruction about remembrance after the prayer.

Al-Rāzī observed that no human being is ever outside one of those three positions, so the instruction covers the whole of a person's waking and sleeping life. The verse's description is therefore of a life rather than of a ritual, and the community that reads it is being shown what the constant awareness of God looks like in practice.

**How the Passage Defines the People of Understanding**

The phrase describing those for whom the signs exist is one the Book uses elsewhere to set the thoughtful beside the heedless. One passage asks whether the one who knows that the revelation is the truth can be like the one who is blind, and answers that only people of reason will take the reminder (13:19). The same term appears in the sūrah of the Companies, where the one who devotes his hours to worship, standing and prostrating, is described as hoping for the mercy of his Lord before the question is put: are those who know equal to those who do not (39:9)?

The passage in this sūrah answers that question by showing what the people of understanding do. They remember God in every posture, reflect on the world He made, and turn their reflection into a petition that includes protection from the fire. Elsewhere the Book records the same kind of prayer in a shorter form, asking for good in this world and the next and deliverance from the punishment (2:201). The verse before the reader supplies the conduct that gives the term its content, and the verses that follow complete the portrait.

---

## Verse 3:192

> Our Lord! Indeed, those You commit to the Fire will be ˹completely˺ disgraced! And the wrongdoers will have no helpers

**The Disgrace of the Fire**

The verse continues the prayer of the people of understanding with the statement that whoever is made to enter the Fire has been disgraced, and it adds that the wrongdoers have no helpers. The sentence explains why the petition in the verse before it was made.

The word for disgrace describes the loss of honour before others, and the verse treats the entry into the Fire as the greatest form of it. The people of understanding, who have just reflected on the dignity of the creation, ask to be spared the condition that would undo the standing of a human being.

**The Explanation of the Disgrace**

Al-Quṭūbī and al-Ṭabarī record the view that the disgrace described here belongs specifically to those who enter the Fire and never leave it, rather than to everyone who is punished there for a period. Al-Ṭabarī records a remark of Ḥasan al-Baṣrī on the subject: sinners will be admitted to the fire, yet at some stage they will be brought out of it on account of the belief that remains within them.

---

## Verse 3:193

> Our Lord! We have heard the caller to ˹true˺ belief, ˹proclaiming,˺ ‘Believe in your Lord ˹alone˺,’ so we believed. Our Lord! Forgive our sins, absolve us of our misdeeds, and allow us ˹each˺ to die as one of the virtuous

**The Caller and the Call**

The verse continues the prayer with a report of what the community heard: a caller calling to faith, urging belief in their Lord, and the community's answer that it believed. The identity of the caller is most often given as the Prophet, and al-Rāzī records the view that the call is the Book itself, since the message reaches everyone while only a limited number heard it from the Prophet directly.

Both readings describe the same event from different angles. What the community is testifying to is that it was called and that it responded, and the prayer's next clause asks for the forgiveness that the response makes necessary. The report is made in the first person, so the community speaks as one body about what it heard and accepted.

**The Response Reported in the Prayer**

The words the community uses are a simple statement that it believed, and the simplicity is characteristic of the prayer. The Book's descriptions of faith in such passages avoid elaboration and place the emphasis on the act of accepting the message.

**The Answer the Verse Records**
The prayer in this passage is the prayer of the community, and the verse gives the reply of the believing heart: we heard a caller calling to faith and we believed. The Book uses the same pattern in the Sūrah of the Cow, where the community says that it hears and obeys (2:285), and in the Sūrah of the All-Encompassing, where the answer of the people of the garden is their praise of God. The readers of this passage have been taught what to ask for, and this verse teaches them to state what they have already accepted.
**What the Verse Asks For**
The verse ends with two requests: forgive us our sins and grant us mercy and make us among the successful. The Book names the success in the Sūrah of the Family of ʿImrān's own opening passage, where the people who are successful are those who believe, pray, spend and ask forgiveness at the last part of the night (3:16-17). The prayer therefore closes the sūrah's longest supplication, and the next verse gives the answer: God grants what has been asked and does not let the labour of those who work be lost (3:195).
**Where the Passage Stands**
The supplication runs from 3:190 to 3:194, and it is one of the passages the Prophet recited on waking at night, as the report in Bukhārī says that he would recite the last verses of this sūrah when he got up. The passage begins with the signs in the creation of the heavens and the earth and the men who remember God standing, sitting and lying on their sides, and it ends with the prayer in front of us. The Book gives the community the words, the reasoning and the request, and it leaves the answer to the next verse.

---

## Verse 3:194

> Our Lord! Grant us what You have promised us through Your messengers and do not put us to shame on Judgment Day—for certainly You never fail in Your promise.”

**The Petition About the Promise**

The prayer asks God to give what He promised through His messengers, and the commentators record two ways of taking the phrase: that God made the promise to the messengers themselves, or that the messengers conveyed the promise to the people, or that both senses are intended. The Book's promises to the believers are stated in both forms, so the readings are not exclusive.

**The Word for the Appointed Meeting**

The prayer closes on the declaration that God does not fail the appointment, and the noun used there names a time fixed by agreement. The sūrah has already used the same word in the prayer of the people of understanding's earlier petition, where they ask God to gather humanity on a Day about which there is no doubt and add that God does not break His promise (3:9). The two sentences rest on the same assurance, one stated at the beginning of the passage and the other at its end.

The word also appears in the account of Badr, where the Book notes that even if the two armies had arranged a meeting they would have missed it, and that the encounter happened so that what was destined might be established (8:42). The community's own history had therefore already supplied an instance of an appointment that held, and the petition in this verse takes the same certainty as its ground. What the people of understanding ask for is not a new promise but the fulfilment of the one they have been given.

---

## Verse 3:195

> So their Lord responded to them: “I will never deny any of you—male or female—the reward of your deeds. Both are equal in reward. Those who migrated or were expelled from their homes, and were persecuted for My sake and fought and ˹some˺ were martyred—I will certainly forgive their sins and admit them into Gardens under which rivers flow, as a reward from Allah. And with Allah is the finest reward!”

**The Answer Given**

The verse opens by stating that their Lord answered them, and the sentence follows the prayer that reaches through the end of the previous verse. The Book records the answers to such prayers in several places, and the community in this passage is told that its petitions were heard.

The transition from request to answer gives the closing movement of the sūrah its shape. The people of understanding reflect on the creation, make their requests, and are answered by the one they address, and the reply is reported in the first person.

**The Sufferings Listed**

The answer then names the experiences of the people it concerns: those who emigrated, those who were expelled from their homes, those who were hurt in God's cause, those who fought, and those who were killed. The list follows the community's history, beginning with the migration from Makkah and continuing through the fighting that the sūrah has described.

---

The verse is the answer to the prayer that ends the passage before it, where the believers ask forgiveness, a good end, and to die with the righteous (3:193–194). The Book records the answer in the plainest terms: the petition was heard and granted. The four descriptions of the people addressed — those who migrated, those expelled from their homes, those persecuted for God's sake, those who fought or were killed — are the community's own history written into the promise, and the same list is honoured elsewhere: those who believe, emigrate and strive "can hope for Allah's mercy" (2:218), and the one who leaves his home for God's cause "will find many safe havens and bountiful resources throughout the earth" (4:100). The clause about male and female is the sūrah's answer to a specific complaint, that women's sacrifices had not been named as the men's were, and the Qur'an states the principle repeatedly: the doer of good, whether male or female, and a believer, is rewarded by the best of what they did (16:97), and such people "will never be wronged even as much as the speck on a date stone" (4:124).

**What the Answer Says**
The verse reports that God answered the prayer, and the answer names what the believers had asked for and what they had done: they were the ones who migrated, and were driven from their homes, and were harmed in My way, and fought and were killed. The Book then gives the terms: I will blot out their bad deeds, and admit them to gardens with rivers running under them, a reward from God, and with God is the best reward. The passage that follows turns to the men who were comfortable, and it warns them about the exchange they were making (3:196-197).
**The Two Books in the Passage**
The sūrah of the Family of ʿImrān runs the history of the two earlier communities through its middle, and the account of the Muslims who lost their homes belongs to the same story. The Book uses the same word for those who were driven out in the Sūrah of the Pilgrimage, where permission to fight is given to people who were expelled from their homes unjustly (22:39-40), and the Sūrah of the Bee names the same quality about the emigrants who were patient after their trials (16:110). The verse in front of us puts the migration, the persecution, the wound and the death in one list, and it answers the whole list with the forgiveness and the gardens.
**The Prayer It Answers**
The prayer that this verse answers is the long supplication that begins with the signs in the creation of the heavens and the earth and the men who remember God standing, sitting and lying down (3:190-194). The Prophet recited those verses on waking at night, as the report in Bukhārī records, and the community has read the passage in the night prayer ever since. The answer in front of us is the last sentence of that prayer, and the sūrah leaves it stated in God's own voice.

## Verse 3:196

> Do not be deceived by the prosperity of the disbelievers throughout the land

**The Delusion Named**

The verse addresses the Prophet with a warning against being misled by the ease with which those who deny the message travel the land and prosper in it. The sentence names a conclusion that a person could draw from appearances: that the comfort of the deniers is a sign of their standing before God.

**The Verse and the One It Echoes**

The sentence is the second in the sūrah to deal with the ease of those who deny the message. The earlier passage, which comes a few verses before this one, states that the delay granted to such people should not be mistaken for an advantage, and it warns that their respite is a form of drawing them on. The sentence before the reader repeats the warning from the side of the observer rather than in a statement of fact, since the temptation it answers is the one the community felt when it watched its opponents travel and profit while it went hungry. The Book addresses the same temptation in another sūrah, where it asks those who trade in falsehood to enjoy themselves and names their destination (40:4). The three passages together show a single concern treated from three angles, and the community's teachers used them together when they explained why hardship and prosperity are not the measures of a person's standing.

---

## Verse 3:197

> It is only a brief enjoyment. Then Hell will be their home—what an evil place to rest

**The Brief Enjoyment**

The verse follows the warning of the sentence before it and states what the freedom of the deniers amounts to: a small enjoyment, followed by the Fire as their refuge. The sentence is the answer to the impression that the previous verse had told the reader to resist.

The Book uses the vocabulary of a little or a brief span for the present life in several passages, and the word in this verse carries the same sense. The community was expected to adopt that measure as its own rather than the estimate that appearances suggest. What the deniers have secured is measured in the sentence as small, and the word chosen for it settles the comparison that the community had been tempted to make.

**The Word for Little**

The Arabic term rendered as a little names something slight in extent or duration, and the commentators applied it to the span of worldly comfort. The sentence does not deny that the deniers enjoy their position; it denies that the enjoyment is large.

---

## Verse 3:198

> But those who are mindful of their Lord will be in Gardens under which rivers flow, to stay there forever—as an accommodation from Allah. And what is with Allah is best for the virtuous

**The Contrast Stated**

The verse begins with a turn that sets the destiny of the mindful against the destination described in the sentence before it. Those who revere their Lord are promised gardens with rivers flowing beneath them, and the promise is given in the same terms the sūrah has used earlier for the reward of the righteous.

**The Gardens Described**

The gardens are described through the rivers that run beneath them, which is the Book's most frequent image for the reward. The phrase recalls the promise made earlier in the sūrah to those who are mindful, and it recalls the promise made to the believers and the doers of good in the second sūrah, where the same rivers are named.

The community that hears the description is being reminded of what it has been promised. The image is used consistently across the Book, and the repetition of the same words ties the rewards promised in different passages to the same reality. It conveys both the abundance of what is given and the permanence of the home in which it is enjoyed.

---

## Verse 3:199

> Indeed, there are some among the People of the Book who truly believe in Allah and what has been revealed to you ˹believers˺ and what was revealed to them. They humble themselves before Allah—never trading Allah’s revelations for a fleeting gain. Their reward is with their Lord. Surely Allah is swift in reckoning

**The Objection Reported**

Among the objections raised was the claim that the Negus had been a Christian, and this objection is said to have been voiced both by sincere followers and by the hypocrites, who used it to cast doubt on the Prophet's conduct. The verse answers such objections by describing the qualities of the person who is accepted rather than by debating the objections themselves.

**What the Community Did With the Verse in Practice**

The community's own record shows how the sentence was applied in the weeks after it was heard. When the ruler of Abyssinia died, the Prophet told his companions the news and arranged them in rows, and the funeral prayer was offered for a man who had died in another land. The report of that prayer is the occasion the commentators connect with this verse. The practice it established became a subject of the community's law: the jurists who permitted prayer for a person who dies away from his own town cited the case of the Negus, and those who restricted the practice to that single case argued that the Prophet's action was particular to him.

Al-Nawawī recorded the position of the school of al-Shāfiʿī in favour of the practice, observing that the report about the Negus is sound and that its opponents have no answer to it. The sentence in this verse so has a place in the community's jurisprudence as well as in its history, and the two are connected: the verse describes the qualities of a believer among the People of the Book, and the Prophet's conduct toward a ruler who had them supplied the precedent.

---

The verse's portrait of the believing party among the People of the Book is completed by the passage that opens this section of the sūrah, where they are described as reciting God's verses in the night and prostrating, believing in God and the Last Day, commanding right and forbidding wrong, and hastening to good deeds (3:113–115), with the promise that whatever good they do will never be denied. The same group appears in the sūrah of the Narrations, where those to whom the Scripture was given before the Qur'an believe in it when it reaches them and are given their reward twice over (28:52–54). The section's report about the Negus belongs to this group in the community's memory. The verse's closing words place the whole matter under account: they have their reward with their Lord, and God is swift in reckoning.

## Verse 3:200

> O believers! Patiently endure, persevere, stand on guard, and be mindful of Allah, so you may be successful

**The Closing Address**

The sūrah ends with an address to the believers, calling them to patience, to constancy in patience, to perseverance, and to reverence of God. The four injunctions are stated in succession, and the verse closes with the promise that those who fulfil them may prosper.

**Patience and Contest in Patience**

The first two injunctions come from the same root, one naming patience itself and the second naming a contest in patience in which a person should outlast the one he faces. Al-Rāzī and al-Zamakhsharī explained the second form as outdoing the enemy in endurance and firmness.

**The Reports About the Watch**

The tradition preserved reports about guarding the frontier. One states that a single day spent in that service is better than the world and everything in it, a report found in Bukhārī and Muslim. Other reports mention that the merit of such service continues after a person's death, since the defence of the community allows others to carry on their religious duties.

The reports gave the verse a practical meaning for the community's defence, and the jurists discussed the conditions under which the duty applies. The sentence before the reader was used as one of the foundations of the discussion, and the reports supplied its content.

---
